Endocrine Pharmacology:

Front: MOA of Octreotide and Lanreotide?
Back: Somatostatin analogs; inhibit GH, glucagon, insulin, and LH release.

Front: Clinical use of Desmopressin (dDAVP) beyond Central DI?
Back: Von Willebrand disease and Hemophilia A (increases factor VIII and vWF release).

Front: Preferred treatment for Acromegaly in a patient resistant to somatostatin analogs?
Back: Pegvisomant (GH receptor antagonist).

Front: MOA of Cabergoline and Bromocriptine in Hyperprolactinemia?
Back: Dopamine (D2) agonists; inhibit prolactin secretion from the anterior pituitary.

Front: Primary side effect of Oxytocin administration?
Back: Water intoxication (due to ADH-like effect) and uterine rupture.

Front: MOA of Conivaptan and Tolvaptan?
Back: ADH (V2) receptor antagonists; used in SIADH to increase free water clearance.

Thyroid Pharmacology
Front: Drug of choice for Thyroid Hormone Replacement?
Back: Levothyroxine (T4); preferred over T3 due to longer half-life and easier monitoring.

Front: MOA of Propylthiouracil (PTU) and Methimazole?
Back: Inhibition of Thyroid Peroxidase (TPO); block iodine organification and coupling.

Front: Unique peripheral MOA of Propylthiouracil (PTU) not shared by Methimazole?
Back: Inhibits 5'-deiodinase (blocks peripheral conversion of T4 to T3).

Front: Black Box Warning for Propylthiouracil (PTU)?
Back: Severe hepatotoxicity/liver failure.

Front: Preferred Thionamide in the 1st Trimester of pregnancy?
Back: PTU (Methimazole is teratogenic; causes Aplasia Cutis).

Front: Most serious common adverse effect of Thionamides (PTU/Methimazole)?
Back: Agranulocytosis (requires monitoring of CBC if fever/sore throat occurs).

Front: MOA of Lugol’s Solution (Potassium Iodide) in hyperthyroidism?
Back: Wolff-Chaikoff effect; high iodide levels acutely inhibit thyroid hormone release.

Front: Common use for Beta-blockers (e.g., Propranolol) in Thyroid Storm?
Back: Control of sympathetic symptoms (tachycardia) and inhibition of peripheral T4-to-T3 conversion.

Diabetes and Insulin
Front: Rapid-acting Insulin analogs?
Back: Lispro, Aspart, Glulisine ("No LAG").

Front: Long-acting (basal) Insulin analogs?
Back: Glargine, Detemir, Degludec.

Front: First-line oral agent for Type 2 DM?
Back: Metformin (Biguanide).

Front: MOA of Metformin?
Back: Inhibits hepatic gluconeogenesis and increases peripheral glucose uptake (via AMPK activation).

Front: Most serious adverse effect of Metformin?
Back: Lactic Acidosis (contraindicated in renal insufficiency).

Front: MOA of Sulfonylureas (e.g., Glipizide, Glyburide)?
Back: Close K+ channels in Beta-cells → cell depolarization → Ca2+ influx → Insulin release.

Front: Difference between 1st and 2nd generation Sulfonylureas?
Back: 2nd gen (Glipizide, Glyburide) are more potent and have fewer Disulfiram-like reactions.

Front: MOA of Pioglitazone and Rosiglitazone (TZDs)?
Back: Activate PPAR-gamma (nuclear receptor) to increase insulin sensitivity and Adiponectin.

Front: Major side effects of Thiazolidinediones (TZDs)?
Back: Weight gain, edema, and HF exacerbation (due to fluid retention); increased risk of fractures.

Front: MOA of GLP-1 Agonists (e.g., Exenatide, Liraglutide)?
Back: Increase glucose-dependent insulin secretion, decrease glucagon, and slow gastric emptying.

Front: MOA of DPP-4 Inhibitors (e.g., Sitagliptin)?
Back: Inhibit the enzyme that breaks down GLP-1, increasing endogenous GLP-1 levels.

Front: MOA of SGLT2 Inhibitors (e.g., Canagliflozin, Empagliflozin)?
Back: Inhibit glucose reabsorption in the Proximal Convoluted Tubule (PCT).

Front: Common side effects of SGLT2 Inhibitors?
Back: Glucosuria, UTIs, vaginal candidiasis, and euglycemic DKA.

Front: MOA of Acarbose and Miglitol?
Back: Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors; delay intestinal glucose absorption by inhibiting brush-border enzymes.

Front: Treatment for severe hypoglycemia in an unconscious patient (non-IV access)?
Back: Intramuscular Glucagon.

Adrenal Steroids
Front: MOA of Glucocorticoids?
Back: Inhibit Phospholipase A2 and NF-kappaB (anti-inflammatory).

Front: Result of chronic Glucocorticoid use on the HPA axis?
Back: Adrenal atrophy due to suppression of ACTH.

Front: MOA of Spironolactone in Endocrine therapy?
Back: Aldosterone receptor antagonist and weak androgen receptor antagonist.

Front: MOA and use of Metyrapone?
Back: Inhibits 11-beta-hydroxylase; used in diagnostic testing for ACTH reserve.

Front: MOA of Ketoconazole in Cushing's Syndrome?
Back: Inhibits desmolase (17,20-lyase); blocks all steroid synthesis.

Front: MOA of Mifepristone in an Endocrine context?
Back: Progesterone and Glucocorticoid receptor antagonist (used for medical abortion and hyperglycemia in Cushing's).

Front: Drug used to treat Primary Hyperaldosteronism (Conn Syndrome)?
Back: Spironolactone or Eplerenone.

Bone and Calcium
Front: MOA of Bisphosphonates (e.g., Alendronate)?
Back: Pyrophosphate analogs; bind hydroxyapatite in bone and inhibit osteoclast activity.

Front: Serious adverse effect of oral Bisphosphonates?
Back: Corrosive esophagitis (must stay upright for 30 mins).

Front: MOA of Teriparatide?
Back: Recombinant PTH analog; stimulates osteoblasts more than osteoclasts when given pulsatile.

Front: MOA of Denosumab?
Back: Monoclonal antibody against RANKL; inhibits osteoclast maturation.

Front: MOA of Cinacalcet?
Back: Calcimimetic; sensitizes Ca2+-sensing receptors (CaSR) in parathyroid gland to decrease PTH.

Front: Use of Sevelamer?
Back: Phosphate binder used to treat hyperphosphatemia in Chronic Kidney Disease.

Reproductive Pharmacology
Front: MOA of Clomiphene?
Back: SERM; Antagonist at estrogen receptors in the hypothalamus, preventing feedback inhibition and increasing FSH/LH (ovulation induction).

Front: MOA of Tamoxifen vs. Raloxifene in bone?
Back: Both are agonists at bone estrogen receptors (prevent osteoporosis).

Front: MOA of Tamoxifen in the uterus?
Back: Agonist (increases risk of endometrial cancer).

Front: MOA of Raloxifene in the uterus?
Back: Antagonist (no increased risk of endometrial cancer).

Front: MOA of Anastrozole and Letrozole?
Back: Aromatase inhibitors; block peripheral conversion of androgens to estrogen.

Front: MOA of Finasteride?
Back: 5-alpha-reductase inhibitor; blocks conversion of Testosterone to DHT (used in BPH and male pattern baldness).

Front: MOA of Flutamide?
Back: Competitive inhibitor at androgen receptors (used in prostate cancer).

Front: MOA of Leuprolide (Continuous vs. Pulsatile)?
Back: Pulsatile: GnRH agonist (infertility). Continuous: GnRH antagonist (downregulates receptors) for prostate cancer or endometriosis.

Front: Side effect of Sildenafil when combined with Nitrates?
Back: Severe hypotension (both increase cGMP).

Front: MOA of Tesamorelin?
Back: GHRH analog used to treat HIV-associated lipodystrophy.

Front: Clinical use of Mecasermin?
Back: Recombinant IGF-1; used in children with growth failure due to severe IGF-1 deficiency (GH resistance).

Front: Primary side effect of Mecasermin?
Back: Hypoglycemia (IGF-1 has insulin-like effects).

Front: MOA of Ganirelix and Cetrorelix?
Back: GnRH antagonists; used in controlled ovarian stimulation to prevent premature LH surges.

Front: Comparison: Half-life of Desmopressin vs. Vasopressin?
Back: Desmopressin has a much longer half-life and a higher V2:V1 selectivity ratio.

Thyroid & Antithyroid (Deep Dive)
Front: Effect of Amiodarone on thyroid function?
Back: Can cause both hypothyroidism (Wolff-Chaikoff) and hyperthyroidism (Jod-Basedow or destructive thyroiditis).

Front: Why is Methimazole preferred over PTU in most adults?
Back: Longer half-life (once-daily dosing) and lower risk of serious liver injury.

Front: MOA of Anion Inhibitors (Perchlorate, Thiocyanate)?
Back: Competitive inhibition of the sodium-iodide symporter (NIS).

Front: Major risk of using Radioactive Iodine (I-131) in Graves' disease?
Back: Permanent hypothyroidism (requires lifelong T4 replacement).

Front: MOA of Liotrix?
Back: A 4:1 mixture of synthetic T4 and T3.

Comprehensive Diabetes Management
Front: Which Diabetes drug class is associated with "Euglycemic DKA"?
Back: SGLT2 Inhibitors (e.g., Empagliflozin).

Front: MOA of Pramlintide?
Back: Amylin analog; decreases gastric emptying and inhibits glucagon secretion.

Front: Specific clinical timing for Pramlintide administration?
Back: Injected immediately before meals; used for both Type 1 and Type 2 DM.

Front: Which sulfonylureas have the highest risk of hypoglycemia in the elderly?
Back: Long-acting ones (e.g., Glyburide).

Front: Effect of Metformin on Vitamin B12?
Back: Can cause Vitamin B12 deficiency with long-term use (malabsorption).

Front: MOA of Meglitinides (Repaglinide, Nateglinide)?
Back: Similar to Sulfonylureas (close K+ channels) but with a much shorter duration of action (postprandial control).

Front: Common side effect of Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors (Acarbose)?
Back: GI upset (flatulence, diarrhea) due to fermentation of undigested carbohydrates.

Front: Contraindication for GLP-1 Agonists (e.g., Liraglutide)?
Back: Personal or family history of Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma or MEN 2.

Front: Benefit of GLP-1 Agonists and SGLT2 Inhibitors beyond glucose control?
Back: Cardioprotective benefits and weight loss.

Adrenal Pharmacology (Specifics)
Front: MOA of Mitotane?
Back: Adrenolytic drug; selectively destroys adrenocortical cells (used in inoperable adrenal carcinoma).

Front: Effect of Spironolactone on the heart in HF?
Back: Reduces cardiac remodeling and mortality in heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF).

Front: MOA of Eplerenone vs. Spironolactone?
Back: Eplerenone is a selective aldosterone receptor antagonist (no anti-androgen side effects like gynecomastia).

Front: Drug of choice for primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison’s)?
Back: Hydrocortisone (to replace glucocorticoids) + Fludrocortisone (to replace mineralocorticoids).

Front: Difference between Prednisone and Prednisolone?
Back: Prednisone is a prodrug converted to the active Prednisolone by the liver (11β-HSD).

Reproductive & Steroid Modulators
Front: MOA of Danazol?
Back: Synthetic androgen; acts as a partial agonist at androgen receptors. Used in endometriosis and hereditary angioedema.

Front: Adverse effects of Danazol?
Back: Weight gain, hirsutism, acne, and decreased HDL levels (virilization).

Front: MOA of Fulvestrant?
Back: Pure estrogen receptor antagonist; degrades the estrogen receptor (used in resistant breast cancer).

Front: MOA of Exemestane?
Back: Irreversible (suicide) aromatase inhibitor.

Front: MOA of Mifepristone (RU-486)?
Back: Progesterone receptor antagonist; leads to decidual breakdown and pregnancy termination.

Front: MOA of Misoprostol in medical abortion?
Back: PGE1 analog; causes uterine contractions to expel the fetus.

Front: MOA of Bicalutamide and Enzalutamide?
Back: Nonsteroidal competitive inhibitors of the androgen receptor; used for prostate cancer.

Front: MOA of Abiraterone?
Back: Inhibits 17α-hydroxylase/17,20-lyase (CYP17); blocks androgen synthesis in the testes and adrenal glands.

Front: MOA of Minoxidil (Topical)?
Back: Direct arteriolar vasodilator; used topically for androgenetic alopecia.

Calcium & Bone Metabolism (Advanced)
Front: MOA of Calcitonin (Salmon)?
Back: Directly inhibits osteoclast bone resorption; used in Paget’s disease and acute hypercalcemia.

Front: MOA of Gallium Nitrate?
Back: Inhibits bone resorption; used in cancer-related hypercalcemia resistant to hydration.

Front: MOA of Plicamycin (Mithramycin)?
Back: RNA synthesis inhibitor that lowers calcium by inhibiting osteoclasts (rarely used due to toxicity).

Front: Electrolyte abnormality caused by Cinacalcet?
Back: Hypocalcemia (by suppressing PTH).

Front: Difference in MOA: Ergocalciferol vs. Cholecalciferol?
Back: Ergocalciferol (D2) is plant-derived; Cholecalciferol (D3) is skin/animal-derived.

Front: MOA of Calcitriol?
Back: Active 1,25-(OH)2D3; increases intestinal absorption of calcium and phosphate.

Miscellaneous / High-Yield Connections
Front: Treatment for SIADH in a patient who cannot tolerate Vaptans?
Back: Demeclocycline (an ADH antagonist in the renal tubule).

Front: Side effect of Demeclocycline?
Back: Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus and Photosensitivity.

Front: Drug used to prevent Hemorrhagic Cystitis from Cyclophosphamide?
Back: Mesna (binds to acrolein).

Front: MOA of Octreotide in variceal bleeding?
Back: Induces splanchnic vasoconstriction, reducing portal venous pressure.

Front: MOA of Pasireotide?
Back: Somatostatin analog with high affinity for SSTR5; used specifically for Cushing's Disease.

Front: Most common side effect of Alprostadil (PGE1) for ED?
Back: Penile pain and priapism.

Front: MOA of Ulipristal?
Back: Selective progesterone receptor modulator (SPRM); used for emergency contraception (up to 5 days).

Front: Drug for Hyperprolactinemia that is safe in pregnancy?
Back: Bromocriptine (more clinical data/safety history than Cabergoline).

Front: Primary difference in MOA between Degarelix and Leuprolide?
Back: Degarelix is a GnRH antagonist (immediate effect); Leuprolide is an agonist (initial flare).

Front: Why is Flutamide given with Leuprolide?
Back: To prevent the "testosterone flare" symptoms during the first week of Leuprolide therapy.

Front: Drug used to close a Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA)?
Back: Indomethacin (NSAID).

Front: Drug used to keep a PDA open?
Back: Alprostadil (PGE1).

Front: MOA of Cyproterone acetate?
Back: Progestin and androgen receptor antagonist; used for hirsutism and prostate cancer.

Front: What drug is a "Calcimimetic"?
Back: Cinacalcet.

Front: What drug is a "Selective Estrogen Receptor Downregulator" (SERD)?
Back: Fulvestrant.

Front: Which endocrine hormones signal via the cAMP/PKA pathway?
Back: FLAT ChAMP: FSH, LH, ACTH, TSH, CRH, hCG, ADH (V2), MSH, PTH, Glucagon.

Front: Which endocrine hormones signal via the IP3/DAG (Gq) pathway?
Back: GOAT HAG: GnRH, Oxytocin, ADH (V1), TRH, Histamine (H1), Angiotensin II, Gastrin.

Front: Which endocrine hormones use Intracellular/Nuclear receptors?
Back: PET CAT on TV: Progesterone, Estrogen, Testosterone, Cortisol, Aldosterone, T3/T4, Vitamin D.

Front: MOA of IGF-1 and Insulin signaling?
Back: Receptor Tyrosine Kinase (MAPK and PI3K pathways).

Front: MOA of GH and Prolactin signaling?
Back: JAK/STAT pathway (non-receptor tyrosine kinase).

Advanced Insulin & Glucose Modulators
Front: Which DM medication class increases the risk of bladder cancer?
Back: Pioglitazone (TZDs).

Front: Only non-insulin antidiabetic drug approved for Type 1 DM?
Back: Pramlintide (Amylin analog).

Front: MOA of Diazoxide in the pancreas?
Back: K+ channel opener; hyperpolarizes Beta-cells to inhibit insulin release (used for Insulinoma).

Front: Which SGLT2 inhibitor is specifically known for reducing the risk of HF hospitalization?
Back: Empagliflozin (and Dapagliflozin).

Front: Main metabolic difference between Glucagon and Epinephrine?
Back: Glucagon does not affect muscle glycogen (muscle lacks glucagon receptors).

Specialized Adrenal & Steroid Agents
Front: MOA of Trilostane?
Back: Inhibits 3β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase; blocks conversion of pregnenolone to progesterone.

Front: Effect of Spironolactone on 17α-hydroxylase?
Back: Inhibition; contributes to its anti-androgenic side effects (gynecomastia).

Front: Why is Fludrocortisone the drug of choice for mineralocorticoid replacement?
Back: High mineralocorticoid-to-glucocorticoid potency ratio (250:10).

Front: Diagnostic use of Cosyntropin?
Back: Synthetic ACTH; used to distinguish primary from secondary adrenal insufficiency.

Front: Glucocorticoid with the highest anti-inflammatory potency and zero salt-retention?
Back: Dexamethasone.

Bone, Calcium & Phosphate (Niche Tox)
Front: MOA of Strontium Ranelate?
Back: Dual action: increases bone formation (osteoblasts) and decreases bone resorption (osteoclasts).

Front: MOA of Romosozumab?
Back: Sclerostin inhibitor; increases bone formation and decreases resorption.

Front: Unique side effect of Alendronate besides esophagitis?
Back: Osteonecrosis of the jaw (ONJ) and atypical subtrochanteric fractures.

Front: MOA of Cinacalcet in Tertiary Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: Increases sensitivity of Ca2+-sensing receptors in the parathyroid to suppress PTH.

Front: Most common electrolyte toxicity of Vitamin D overdose?
Back: Hypercalcemia and hypercalciuria (leads to polyuria and renal stones).

Reproductive Nuances & Contraception
Front: MOA of the "Mini-pill" (Progestin-only)?
Back: Thickens cervical mucus (prevents sperm penetration) and thins endometrium.

Front: Component of OCPs responsible for inhibiting the LH surge?
Back: Estrogen (suppresses FSH/LH release).

Front: Effect of OCPs on SHBG (Sex Hormone Binding Globulin)?
Back: Increases SHBG; leads to decreased free testosterone (often improves acne).

Front: MOA of Letrozole in PCOS?
Back: Aromatase inhibitor; induces ovulation by decreasing estrogen feedback, increasing FSH.

Front: Interaction between Rifampin and OCPs?
Back: Rifampin induces CYP3A4, increasing OCP metabolism and decreasing efficacy.

Front: MOA of Drospirenone (found in Yaz/Yasmin)?
Back: Progestin with anti-mineralocorticoid and anti-androgenic activity (spironolactone analog).

Pituitary & Growth Disorders
Front: MOA of Sermorelin?
Back: GHRH analog used to stimulate GH secretion for diagnostic testing.

Front: How does Somatostatin inhibit TSH release?
Back: Via Gi-coupled receptors on pituitary thyrotropes.

Front: Common side effect of Octreotide related to the biliary system?
Back: Biliary sludge and gallstones (due to inhibition of CCK/gallbladder contractility).

Front: Effect of GH on adipose tissue?
Back: Lipolytic; increases free fatty acids in the blood.

Final High-Yield Integration
Front: Drug of choice for nephrogenic DI induced by Lithium?
Back: Amiloride (blocks Li+ entry into collecting duct via ENaC).

Front: MOA of Metyrapone in a "Metyrapone Test"?
Back: Blocks 11-beta-hydroxylase → ↓ Cortisol → ↑ ACTH (if HPA axis is intact).

Front: Why is Methimazole avoided in the 1st trimester of pregnancy?
Back: Risk of Choanal Atresia and Aplasia Cutis.

Front: MOA of Ketoconazole's effect on Leydig cells?
Back: Inhibits 17,20-desmolase; decreases testosterone production.

Front: Major advantage of Eplerenone over Spironolactone?
Back: Higher selectivity for mineralocorticoid receptors; lacks progesterone and androgen receptor affinity.

Front: MOA of Ospemifene?
Back: SERM used specifically for dyspareunia (vaginal atrophy) in postmenopausal women.

Front: Treatment for Hyperprolactinemia that also treats Parkinson's?
Back: Bromocriptine.

Front: Which class of DM drugs is contraindicated in Gastroparesis?
Back: GLP-1 Agonists and Pramlintide (both slow gastric emptying).

Front: Treatment for GH-producing pituitary adenoma if surgery fails?
Back: Octreotide (Somatostatin analog) or Pegvisomant (GH receptor antagonist).

Front: MOA of Triptorelin?
Back: GnRH agonist; continuous administration used for chemical castration in prostate cancer.

Front: MOA of Lixisenatide and Albiglutide?
Back: GLP-1 receptor agonists (Incretin mimetics).

Front: Which DM drug class can cause "Fractures in women" as a side effect?
Back: Thiazolidinediones (TZDs) like Pioglitazone (due to decreased bone formation).

Front: MOA of Miglitol vs. Acarbose?
Back: Both are Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors; Miglitol is absorbed systemically, while Acarbose is not.

Front: Why is Metformin withheld before giving IV radiocontrast?
Back: To prevent acute renal failure leading to Metformin-induced Lactic Acidosis.

Front: Effect of Pioglitazone on Lipid profiles?
Back: Increases HDL and lowers Triglycerides (more favorable than Rosiglitazone).

Front: MOA of Linagliptin?
Back: DPP-4 inhibitor; unique because it is excreted via bile (safe in renal failure).

Advanced Thyroid & Parathyroid
Front: MOA of Ipodate and Iopanoic acid?
Back: Radiopaque contrast media that also inhibit peripheral T4 to T3 conversion.

Front: Treatment for "Amiodarone-induced Thyrotoxicosis" Type 1?
Back: Thionamides (PTU/Methimazole).

Front: Treatment for "Amiodarone-induced Thyrotoxicosis" Type 2?
Back: Glucocorticoids (it is a destructive thyroiditis).

Front: MOA of Natpara?
Back: Recombinant human PTH (1-84) used for hypocalcemia in hypoparathyroidism.

Front: Effect of Thiazide diuretics on Calcium?
Back: Increase renal calcium reabsorption (used to prevent calcium stones).

Front: Effect of Loop diuretics on Calcium?
Back: Decrease renal calcium reabsorption ("Loops Lose Calcium").

Reproductive Toxicology & Oncology
Front: MOA of Megestrol acetate?
Back: Progestin used to treat anorexia and cachexia in AIDS or cancer patients.

Front: Side effect of Flutamide often monitored with LFTs?
Back: Hepatotoxicity.

Front: MOA of Finasteride in BPH?
Back: Reduces prostate volume by blocking DHT (takes 6–12 months for maximal effect).

Front: MOA of Degarelix?
Back: GnRH antagonist; provides immediate suppression of testosterone without the "flare."

Front: Preferred treatment for PCOS-related Hirsutism?
Back: Spironolactone or OCPs.

Front: MOA of Mifepristone in Cushing's Syndrome?
Back: Competitive Glucocorticoid receptor antagonist; controls hyperglycemia.

Adrenal Nuances & High-Yield Pearls
Front: MOA of Fludrocortisone?
Back: Potent mineralocorticoid analog with some glucocorticoid activity.

Front: Drug used to treat Pheochromocytoma before surgery?
Back: Phenoxybenzamine (irreversible alpha-blocker).

Front: Why must Alpha-blockers be given before Beta-blockers in Pheochromocytoma?
Back: To prevent hypertensive crisis from "unopposed alpha-stimulation."

Front: MOA of Aminoglutethimide?
Back: Inhibits conversion of Cholesterol to Pregnenolone (blocks all steroid synthesis).

Front: Major side effect of Metyrapone?
Back: Hirsutism (due to buildup of 11-deoxycortisol which shunts to androgens).

Pituitary & Posterior Lobe
Front: MOA of Pitocin?
Back: Synthetic Oxytocin used for labor induction and postpartum hemorrhage.

Front: Drug of choice for Central Diabetes Insipidus?
Back: Desmopressin (dDAVP).

Front: Why is Vasopressin used in Septic Shock?
Back: V1 receptor agonist causing potent peripheral vasoconstriction.

Front: MOA of Atosiban?
Back: Oxytocin receptor antagonist; used as a tocolytic to inhibit preterm labor.

Front: MOA of Goserelin?
Back: GnRH agonist; similar to Leuprolide.

Final Integration & Lab Values
Front: Effect of Estrogen on Thyroid Binding Globulin (TBG)?
Back: Increases TBG; leads to higher Total T4 levels (though Free T4 remains normal).

Front: Which drug class is known to cause "Blue-green vision" (Cyanopsia)?
Back: PDE-5 inhibitors (Sildenafil) due to inhibition of PDE-6 in the retina.

Front: MOA of Teduglutide?
Back: GLP-2 analog; used for Short Bowel Syndrome to enhance intestinal absorption.

Front: Drug of choice for medical management of Prolactinoma?
Back: Cabergoline (preferred over Bromocriptine due to better efficacy and fewer side effects).

Front: MOA of Tranexamic Acid in heavy menstrual bleeding?
Back: Antifibrinolytic; inhibits plasminogen activation.

Front: MOA of Clomiphene on the Pituitary?
Back: Blocks estrogen's negative feedback on the hypothalamus/pituitary → ↑ GnRH, FSH, LH.


Front: MOA of Sermorelin?
Back: GHRH analog; used as a diagnostic test for pituitary GH secretory capacity.

Front: Which somatostatin analog is most effective for Cushing’s Disease?
Back: Pasireotide (has a much higher affinity for SSTR5, which is prevalent in ACTH-secreting adenomas).

Front: MOA of Mecasermin rinfabate?
Back: Complex of recombinant IGF-1 and IGFBP-3; increases the half-life of IGF-1.

Thyroid Nuances
Front: MOA of the "Escaped" Wolff-Chaikoff effect?
Back: Downregulation of the Sodium-Iodide Symporter (NIS) to prevent excessive iodine entry into the thyroid.

Front: What is the "Jod-Basedow" phenomenon?
Back: Iodine-induced hyperthyroidism; occurs when excess iodine is given to a patient with a multinodular goiter or latent Graves' disease.

Front: Effect of glucocorticoids on thyroid hormone metabolism?
Back: Inhibit the peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 by inhibiting 5’-deiodinase.

Front: Management of Myxedema Coma?
Back: Loading dose of IV Levothyroxine (T4) + IV Liothyronine (T3) + Stress-dose Glucocorticoids.

Diabetes: Rare Side Effects & Interactions
Front: Which class of DM drugs is associated with "Fournier’s Gangrene"?
Back: SGLT2 Inhibitors (e.g., Empagliflozin, Canagliflozin).

Front: MOA of "Bromocriptine QR" in Type 2 DM?
Back: Resets circadian dopaminergic tone in the hypothalamus, which reduces insulin resistance and hepatic glucose production.

Front: Which DM medication is associated with a risk of "Cholestatic Hepatitis"?
Back: Sulfonylureas (specifically 1st generation like Chlorpropamide).

Front: Interaction between Salicylates (Aspirin) and Sulfonylureas?
Back: Salicylates displace Sulfonylureas from plasma proteins, increasing the risk of hypoglycemia.

Front: MOA of Welchol (Colesevelam) in DM therapy?
Back: Bile acid sequestrant that activates TGR5 or reduces FXR signaling, leading to decreased glucose levels.

Adrenal: Molecular Pathways
Front: MOA of Mitotane’s selective toxicity?
Back: Metabolized by adrenal mitochondria into a reactive acyl chloride that causes focal degeneration of the fasciculata and reticularis.

Front: Enzyme inhibited by Aminoglutethimide?
Back: CYP11A1 (Side-chain cleavage enzyme/Desmolase).

Front: MOA of LCI699 (Osilodrostat)?
Back: Potent 11β-hydroxylase inhibitor used for Cushing’s disease.

Front: Why is Dexamethasone used in the "Dexamethasone Suppression Test"?
Back: It provides potent negative feedback to the pituitary but does not interfere with the measurement of endogenous cortisol in the blood/urine.

Bone & Mineral Advanced Pharmacology
Front: MOA of Denosumab in Giant Cell Tumor of Bone?
Back: Inhibits RANKL, which is expressed by the stromal cells of the tumor, preventing osteoclast-mediated bone destruction.

Front: MOA of Sodium Fluoride in bone?
Back: Increases osteoblast proliferation and bone crystals; however, the new bone is often brittle and prone to fracture.

Front: Management of Paget’s Disease of Bone?
Back: Bisphosphonates (first line) or Calcitonin.

Front: MOA of Calcipotriene?
Back: Vitamin D analog used topically to treat Psoriasis by inhibiting keratinocyte proliferation.

Reproductive: Clinical Specifics
Front: MOA of Toremifene?
Back: SERM similar to Tamoxifen; used in postmenopausal women with ER-positive metastatic breast cancer.

Front: MOA of Bazedoxifene (used in Duavee)?
Back: SERM paired with conjugated estrogens to treat menopausal symptoms while protecting the endometrium.

Front: MOA of Degarelix vs. Cetrorelix?
Back: Degarelix is for Prostate Cancer (long-term suppression); Cetrorelix is for Infertility (short-term prevention of LH surge).

Front: Common side effect of Aromatase Inhibitors (Anastrozole) compared to Tamoxifen?
Back: Increased risk of osteoporosis and joint pain (due to total estrogen depletion).

Front: MOA of Indomethacin as a tocolytic?
Back: Non-selective COX inhibitor; decreases prostaglandin synthesis to stop uterine contractions.

Front: Side effect of Indomethacin on the fetus when used as a tocolytic?
Back: Premature closure of the Ductus Arteriosus and Oligohydramnios.

Front: MOA of Nifedipine as a tocolytic?
Back: DHP-Calcium Channel Blocker; prevents Ca2+ influx into myometrial cells to induce relaxation.

Miscellaneous
Front: MOA of Tesamorelin?
Back: GHRH analog that reduces abdominal fat in HIV patients with lipodystrophy.

Front: Drug used to treat nocturnal enuresis by increasing water reabsorption?
Back: Desmopressin (dDAVP).

Front: MOA of V2 receptor activation in the collecting duct?
Back: Gs-coupled → ↑ cAMP → PKA phosphorylation → insertion of Aquaporin-2 channels into the apical membrane.

Front: MOA of V1 receptor activation in vascular smooth muscle?
Back: Gq-coupled → ↑ IP3/DAG → ↑ intracellular Ca2+ → Vasoconstriction.

Front: Which endocrine drug is famously associated with "Disulfiram-like reactions" with alcohol?
Back: Chlorpropamide (1st gen Sulfonylurea).

Front: Which DM drug's metabolism is affected by CYP2C9 polymorphism?
Back: Sulfonylureas (e.g., Glimepiride); slow metabolizers have a significantly higher risk of prolonged hypoglycemia.

Front: Effect of Cholestyramine on Levothyroxine absorption?
Back: Significantly decreases absorption; Levothyroxine must be taken 4 hours apart from bile acid sequestrants.

Front: Why is Methimazole preferred for "Thyroid Storm" after initial stabilization?
Back: It has a longer duration of action and faster onset of intra-thyroidal action once the T4-to-T3 peripheral conversion is controlled.

Front: MOA of Estrogen's effect on total T4 levels?
Back: Estrogen increases Thyroid-Binding Globulin (TBG) in the liver; leads to an increase in Total T4, though Free T4 remains normal (euthyroid).

Adrenal & Steroid Toxicity
Front: Why does chronic Glucocorticoid therapy cause "Steroid Diabetes"?
Back: Due to increased hepatic gluconeogenesis and inhibition of peripheral glucose uptake (insulin resistance).

Front: Effect of Glucocorticoids on Osteoprotegerin (OPG)?
Back: They decrease OPG and increase RANKL expression, leading to increased osteoclast activity and bone loss.

Front: MOA of Abiraterone-induced mineralocorticoid excess?
Back: Inhibiting CYP17 causes a buildup of upstream mineralocorticoid precursors (e.g., Deoxycorticosterone); treated by co-administering Prednisone.

Infertility & Pregnancy Pharmacology
Front: MOA of Menotropins (hMG)?
Back: Purified FSH and LH obtained from the urine of postmenopausal women; used to stimulate follicular development.

Front: MOA of Urofollitropin?
Back: Purified FSH; used for ovulation induction in women with PCOS or for assisted reproduction.

Front: What is "Ovarian Hyperstimulation Syndrome" (OHSS)?
Back: A life-threatening complication of ovulation induction; characterized by ovarian enlargement, ascites, and hemoconcentration (mediated by VEGF).

Front: Drug used to treat "Endometriosis-associated pain" via GnRH antagonism?
Back: Elagolix (oral GnRH antagonist).

Bone & Mineral: The "Final Frontier"
Front: MOA of Calcitriol vs. Calcifediol?
Back: Calcifediol is 25-(OH)D; Calcitriol is 1,25-(OH)2D (the active form).

Front: Why is Calcitriol preferred in patients with end-stage renal disease (ESRD)?
Back: ESRD patients lack 1-alpha-hydroxylase in the kidney, so they cannot convert 25-(OH)D to active Calcitriol.

Front: MOA of Denosumab's effect on the immune system?
Back: RANKL is also found on T-lymphocytes; Denosumab may slightly increase the risk of serious infections (cellulitis).

Front: MOA of Raloxifene's effect on LDL cholesterol?
Back: It acts as an estrogen agonist in the liver, leading to a decrease in LDL cholesterol.

Clinical "Red Flags" & Contraindications
Front: Absolute contraindication for the use of OCPs in women >35?
Back: Smoking >15 cigarettes per day (due to high risk of thromboembolism/stroke).

Front: Why is the "Mini-pill" (Progestin-only) preferred for lactating women?
Back: Unlike Estrogen, Progestin does not interfere with the quantity or quality of breast milk.

Front: Contraindication for the use of Bisphosphonates?
Back: Hypocalcemia (must be corrected first) and esophageal strictures/achalasia.

Front: Which endocrine drug is famously associated with "Yellow-halo vision"?
Back: Not primarily endocrine, but Digoxin (often tested alongside endocrine electrolyte disturbances like Hypokalemia).

Front: MOA of Bromocriptine in treating Type 2 DM?
Back: It is a "Quick-Release" (QR) formulation that acts on the hypothalamus to improve insulin sensitivity via circadian rhythm modulation.


Front: MOA of Estrogen's cardioprotective effect?
Back: Increases HDL, decreases LDL, and induces vasodilation by increasing Nitric Oxide (NO) production.

Front: Why do Glucocorticoids cause "Neutrophilia" while being immunosuppressive?
Back: They inhibit neutrophil adhesion molecule expression, causing "demargination" of neutrophils from the vessel walls into the blood.

Front: MOA of the interaction between Spironolactone and ACE Inhibitors?
Back: Synergistic risk of hyperkalemia (both decrease aldosterone's effect on potassium excretion).

Front: Effect of Thyroid hormone on the heart's Beta-1 receptors?
Back: Increases the number and sensitivity of Beta-1 receptors (upregulation), leading to tachycardia and increased contractility.

Specific Metabolic Effects
Front: Which DM drug class is known to decrease levels of Plasminogen Activator Inhibitor-1 (PAI-1)?
Back: Thiazolidinediones (TZDs); this contributes to their potential cardiovascular benefits.

Front: MOA of "Somatopause" treatment?
Back: Use of Growth Hormone (GH) or GHRH analogs in elderly patients to increase lean muscle mass and decrease fat (though clinically controversial).

Front: How do Glucocorticoids affect the synthesis of Lipocortin-1 (Annexin A1)?
Back: They induce its synthesis, which then inhibits Phospholipase A2, blocking the release of Arachidonic Acid.

Comparative Pharmacology
Front: Compare the half-lives of Methimazole vs. PTU.
Back: Methimazole: ~6 hours (once daily dosing); PTU: ~1.5 hours (needs 2–3 doses/day).

Front: Primary difference in indication between Alprostadil and Misoprostol?
Back: Alprostadil: PDA maintenance or ED; Misoprostol: NSAID-induced ulcer prevention or medical abortion.

Front: Comparison: Potency of Dexamethasone vs. Hydrocortisone?
Back: Dexamethasone is ~25–30 times more potent with negligible mineralocorticoid activity.

The "Must-Know" Toxicities (Step 1 Classics)
Front: Which Endocrine drug causes "Red-man syndrome" if infused too quickly?
Back: None—but be careful not to confuse "Red-man" (Vancomycin) with the "Flushing" caused by Niacin or the "Hot Flashes" caused by Tamoxifen/Raloxifene.

Front: MOA of Levothyroxine-induced cardiac arrhythmias?
Back: Excessive T4 levels mimic hyperthyroidism, increasing myocardial oxygen demand and sympathetic sensitivity.

Front: Side effect of Octreotide on the Exocrine Pancreas?
Back: Steatorrhea (due to inhibition of pancreatic enzyme secretion).

Front: Drug used to treat "Osteoporosis" that carries a risk of Venous Thromboembolism (VTE)?
Back: SERMs (Raloxifene).


=====================================================================
=====================================================================


Clinical Toxicology:

Front: Immediate priority in any acute poisoning?

Back: Stabilization of ABCs (Airway, Breathing, Circulation).

Front: Formula for calculating the Anion Gap (AG)?

Back: $Na - (Cl + HCO_3)$. Normal range: 8–12 mEq/L.

Front: Most effective timeframe for Activated Charcoal administration?

Back: Within 1 hour of ingestion.

Front: Substances poorly adsorbed by Activated Charcoal? (Mnemonic: PHAILS)

Back: Pesticides, Hydrocarbons, Acids/Alkali, Iron, Lithium, Solvents (also Alcohols).

Front: Indications for Whole Bowel Irrigation (WBI)?

Back: Body packers, iron, lithium, or sustained-release enteric-coated tablets.

Front: Primary complication of Gastric Lavage?

Back: Aspiration pneumonia or esophageal perforation.

Front: Antidote for Acetaminophen toxicity?

Back: N-acetylcysteine (NAC).

Front: Mechanism of NAC in Acetaminophen overdose?

Back: Restores glutathione stores and acts as a glutathione substitute.

Front: Timeframe for optimal NAC efficacy?

Back: Within 8 hours of ingestion.

Front: Tool used to predict hepatotoxicity after acute Acetaminophen ingestion?

Back: Rumack-Matthew Nomogram.

Section 2: Autonomic Toxidromes

Front: Classic Toxidrome: Mydriasis, tachycardia, hyperthermia, dry skin, urinary retention?

Back: Anticholinergic (Atropine, TCAs, Antihistamines).

Front: Classic Toxidrome: Miosis, bradycardia, diaphoresis, salivation, lacrimation?

Back: Cholinergic (Organophosphates, Carbamates).

Front: Cholinergic mnemonic: DUMBELS?

Back: Diarrhea, Urination, Miosis, Bradycardia/Bronchospasm, Emesis, Lacrimation, Salivation.

Front: Antidote for Anticholinergic toxicity (if severe CNS symptoms)?

Back: Physostigmine.

Front: Contraindication for Physostigmine?

Back: Suspected TCA overdose (increases risk of asystole).

Front: Treatment for Organophosphate poisoning (Muscarinic vs. Nicotinic)?

Back: Atropine (Muscarinic); Pralidoxime/2-PAM (Nicotinic/Muscle fasciculations).

Section 3: Cardiovascular Toxins

Front: First-line treatment for Beta-blocker overdose?

Back: Glucagon (increases cAMP via non-adrenergic pathway).

Front: Treatment for Calcium Channel Blocker (CCB) toxicity (Refractory)?

Back: High-dose Insulin euglycemic therapy (HIET).

Front: ECG finding classic for Digoxin toxicity?

Back: "Scooped" ST segments (Digoxin effect) or any arrhythmia (PVCs most common).

Front: Electrolyte abnormality that worsens Digoxin toxicity?

Back: Hypokalemia (K+ and Digoxin compete for Na+/K+ ATPase).

Front: Definitive treatment for severe Digoxin toxicity?

Back: Digoxin-specific antibody fragments (DigiFab).

Front: Antidote for Heparin reversal?

Back: Protamine sulfate.

Front: Antidote for Warfarin (Acute/Life-threatening)?

Back: Fresh Frozen Plasma (FFP) or Prothrombin Complex Concentrate (PCC).

Front: Antidote for Warfarin (Non-emergent)?

Back: Vitamin K (Phytonadione).

Section 4: Alcohols & Metabolic Toxins

Front: Toxic metabolite of Methanol?

Back: Formic acid.

Front: Characteristic organ damage in Methanol poisoning?

Back: Retinal injury (blindness) and Basal Ganglia necrosis.

Front: Toxic metabolite of Ethylene Glycol?

Back: Oxalic acid (Glycolic acid).

Front: Characteristic renal finding in Ethylene Glycol poisoning?

Back: Calcium oxalate crystals in urine (envelope-shaped).

Front: Preferred inhibitor of Alcohol Dehydrogenase in Methanol/Ethylene Glycol poisoning?

Back: Fomepizole.

Front: Why is Ethanol used if Fomepizole is unavailable?

Back: Higher affinity for Alcohol Dehydrogenase than toxic alcohols.

Front: Alcohol ingestion with high Osmolal Gap but NO Anion Gap acidosis?

Back: Isopropyl Alcohol.

Front: Classic triad of Wernicke’s Encephalopathy?

Back: Ataxia, Ophthalmoplegia, Confusion.

Section 5: Sedative-Hypnotics & Drugs of Abuse

Front: Antidote for Benzodiazepine overdose?

Back: Flumazenil.

Front: Major risk of Flumazenil use in chronic BZD users?

Back: Precipitation of seizures.

Front: Opioid withdrawal symptoms?

Back: Mydriasis, piloerection (cold turkey), lacrimation, diarrhea, yawning.

Front: Treatment for Cocaine-induced chest pain (Class of drug to avoid)?

Back: Avoid Beta-blockers (risk of unopposed alpha-adrenergic stimulation).

Front: Life-threatening complication of MDMA (Ecstasy)?

Back: Hyponatremia and Hyperthermia.

Front: Characteristic "nystagmus" in PCP (Phencyclidine) intoxication?

Back: Rotary (or vertical/horizontal) nystagmus.

Front: Antidote for Cyanide poisoning (classic triple-kit)?

Back: Amyl nitrite, Sodium nitrite, Sodium thiosulfate.

Front: Modern preferred Cyanide antidote (turns urine red)?

Back: Hydroxocobalamin (Vitamin B12a).

Section 6: Heavy Metals

Front: Treatment for Lead poisoning (Adult vs. Pediatric)?

Back: Adult: Succimer or EDTA. Pediatric: Succimer (oral) or Dimercaprol + EDTA (if severe).

Front: Classic radiographic finding in pediatric Lead poisoning?

Back: Lead lines at the metaphyses of long bones.

Front: Antidote for Acute Iron poisoning?

Back: Deferoxamine (Vin-rosé urine).

Front: Antidote for Arsenic or Mercury poisoning?

Back: Dimercaprol (BAL) or Succimer (DMSA).

Front: Antidote for Copper toxicity (Wilson’s Disease)?

Back: Penicillamine.

Section 7: Envenomation & Environmental

Front: First-line treatment for Black Widow spider bite (latrodectism)?

Back: Benzodiazepines and Opioids (muscle relaxants).

Front: Characteristic skin finding of Brown Recluse bite?

Back: "Red, white, and blue" lesion (central necrosis/eschar).

Front: Indication for CroFab antivenom in Pit Viper bites?

Back: Progressive swelling or systemic coagulopathy.

Front: Management of Carbon Monoxide (CO) poisoning?

Back: 100% Oxygen (reduces CO-Hb half-life). Hyperbaric $O_2$ if severe.

Front: Color of blood in Methemoglobinemia?

Back: Chocolate-colored blood.

Front: Treatment for Methemoglobinemia?

Back: Methylene Blue.

Section 8: Psychotropic & Neurologic Tox

Front: Triad of Serotonin Syndrome?

Back: Autonomic instability, Altered mental status, Neuromuscular irritability (Hyperreflexia/Clonus).

Front: Antidote for Serotonin Syndrome?

Back: Cyproheptadine (5-HT2 antagonist).

Front: Key difference between Serotonin Syndrome and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS)?

Back: NMS has "Lead-pipe" rigidity; Serotonin Syndrome has hyperreflexia/clonus.

Front: Treatment for NMS?

Back: Dantrolene or Bromocriptine.

Front: ECG hallmark of TCA toxicity?

Back: Widened QRS complex (due to Na+ channel blockade).

Front: Treatment for TCA-induced QRS widening?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate ($NaHCO_3$).

Front: Treatment for Lithium toxicity (Severe)?

Back: Hemodialysis.

Section 9: Miscellaneous High-Yield

Front: Isoniazid (INH) toxicity triad?

Back: Seizures, Acidosis, Coma.

Front: Antidote for Isoniazid-induced seizures?

Back: Pyridoxine (Vitamin B6).

Front: Salicylate poisoning (Early vs. Late acid-base status)?

Back: Early: Respiratory Alkalosis. Late: Mixed AG Metabolic Acidosis and Respiratory Alkalosis.

Front: Effect of Salicylates on the mitochondria?

Back: Uncoupling of oxidative phosphorylation.

Front: Why is Sodium Bicarbonate used in Salicylate poisoning?

Back: To alkalinize urine (Ion Trapping) and enhance excretion.

Front: Classic finding in Theophylline toxicity?

Back: Severe hypokalemia and seizures.

Front: Toxicity of Sulfonylureas (Management)?

Back: Octreotide (inhibits insulin release).

Front: Mechanism of Carbon Monoxide toxicity?

Back: Left-shift of the Oxygen-Hemoglobin dissociation curve (inhibits $O_2$ unloading).

Front: Mushroom toxin causing delayed hepatic failure (Amanita phalloides)?

Back: Alpha-amanitin (inhibits RNA polymerase II).

Front: Antidote for Valproic acid-induced hyperammonemia?

Back: L-carnitine.

Section 10: Advanced Tox Pearls

Front: What causes "Red Man Syndrome" and how to treat?

Back: Vancomycin (rapid infusion); slow the infusion and give antihistamines.

Front: Mechanism of Cyanide's cellular toxicity?

Back: Inhibits Cytochrome c Oxidase (Complex IV) in the electron transport chain.

Front: Characteristic breath odor of Arsenic/Organophosphates?

Back: Garlic-like odor.

Front: Breath odor of Cyanide?

Back: Bitter almonds.

Front: Management of Hydrofluoric Acid skin burns?

Back: Topical Calcium Gluconate gel.

Front: Drug used to "trap" weakly acidic drugs in urine?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate (Alkalinization).

Front: Drug used to "trap" weakly basic drugs in urine?

Back: Ammonium Chloride (Acidification - though rarely used clinically).

Front: Classic feature of Ciguatera fish poisoning?

Back: Temperature reversal (cold feels hot, hot feels cold).

Front: Ethylene Glycol vs. Methanol (Commonality)?

Back: Both cause a high Anion Gap and high Osmolal Gap.

Front: Most common cause of death in TCA toxicity?

Back: Arrhythmias (due to Sodium channel blockade).

Front: Visual "halos" or yellow-green vision?

Back: Digoxin toxicity (Xanthopsia).

Front: Treatment for Body Packers (leaking drug packets)?

Back: Surgical intervention if packets are obstructed or leaking.

Front: Antidote for Beta-blockers if Glucagon fails?

Back: High-dose Insulin or Intravenous Lipid Emulsion (ILE).

Front: Which metal toxicity causes "Mad Hatter" symptoms?

Back: Mercury (Erethism mercurialis).

Front: Management of extrapyramidal symptoms (EPS) from Metoclopramide?

Back: Diphenhydramine or Benztropine.

Front: Why is Glucose given with Thiamine in alcoholics?

Back: Thiamine before Glucose to prevent Wernicke’s precipitation.

Front: "Rice-water stools" and QTc prolongation?

Back: Arsenic poisoning.

Front: Methemoglobinemia inducer (Classic antibiotic)?

Back: Dapsone or Sulfonamides.

Front: Treatment for Cocaine-induced hypertensive crisis?

Back: Phentolamine (Alpha-blocker) or Benzodiazepines.

Front: Characteristic CBC finding in chronic Lead poisoning?

Back: Basophilic stippling.

Front: Characteristic bone marrow finding in Sideroblastic anemia (Lead)?

Back: Ringed sideroblasts.

Front: Drug-induced Lupus (common toxins)?

Back: Procainamide, Hydralazine, Isoniazid (HIP).

Front: Tinnitus and hyperpnea?

Back: Salicylate (Aspirin) toxicity.

Front: Treatment for severe Methanol toxicity if Fomepizole fails?

Back: Hemodialysis and Folate.

Front: Treatment for severe Ethylene Glycol toxicity if Fomepizole fails?

Back: Hemodialysis and Thiamine/Pyridoxine.

Front: Gray Baby Syndrome cause?

Back: Chloramphenicol.

Front: Mechanism of Local Anesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST)?

Back: Sodium channel blockade; treat with Lipid Emulsion.

Front: First-line for Chlorine gas inhalation?

Back: Humidified oxygen and nebulized Sodium Bicarbonate.

Front: "Dry as a bone, Red as a beet, Mad as a hatter"?

Back: Anticholinergic Toxidrome.

Front: Mechanism of Cocaine’s effect on neurotransmitters?

Back: Inhibits reuptake of Norepinephrine, Dopamine, and Serotonin.

Front: Organophosphate mnemonic: SLUDGE?

Back: Salivation, Lacrimation, Urination, Defecation, GI distress, Emesis.

Front: Difference between Type 1 and Type 2 Hypersensitivity?

Back: Type 1 (IgE/Mast cell); Type 2 (Antibody-mediated cytotoxic).

Front: Antidote for Isoniazid (Primary CNS risk)?

Back: Pyridoxine (B6).

Front: "Mees' lines" (transverse white bands on nails)?

Back: Arsenic poisoning.

Front: Difference between Cocaine and Amphetamine pupils?

Back: Both cause Mydriasis, but Cocaine has shorter duration and localized vasoconstriction.

Front: Management of Serotonin Syndrome hyperthermia?

Back: External cooling and Benzodiazepines (not Antipyretics).

Front: Most common ECG abnormality in CO poisoning?

Back: Sinus tachycardia; ST-depression suggests ischemia.

Front: Gold standard for measuring Carboxyhemoglobin?

Back: Arterial/Venous Blood Gas with Co-oximetry (Pulse ox is unreliable).

Front: Why is pulse oximetry normal in Methemoglobinemia?

Back: Pulse ox often "reads" Met-Hb as 85%, regardless of true saturation.

Front: Fatal dose of Acetaminophen (Adult)?

Back: Approx. 150 mg/kg or >7.5 to 10 grams.


Front: Mechanism of action of Pralidoxime (2-PAM)?

Back: Regenerates active Acetylcholinesterase by removing the phosphate group from the enzyme (if given before "aging").

Front: Why is Atropine given before Pralidoxime in Organophosphate poisoning?

Back: To prevent a transient worsening of symptoms caused by 2-PAM's weak transient inhibition of AChE.

Front: Antidote for Magnesium Sulfate toxicity?

Back: Calcium Gluconate (antagonizes neuromuscular blockade).

Front: Specific antidote for Hydrofluoric acid systemic toxicity?

Back: Intravenous Calcium and Magnesium (HF causes profound hypocalcemia/hypomagnesemia).

Front: Treatment for Bromide toxicity (Bromism)?

Back: Sodium Chloride (Chloride competes with Bromide for renal reabsorption).

Front: Antidote for Caffeine or Theophylline-induced tachyarrhythmias?

Back: Esmolol (Short-acting Beta-blocker).

Section 12: Laboratory & Diagnostic Toxicology

Front: Formula for Serum Osmolality?

Back: $2(Na) + \frac{Glucose}{18} + \frac{BUN}{2.8}$.

Front: Formula for Osmolal Gap?

Back: $Measured\ Osmolality - Calculated\ Osmolality$. (Normal < 10).

Front: Causes of "Normal Anion Gap" Metabolic Acidosis (HARDUP)?

Back: Hyperalimentation, Acetazolamide, Renal Tubular Acidosis, Diarrhea, Ureteroenteric fistula, Pancreatic fistula.

Front: Co-oximetry finding in Methemoglobinemia?

Back: Absorption peak at 630 nm.

Front: What does "Double Gap" refer to in Toxicology?

Back: Simultaneous presence of an Anion Gap and an Osmolal Gap (Typical of Methanol/Ethylene Glycol).

Section 13: Plant & Natural Toxins

Front: Toxin in Foxglove (Digitalis purpurea)?

Back: Cardiac glycosides (Digoxin-like effect).

Front: Toxin in Jimson Weed (Datura stramonium)?

Back: Scopolamine/Atropine (causes Anticholinergic toxidrome).

Front: Poison Hemlock (Conium maculatum) mechanism?

Back: Nicotinic receptor antagonist (causes ascending paralysis).

Front: Clinical effect of Water Hemlock (Cicutoxin)?

Back: Status epilepticus (GABA antagonist).

Front: Mechanism of Ricin (Castor beans)?

Back: Inhibits the 60S ribosomal subunit (stops protein synthesis).

Front: Management of Jellyfish stings (Box jellyfish)?

Back: Acetic acid (Vinegar) to deactivate nematocysts; avoid fresh water.

Section 14: Industrial & Chemical Toxins

Front: "Monday Disease" in dynamite factory workers?

Back: Nitroglycerin exposure; withdrawal over the weekend causes vasospasm and chest pain.

Front: Most common cause of death in Fire victims (besides burns)?

Back: Combined Cyanide and Carbon Monoxide poisoning.

Front: Vinyl Chloride chronic exposure risk?

Back: Hepatic Angiosarcoma.

Front: Mechanism of Paraquat (herbicide) toxicity?

Back: Redox cycling produces reactive oxygen species; leads to progressive Pulmonary Fibrosis.

Front: Ethylene Dibromide primary organ toxicity?

Back: Severe hepatic necrosis and nephrotoxicity.

Section 15: Pediatric Specific Toxicology

Front: Most common cause of pediatric poisoning death (supplement)?

Back: Iron (Prenatal vitamins).

Front: Management of a child who swallowed a single "button battery" (Asymptomatic, in stomach)?

Back: Wait and watch (repeat X-ray); if in esophagus, it is a surgical emergency.

Front: Toxic dose of Salicylates in children?

Back: $>150\ mg/kg$.

Front: Why is Oil of Wintergreen dangerous in small amounts for children?

Back: Contains concentrated Methyl Salicylate (1 tsp = ~21 adult aspirins).

Section 16: Withdrawal Syndromes

Front: Earliest sign of Ethanol withdrawal?

Back: Tremulousness ("The Shakes") at 6–12 hours.

Front: Timeframe for Delirium Tremens (DTs)?

Back: 48–96 hours after the last drink.

Front: Treatment for Alcohol withdrawal seizures?

Back: Benzodiazepines (Phenytoin is ineffective).

Front: Neonatal Abstinence Syndrome (NAS) symptoms?

Back: High-pitched cry, tremors, sneezing, diarrhea, tachypnea.

Front: GHB (Gamma-Hydroxybutyrate) withdrawal hallmark?

Back: Severe agitation and autonomic instability (similar to severe EtOH withdrawal).

Section 17: "The Goldfrank Pearls" (Refined Management)

Front: Preferred route for NAC (IV vs Oral)?

Back: Both effective; IV preferred for vomiting, hepatic failure, or pregnancy.

Front: Indication for Urinary Alkalinization in Phenobarbital overdose?

Back: Enhances elimination of weak acids.

Front: Why avoid Ipecac syrup in modern toxicology?

Back: Risk of aspiration and interference with Activated Charcoal/Endoscopy.

Front: Dose-limiting toxicity of Deferoxamine?

Back: ARDS (pulmonary toxicity) with prolonged infusions (>24 hrs).

Front: Primary treatment for Hydrocarbon aspiration?

Back: Supportive care; avoid Gastric Lavage (increased risk of aspiration).

Front: Mechanism of Pyridoxine in Ethylene Glycol poisoning?

Back: Diverts Glyoxylic acid metabolism toward Glycine (non-toxic) instead of Oxalate.

Front: "Saturday Night Palsy" in a toxic patient?

Back: Radial nerve compression due to prolonged immobilization while intoxicated.


Front: Mechanism of "Red Man Syndrome" from Vancomycin?

Back: Non-immunologic (Direct) mast cell degranulation and histamine release; not a true allergy.

Front: Management of Valproic Acid-induced hyperammonemic encephalopathy?

Back: L-carnitine supplementation.

Front: Classic ECG finding in Citalopram/Escitalopram overdose?

Back: QTc prolongation (risk of Torsades de Pointes).

Front: Management of Dystonic reaction from Antipsychotics?

Back: Diphenhydramine (IV/IM) or Benztropine.

Front: Why is Glucose alone insufficient in an alcoholic with hypoglycemia?

Back: Must give Thiamine first to prevent Wernicke’s Encephalopathy.

Front: Drug of choice for hypertensive crisis caused by MAO-Inhibitors + Tyramine?

Back: Phentolamine (Alpha-blocker).

Front: Propofol Infusion Syndrome (PRIS) hallmarks?

Back: Metabolic acidosis, rhabdomyolysis, hyperkalemia, and cardiac failure.

Section 19: Environmental & Occupational Toxins

Front: Mechanism of Methemoglobinemia caused by Nitrites?

Back: Oxidizes Ferrous iron ($Fe^{2+}$) to Ferric iron ($Fe^{3+}$) in hemoglobin.

Front: High-yield source of Acrylonitrile poisoning?

Back: Burning plastics and synthetic rubber (presents similarly to Cyanide).

Front: Treatment for Hydrogen Sulfide ($H_2S$) poisoning?

Back: Nitrites (to create Sulfmethemoglobin) and 100% Oxygen.

Front: Toxic element in "Magic Shaving Powder" or older depilatories?

Back: Barium (causes profound hypokalemia and paralysis).

Front: Cause of "Metal Fume Fever"?

Back: Inhalation of Zinc Oxide fumes (common in welding).

Section 20: Botanical & Food-Borne Toxins

Front: Toxin in Pufferfish (Fugu)?

Back: Tetrodotoxin.

Front: Mechanism of Tetrodotoxin?

Back: Blocks voltage-gated Sodium channels (prevents action potential).

Front: Scombroid poisoning cause?

Back: Histamine ingestion from improperly refrigerated fish (Tuna, Mahi-mahi).

Front: Scombroid poisoning presentation?

Back: Flushed skin, peppery taste in mouth, headache, and diarrhea (treat with antihistamines).

Front: Toxin in "Death Cap" mushrooms (Amanita phalloides)?

Back: Amatoxin (inhibits RNA polymerase II).

Section 21: High-Yield Acid-Base & Electrolyte "Triggers"

Front: "Cat's eye" (Mydriasis) + High Anion Gap Acidosis?

Back: Methanol poisoning.

Front: Hypocalcemia + High Anion Gap Acidosis?

Back: Ethylene Glycol poisoning (Calcium is consumed by oxalate).

Front: Seizures + High Anion Gap Acidosis (Refractory to BZDs)?

Back: Isoniazid (INH) toxicity.

Front: Normal AG Acidosis + Nephrolithiasis + Hypokalemia?

Back: Topiramate toxicity (Carbonic Anhydrase inhibition).

Section 22: Procedures & Disposition

Front: Indication for Hemodialysis in Salicylate poisoning?

Back: Level > 100 mg/dL (acute), altered mental status, or renal failure.

Front: Indication for Hemodialysis in Lithium poisoning?

Back: Level > 4.0 mEq/L (acute) or > 2.5 mEq/L (chronic with symptoms).

Front: When is Multi-dose Activated Charcoal (MDAC) indicated?

Back: For "Theophylline, Phenobarbital, Carbamazepine, Dapsone, Quinine" (Mnemonic: ABCD-Q).

Front: Maximum duration for NAC infusion (Standard IV protocol)?

Back: 21 hours (total of 3 bags).

Front: Primary risk of performing Gastric Lavage on a Hydrocarbon ingestion?

Back: Chemical pneumonitis (aspiration risk outweighs benefit).

Section 23: Clinical "Must-Knows"

Front: Most common cause of late-onset death in Paraquat ingestion?

Back: Progressive Pulmonary Fibrosis.

Front: Why should you avoid "Vigorous" suctioning in a Caustic ingestion?

Back: Risk of esophageal perforation.

Front: Drug used to treat Serotonin Syndrome when BZDs fail?

Back: Cyproheptadine.

Front: Name of the scale used to assess Opioid withdrawal?

Back: COWS (Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale).

Front: Treatment for Black Widow spider bite with severe muscle spasms?

Back: Benzodiazepines and Antivenom (if available/severe).

Front: Classic feature of Ergotism?

Back: Intense vasoconstriction leading to "St. Anthony’s Fire" (burning pain and gangrene).

Front: The "Antidote" for Beta-blockers that acts via Myocardial G-protein?

Back: Glucagon.

Front: Mechanism of Octreotide in Sulfonylurea overdose?

Back: Somatostatin analog that suppresses Insulin release from Beta cells.

Front: Zero-order kinetics definition?

Back: Constant amount of drug is eliminated per unit time (e.g., Ethanol, Salicylates/ASA at high doses, Phenytoin).

Front: First-order kinetics definition?

Back: Constant fraction of drug is eliminated per unit time (Most drugs).

Front: Volume of Distribution ($V_d$) formula?

Back: $V_d = \frac{Total\ amount\ of\ drug\ in\ body}{Plasma\ drug\ concentration}$.

Front: How does $V_d$ affect hemodialysis efficacy?

Back: Drugs with a large $V_d$ (> 1 L/kg) are not easily removed by dialysis because they are "hidden" in tissues.

Front: Definition of "Enterohepatic Circulation" in toxicology?

Back: Drugs excreted in bile are reabsorbed in the small intestine (e.g., TCAs, Phenobarbital); targeted by Multi-dose Activated Charcoal.

Section 25: The "ECG" Tox-Screen

Front: Specific ECG change in Phenothiazine (Antipsychotic) overdose?

Back: Blunting of T-waves and prominent U-waves.

Front: ECG sign of hyperkalemia (Common in Digoxin/Renal tox)?

Back: Peaked T-waves, PR prolongation, and eventually "Sine Wave" pattern.

Front: Brugada Pattern (Pseudo-right bundle branch block) is associated with which toxicity?

Back: Sodium Channel Blocker overdose (e.g., Cocaine, TCAs, Flecainide).

Front: "Osborn Wave" (J-wave) on ECG?

Back: Characteristic of Hypothermia (often seen in intoxicated "found down" patients).

Section 26: Pesticides & Chemical Warfare

Front: Mechanism of Cyanogen Chloride or Hydrogen Cyanide?

Back: Arrests aerobic respiration by inhibiting Cytochrome c Oxidase.

Front: Nerve agent (Sarin, VX) mechanism?

Back: Irreversible inhibition of Acetylcholinesterase (more potent than organophosphates).

Front: Difference between Carbamate and Organophosphate poisoning?

Back: Carbamate binding is reversible and does not undergo "aging"; 2-PAM is usually not required.

Front: Primary cause of death in Cholinergic crisis?

Back: "The Killer Bs": Bronchospasm and Bronchorrhea (respiratory failure).

Section 27: Reproductive & Pregnancy Toxicology

Front: Pregnancy category of ACE Inhibitors/ARBs?

Back: Teratogenic (Category X/D); causes fetal renal dysgenesis and skull hypoplasia.

Front: Preferred treatment for Lead poisoning in a pregnant patient?

Back: Chelation is generally avoided unless maternal life is at risk; consult specialists (Succimer is often used).

Front: Fetal Alcohol Syndrome (FAS) facial features?

Back: Smooth philtrum, thin upper lip, and short palpebral fissures.

Front: Is NAC safe to give in pregnancy for Acetaminophen overdose?

Back: Yes, it crosses the placenta and protects the fetus.

Section 28: Miscellaneous Board-Style "Must-Knows"

Front: "Red wheals" or "flagellate dermatitis" after eating mushrooms?

Back: Shiitake Mushroom dermatitis.

Front: Treatment for Methemoglobinemia in patients with G6PD deficiency?

Back: Avoid Methylene Blue (risk of hemolysis); use Vitamin C or Exchange Transfusion.

Front: Definition of the "Window Period" in Acetaminophen ingestion?

Back: The time after the initial GI symptoms resolve but before liver enzymes (ALT/AST) spike.

Front: Why is Calcium Chloride preferred over Calcium Gluconate in a code situation?

Back: Calcium Chloride provides 3x more elemental calcium and does not require hepatic metabolism.

Front: Management of Beta-blocker/CCB overdose (the "Lipid Sink" theory)?

Back: Intravenous Lipid Emulsion (ILE) to sequester lipophilic drugs.

Front: Mechanism of N-acetylcysteine in non-Acetaminophen liver failure?

Back: Improves systemic hemodynamics and oxygen delivery to tissues.

Front: What toxin causes "Phossy Jaw" (bone necrosis)?

Back: White Phosphorus (industrial exposure).

Front: Mechanism of "Body Stuffers" vs. "Body Packers"?

Back: Stuffers: Swallow small amounts in a panic (high risk of rupture). Packers: Professional transport (well-wrapped, lower immediate risk).

Front: Name of the criteria used to diagnose Serotonin Syndrome?

Back: Hunter Criteria.

Front: How to treat Valproic Acid overdose with metabolic crisis?

Back: Hemodialysis and L-carnitine.

Front: The "Tox-Handshake": What four things do you check in every poisoned patient?
Back: 1. Mental Status, 2. Pupil Size, 3. Skin Moisture/Temperature, 4. Vital Signs.


Front: Definition of "The Toxic Window"?

Back: The time between drug ingestion and the onset of organ damage where intervention (like NAC for APAP) is most effective.

Front: Most common cause of a falsely elevated Pulse Oximetry reading?

Back: Carbon Monoxide poisoning (Carboxyhemoglobin is misread as Oxyhemoglobin).

Front: Most common cause of a falsely LOW Pulse Oximetry reading (around 85%)?

Back: Methemoglobinemia.

Front: Formula for "Clearing" a drug via Hemodialysis?

Back: High water solubility, low molecular weight (< 500 Da), low protein binding, and small Volume of Distribution ($V_d$).

Front: Mechanism of "Ion Trapping" in the urine?

Back: Adjusting urine pH to keep a toxin in its ionized (charged) state, preventing reabsorption across the renal tubule.

Section 30: Specialized Antidote Protocols

Front: Why is Sodium Bicarbonate ($NaHCO_3$) given in Sodium Channel Blocker toxicity (e.g., TCAs)?

Back: It increases extracellular $Na^+$ and increases serum pH, which decreases the drug’s affinity for the $Na^+$ channel.

Front: Dose of Glucagon for Beta-blocker overdose?

Back: 3–10 mg IV bolus, followed by an infusion (significantly higher than doses used for hypoglycemia).

Front: Target Blood Glucose in High-Dose Insulin Euglycemic Therapy (HIET)?

Back: 100–200 mg/dL (requires concurrent concentrated Dextrose infusion).

Front: Primary side effect of IV Methylene Blue?

Back: Serotonin Syndrome (it is a potent MAO-inhibitor).

Front: First-line treatment for Isoniazid (INH) induced status epilepticus?

Back: Pyridoxine (Vitamin B6) given gram-for-gram to the amount of INH ingested.

Section 31: Complex Industrial & Environmental Toxins

Front: Mechanism of Hydrofluoric Acid (HF) toxicity?

Back: Liquefactive necrosis and profound systemic Hypocalcemia/Hypomagnesemia due to Fluoride ion binding.

Front: Treatment for "Phosgene" gas inhalation?

Back: Supportive care (O2/Rest); there is no specific antidote.

Front: Classic symptom of "Aconitine" (Monkshood) poisoning?

Back: Perioral numbness and fatal ventricular arrhythmias ("The Queen of Poisons").

Front: Mechanism of "Strychnine" poisoning?

Back: Antagonizes Glycine receptors in the spinal cord, leading to "risus sardonicus" and tetanic convulsions.

Front: Clinical triad of "Ergotism"?

Back: 1. Severe burning pain (St. Anthony's Fire), 2. Muscle spasms, 3. Gangrene.

Section 32: Drugs of Abuse & Specialized Withdrawal

Front: "Meth Mouth" pathophysiology?

Back: Combination of drug-induced xerostomia (dry mouth), poor hygiene, and teeth grinding (bruxism).

Front: Primary cause of death in "Bath Salts" (Synthetic Cathinones) intoxication?

Back: Excited Delirium leading to Hyperthermia and Rhabdomyolysis.

Front: "Krokodil" (Desomorphine) characteristic clinical feature?

Back: Severe skin necrosis and gangrene at injection sites (due to toxic byproducts like Phosphorus).

Front: Management of Gamma-Hydroxybutyrate (GHB) overdose?

Back: Supportive care; patients often "wake up" suddenly with extreme agitation (the "G-sleep").

Section 33: Pediatric & OB/GYN Toxicology High-Yields

Front: "Fetal Hydantoin Syndrome" cause?

Back: Maternal use of Phenytoin during pregnancy.

Front: Classic presentation of "Fetal Hydantoin Syndrome"?

Back: Microcephaly, cleft lip/palate, and hypoplastic nails/phalanges.

Front: Management of a pregnant patient with CO poisoning?

Back: Lower threshold for Hyperbaric Oxygen ($HBO_2$) because Fetal Hemoglobin has a higher affinity for CO than maternal Hb.

Front: Why is Ceftriaxone avoided in neonates?

Back: It displaces Bilirubin from albumin, increasing the risk of Kernicterus.

Section 34: The "Next Best Step" (Management Logic)

Front: Next best step for a patient with a "Scooped ST" and heart block?

Back: Check Potassium level and order a Digoxin level.

Front: Next best step for an unconscious patient with "Pinpoint Pupils"?

Back: Trial of Naloxone and check bedside Glucose.

Front: Next best step for a patient with QRS > 120ms after an unknown ingestion?

Back: Administer Sodium Bicarbonate ($NaHCO_3$).

Front: Next best step in a symptomatic "Body Packer"?

Back: Emergency surgical consultation (not WBI or Charcoal).

Section 35: Visual & Physical Exam Pearls

Front: "Blue Line" on the gingival margin (Burton's Line)?

Back: Lead poisoning.

Front: "Acrodynia" (Painful, pink/red hands and feet)?

Back: Mercury poisoning (common in children).

Front: Horizontal AND Vertical Nystagmus?

Back: Highly suggestive of Phencyclidine (PCP) intoxication.

Front: "Cherry Red" skin color (Post-mortem)?

Back: Carbon Monoxide or Cyanide poisoning.


Front: Antidote for Valproic Acid?
Back: L-Carnitine.

Front: Antidote for Sulfonylureas?
Back: Octreotide.

Front: Antidote for Black Widow bite?
Back: Latrodectus antivenom (Equine-derived).

Front: Antidote for Calcium Channel Blockers?
Back: Calcium, Glucagon, High-dose Insulin, Lipid Emulsion.

Front: Why avoid Beta-blockers in Cocaine chest pain?

Back: Risk of unopposed alpha-stimulation leading to severe hypertension and coronary artery vasospasm.

Front: Why avoid Ipecac in a patient who swallowed a caustic agent?

Back: It causes "double-damage" by re-exposing the esophagus to the caustic substance during emesis.

Front: Contraindication for Flumazenil in an unknown overdose?

Back: Potential for intractable seizures if the patient has co-ingested a pro-convulsant (like TCAs).

Front: Why is Acidification of urine (using Ammonium Chloride) no longer recommended?

Back: It can worsen systemic acidosis and increase the risk of rhabdomyolysis-induced renal failure.

Front: Why is Magnesium-containing cathartics avoided in renal failure patients?

Back: Risk of severe hypermagnesemia and neuromuscular blockade.

Section 39: The "Toxicology Differential" (Symptoms to Toxin)

Front: Differential for "Hyperthermia and Rigidity"?

Back: Serotonin Syndrome, Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS), Malignant Hyperthermia (anesthesia), or Salicylate toxicity.

Front: Differential for "Miosis" (Pinpoint Pupils)?

Back: Opioids, Organophosphates (Cholinergics), Clonidine, and Sedative-hypnotics (sometimes).

Front: Differential for "Mydriasis" (Dilated Pupils)?

Back: Sympathomimetics (Cocaine/Amphetamines), Anticholinergics, and Opioid withdrawal.

Front: Toxin causing "Vertical Nystagmus" specifically?

Back: Phencyclidine (PCP) or Wernicke’s Encephalopathy.

Section 40: Specific Pharmacology & Receptor Profiles

Front: Mechanism of Digoxin at the cellular level?

Back: Inhibits the $Na^+/K^+$-ATPase pump, leading to increased intracellular $Ca^{2+}$.

Front: Mechanism of Clonidine toxicity?

Back: Alpha-2 adrenergic agonist; causes a "Central Sympatholytic" effect (mimics opioid overdose).

Front: Mechanism of Batrachotoxin (from Poison Dart Frogs)?

Back: Irreversibly opens Sodium channels (leads to paralysis and arrhythmias).

Front: Mechanism of Botulinum Toxin?

Back: Cleaves SNARE proteins, preventing the release of Acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction.

Section 41: Forensic & Legal Toxicology

Front: Definition of "Chain of Custody" in a toxicology screen?

Back: The documented, chronological history of the seizure, custody, and analysis of a sample to ensure it hasn't been tampered with.

Front: Most common "False Positive" on a Urine Drug Screen (UDS) for Amphetamines?

Back: Pseudoephedrine, Bupropion, or Selegiline.

Front: Most common "False Positive" for Opioids on UDS?

Back: Poppy seeds or Rifampin.

Front: Why does a standard UDS often miss Fentanyl or Oxycodone?

Back: Standard screens usually only detect natural opiates (morphine/codeine); synthetic opioids require specific assays.

Section 42: "Last Call" High-Yield Pearls

Front: "Boiled Lobster" appearance of the skin?

Back: Boric Acid poisoning.

Front: Management of "Button Battery" in the Esophagus vs. Stomach?

Back: Esophagus: Emergency endoscopic removal. Stomach: Observation (repeat X-ray) if the patient is asymptomatic.

Front: Mechanism of "Metformin-Associated Lactic Acidosis" (MALA)?

Back: Inhibits mitochondrial respiration, leading to a shift toward anaerobic metabolism.

Front: Preferred treatment for QRS widening in an Arsenic overdose?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate (same principle as TCA overdose).

Front: Toxin found in "Apricot Pits" or "Laetrile"?

Back: Amygdalin (metabolized to Cyanide).

Front: Hallmark of Ciguatera poisoning (Fish)?

Back: Reversal of hot/cold temperature sensation.


=====================================================================
=====================================================================



GI Pharmacology:


1. Front: Mechanism of action (MOA) of H2-receptor antagonists (e.g., Cimetidine, Famotidine)?

Back: Reversibly block H2 receptors $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ H+ secretion by parietal cells.

2. Front: MOA of Proton Pump Inhibitors (PPIs) (e.g., Omeprazole, Lansoprazole)?

Back: Irreversibly inhibit H+/K+ ATPase in stomach parietal cells.

3. Front: Specific side effects of Cimetidine?

Back: Cytochrome P-450 inhibitor; antiandrogenic effects (gynecomastia, impotence); crosses blood-brain barrier (confusion).

4. Front: Which PPI/H2RA is known to decrease the renal excretion of creatinine?

Back: Cimetidine.

5. Front: MOA of Misoprostol in GI protection?

Back: PGE1 analog; $\uparrow$ production of gastric mucous barrier and $\downarrow$ acid secretion.

6. Front: Primary clinical use of Misoprostol in GI?

Back: Prevention of NSAID-induced gastric ulcers.

7. Front: Contraindication for Misoprostol?

Back: Pregnancy (it is an abortifacient/causes uterine contractions).

8. Front: MOA of Sucralfate?

Back: Binds to ulcers/erosions, providing physical protection (requires acidic environment to polymerize).

9. Front: MOA of Bismuth subsalte (Pepto-Bismol)?

Back: Coats ulcers, stimulates mucosal protective factors; has antimicrobial effects against H. pylori.

10. Front: Common harmless side effect of Bismuth?

Back: Black tongue and black stools (may mimic melena).

11. Front: Adverse effect associated with Aluminum hydroxide antacids?

Back: Constipation ("Alumin-um" = "Minimum" stools).

12. Front: Adverse effect associated with Magnesium hydroxide antacids?

Back: Diarrhea ("Magnesi-um" = "Must go" to the bathroom).

13. Front: Which antacid is associated with "Milk-alkali syndrome"?

Back: Calcium carbonate (hypercalcemia, metabolic alkalosis, renal insufficiency).

14. Front: Standard "Triple Therapy" for H. pylori?

Back: PPI + Amoxicillin (or Metronidazole) + Clarithromycin.

15. Front: Standard "Quadruple Therapy" for H. pylori?

Back: PPI + Bismuth + Metronidazole + Tetracycline.

Section 2: Antiemetics

16. Front: MOA of Ondansetron (Zofran)?

Back: 5-HT3 antagonist; vagal afferents and central (Chemoreceptor Trigger Zone - CTZ).

17. Front: Major ECG side effect of Ondansetron?

Back: QT interval prolongation (risk of Torsades de Pointes).

18. Front: MOA of Metoclopramide?

Back: D2 receptor antagonist in CTZ; also a prokinetic (stimulates 5-HT4 receptors).

19. Front: Neurological side effects of Metoclopramide?

Back: Extrapyramidal symptoms (dystonia, parkinsonism, tardive dyskinesia).

20. Front: Contraindications for Metoclopramide?

Back: Small bowel obstruction, Parkinson's disease.

21. Front: MOA of Aprepitant?

Back: Substance P antagonist; blocks NK1 (neurokinin-1) receptors in the brain.

22. Front: Which class of antiemetic is best for chemotherapy-induced vomiting?

Back: 5-HT3 antagonists (Ondansetron) and NK1 antagonists (Aprepitant).

23. Front: MOA of Scopolamine for motion sickness?

Back: Antimuscarinic (M1 antagonist).

24. Front: MOA of Promethazine/Prochlorperazine?

Back: D2 and H1 antagonists (used for severe N/V).

Section 3: Laxatives & Antidiarrheals

25. Front: MOA of Psyllium and Methylcellulose?

Back: Bulk-forming laxatives; absorb water to soften stool and increase bulk.

26. Front: MOA of Magnesium citrate and Polyethylene glycol (PEG)?

Back: Osmotic laxatives; draw water into the bowel lumen.

27. Front: MOA of Senna and Bisacodyl?

Back: Stimulant laxatives; enteric nerve stimulation $\rightarrow$ colonic contraction.

28. Front: MOA of Docusate?

Back: Stool softener; acts as a surfactant to allow water and lipids into stool.

29. Front: MOA of Loperamide?

Back: $\mu$-opioid receptor agonist; slows gut motility (poor CNS penetration, low addiction potential).

30. Front: MOA of Diphenoxylate?

Back: $\mu$-opioid receptor agonist; often combined with Atropine to discourage abuse.

31. Front: MOA of Octreotide in GI?

Back: Somatostatin analog; inhibits secretion of splanchnic hormones (gastrin, CCK, VIP, insulin).

32. Front: Primary clinical use of Octreotide in GI emergency?

Back: Acute variceal bleeds (decreases splanchnic blood flow).

Section 4: Inflammatory Bowel Disease (IBD)

33. Front: MOA of Sulfasalazine?

Back: Combination of Sulfapyridine (antibacterial) and 5-aminosalicylic acid (anti-inflammatory) activated by gut bacteria.

34. Front: Active component of Sulfasalazine that treats IBD?

Back: 5-aminosalicylic acid (5-ASA / Mesalamine).

35. Front: Common side effects of the Sulfapyridine component?

Back: Reversible oligospermia, malaise, sulfonamide toxicity.

36. Front: MOA of Infliximab and Adalimumab in IBD?

Back: Anti-TNF-$\alpha$ monoclonal antibodies.

37. Front: Necessary screening before starting Infliximab?

Back: Latent Tuberculosis (TB) screen (risk of reactivation).

38. Front: MOA of Vedolizumab?

Back: Integrin antagonist ($\alpha_4\beta_7$); gut-specific anti-inflammatory.

39. Front: MOA of Azathioprine/6-Mercaptopurine (6-MP)?

Back: Purine synthesis inhibitors; used for IBD maintenance.

40. Front: Dangerous drug interaction with Azathioprine?

Back: Allopurinol (inhibits Xanthine Oxidase $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ toxic 6-MP levels).

Section 5: Motility & Miscellaneous

41. Front: MOA of Erythromycin as a prokinetic?

Back: Stimulates motilin receptors.

42. Front: Clinical use for Erythromycin in GI?

Back: Gastroparesis (especially diabetic).

43. Front: MOA of Orlistat?

Back: Inhibits gastric and pancreatic lipase $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ dietary fat absorption.

44. Front: Primary side effect of Orlistat?

Back: Steatorrhea, decreased absorption of fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K).

45. Front: MOA of Ursodeoxycholic acid (Ursodiol)?

Back: Bile acid; reduces cholesterol secretion and $\uparrow$ bile flow (used to dissolve gallstones).

46. Front: Treatment for Wilson’s Disease?

Back: Penicillamine or Trientine (Copper chelators).

47. Front: Treatment for Hepatic Encephalopathy (to $\downarrow$ Ammonia)?

Back: Lactulose and Rifaximin.

48. Front: MOA of Lactulose in Hepatic Encephalopathy?

Back: Converted to lactic acid by bacteria $\rightarrow$ acidifies gut $\rightarrow$ converts $NH_3$ to $NH_4+$ (trapping).

49. Front: MOA of Pancrelipase?

Back: Replacement of lipase, amylase, and protease for pancreatic insufficiency (Cystic Fibrosis, chronic pancreatitis).

50. Front: MOA of Lubiprostone?

Back: Activates Type 2 chloride channels in the gut; used for chronic constipation/IBS-C.

Section 6: GI Physiology & Integration

51. Front: Effect of Gastrin on parietal cells?

Back: Stimulates H+ secretion via CCK-B receptors and ECL cells ($\uparrow$ histamine).

52. Front: Effect of Somatostatin on the GI tract?

Back: Inhibitory; $\downarrow$ gastric acid, pepsinogen, pancreatic/biliary secretions, and gallbladder contraction.

53. Front: MOA of Linaclotide?

Back: Guanylate cyclase-C agonist $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ cGMP $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ Cl- and $HCO_3-$ secretion into lumen.

54. Front: First-line drug for primary biliary cholangitis?

Back: Ursodeoxycholic acid (Ursodiol).

55. Front: MOA of Alosetron?

Back: 5-HT3 antagonist used specifically for severe IBS-D in women.

56. Front: Black box warning for Alosetron?

Back: Ischemic colitis.

57. Front: Drug used to prevent esophageal variceal bleeding (prophylaxis)?

Back: Non-selective beta-blockers (Propranolol, Nadolol).

58. Front: MOA of Sulfasalazine-induced anemia?

Back: Interferes with Folate absorption.

59. Front: Why use Metronidazole in Crohn’s disease?

Back: Treats perianal disease and abscesses.

60. Front: Drug used for localized distal Ulcerative Colitis?

Back: Mesalamine enemas or suppositories.

Section 7: Toxins & Specific Antidotes

61. Front: Antidote for Acetaminophen toxicity?

Back: N-acetylcysteine (replenishes glutathione).

62. Front: Treatment for Iron overdose (acute)?

Back: Deferoxamine.

63. Front: GI side effect of chronic Lead poisoning?

Back: Colicky abdominal pain ("Lead colic").

64. Front: Treatment for Arsenic poisoning?

Back: Dimercaprol.

65. Front: Effect of Opioids on the Sphincter of Oddi?

Back: Spasm/contraction (can worsen biliary colic; Meperidine is traditionally preferred).

Section 8: Expanded High-Yield (Sleisenger/G&G Additions)

66. Front: MOA of Teduglutide?

Back: GLP-2 analog; enhances intestinal villus growth in Short Bowel Syndrome.

67. Front: Use of Cholestyramine in GI?

Back: Bile acid sequestrant; used for bile acid-induced diarrhea (e.g., after ileal resection).

68. Front: MOA of Telotristat?

Back: Tryptophan hydroxylase inhibitor; $\downarrow$ serotonin production in Carcinoid Syndrome.

69. Front: MOA of Eluxadoline?

Back: $\mu$ and $\kappa$ opioid agonist and $\delta$ antagonist; used for IBS-D.

70. Front: Effect of Glucagon on the esophagus?

Back: Relaxes smooth muscle (lower esophageal sphincter); used to dislodge food boluses.

71. Front: MOA of Simethicone?

Back: Antifoaming agent; breaks up gas bubbles (anti-flatulent).

72. Front: MOA of Mineral oil?

Back: Lubricant laxative; softens stool and coats the intestine.

73. Front: Danger of Mineral oil aspiration?

Back: Lipid pneumonia.

74. Front: Drug that causes "Grey baby syndrome" but is used for GI infections abroad?

Back: Chloramphenicol.

75. Front: MOA of Fidaxomicin?

Back: Inhibits RNA polymerase; used specifically for C. difficile infection.

76. Front: First-line treatment for C. difficile (current guidelines)?

Back: Oral Vancomycin or Fidaxomicin.

77. Front: MOA of Obeticholic acid?

Back: FXR (Farnesoid X receptor) agonist; used for primary biliary cholangitis.

78. Front: Side effect of high-dose Calcium carbonate?

Back: Rebound acid hypersecretion.

79. Front: MOA of Prucalopride?

Back: Selective 5-HT4 receptor agonist; used for chronic idiopathic constipation.

80. Front: MOA of Methylnaltrexone?

Back: Peripheral $\mu$-opioid receptor antagonist; treats opioid-induced constipation without crossing BBB.

Section 9: Biliary and Pancreatic Meds

81. Front: Use of Secretin in GI testing?

Back: Stimulates pancreatic $HCO_3-$ secretion; used to diagnose Gastrinoma (Zollinger-Ellison).

82. Front: Response of a Gastrinoma to Secretin injection?

Back: Paradoxic increase in Serum Gastrin.

83. Front: MOA of Cholecystokinin (CCK)?

Back: Stimulates gallbladder contraction and pancreatic enzyme secretion.

84. Front: Therapeutic use of CCK octapeptide (Sincalide)?

Back: Used in HIDA scans to stimulate the gallbladder.

85. Front: MOA of Vasopressin in GI hemorrhage?

Back: Potent splanchnic vasoconstrictor (less preferred than Octreotide).

86. Front: Effect of Somatostatin on Glucagon and Insulin?

Back: Inhibits both.

87. Front: Common GI side effect of Methotrexate (used in IBD)?

Back: Mucositis and oral ulcers.

88. Front: GI side effect of Mycophenolate mofetil?

Back: Severe diarrhea.

89. Front: Interaction between PPIs and Clopidogrel?

Back: PPIs (especially Omeprazole) may inhibit CYP2C19 $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ activation of Clopidogrel.

90. Front: Drug used to treat Portal Hypertension in Cirrhosis?

Back: Propranolol (non-selective beta-blocker).
