91. Front: MOA of Rifaximin in Hepatic Encephalopathy?

Back: Poorly absorbed antibiotic; $\downarrow$ ammonia-producing bacteria in the gut.

92. Front: Antibiotic associated with Pseudomembranous colitis?

Back: Clindamycin (classic), but also Amoxicillin and Cephalosporins.

93. Front: Treatment for VIPoma?

Back: Octreotide.

94. Front: What vitamin should be supplemented in patients on Sulfasalazine?

Back: Folate (Vitamin B9).

95. Front: MOA of Naloxegol?

Back: Peripheral $\mu$-opioid receptor antagonist (PEGylated Naloxone) for opioid-induced constipation.

96. Front: Major GI side effect of NSAIDs?

Back: Inhibition of PGE2 $\rightarrow$ gastric ulcers and bleeding.

97. Front: GI side effect of chronic Alcohol use?

Back: Gastritis, Pancreatitis, and Cirrhosis.

98. Front: Which drug is used for bowel prep before colonoscopy?

Back: Polyethylene glycol (PEG).

99. Front: Side effect of PPIs regarding bone health?

Back: $\uparrow$ risk of fractures (due to $\downarrow$ Calcium absorption).

100. Front: Risk of PPIs regarding infections?

Back: $\uparrow$ risk of C. difficile and Pneumonia (due to loss of acid barrier).

Section 10: Clinical Scenarios & Rapid Fire

101. Front: 35yo with GERD, wants a drug that doesn't cause gynecomastia. Which H2RA?

Back: Famotidine (or Ranitidine, though mostly withdrawn).

102. Front: Patient with Gastroparesis needs a drug to help with gastric emptying.

Back: Metoclopramide or Erythromycin.

103. Front: Patient with severe Travelers' Diarrhea. First line anti-motility?

Back: Loperamide.

104. Front: Best drug for symptomatic relief of Esophageal Spasm?

Back: Nitrates or Calcium Channel Blockers.

105. Front: Medication that causes "painless" black tongue?

Back: Bismuth subsalicylate.

106. Front: MOA of Linaclotide vs Lubiprostone?

Back: Linaclotide (Guanylate cyclase-C); Lubiprostone (Chloride channel activator).

107. Front: Which antibiotic is contraindicated in pregnancy due to "discolored teeth" but used in Quadruple H. pylori therapy?

Back: Tetracycline.

108. Front: MOA of Tegaserod?

Back: 5-HT4 partial agonist (used for IBS-C; restricted use due to CV risks).

109. Front: Most effective long-term therapy for Barrett's Esophagus symptoms?

Back: PPIs.

110. Front: MOA of Entyvio (Vedolizumab)?

Back: $\alpha_4\beta_7$ Integrin blocker.

111. Front: MOA of Propranolol/Nadolol in preventing variceal hemorrhage?

Back: $\beta_2$ blockade $\rightarrow$ splanchnic vasoconstriction (unopposed $\alpha$) $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ portal venous flow.

112. Front: Drug used to treat Hepatorenal Syndrome (HRS) by causing systemic vasoconstriction?

Back: Terlipressin (Vasopressin analog) + Albumin.

113. Front: MOA of Rifaximin in preventing Hepatic Encephalopathy recurrence?

Back: Non-absorbable antibiotic that kills ammonia-producing enteric bacteria.

114. Front: Why is Neomycin rarely used now for Hepatic Encephalopathy despite being effective?

Back: Risk of ototoxicity and nephrotoxicity.

115. Front: MOA of Nitazoxanide?

Back: Interferes with PFOR enzyme (anaerobic metabolism); used for Cryptosporidium in immunocompetent patients.

116. Front: MOA of Ribavirin (used in Hep C)?

Back: Lethal hypermutation, inhibition of RNA polymerase, and inhibition of IMPDH.

117. Front: Significant side effect of Ribavirin?

Back: Hemolytic anemia and severe teratogenicity.

118. Front: MOA of Sofosbuvir?

Back: NS5B RNA-dependent RNA polymerase inhibitor (HCV).

119. Front: MOA of Simeprevir?

Back: NS3/4A protease inhibitor (HCV).

120. Front: MOA of Ledipasvir?

Back: NS5A inhibitor (HCV).

Section 12: Advanced IBD & Immunology

121. Front: MOA of Ustekinumab (Stelara) in Crohn's?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against IL-12 and IL-23.

122. Front: MOA of Tofacitinib in Ulcerative Colitis?

Back: JAK inhibitor (Janus Kinase).

123. Front: Which IBD drug is associated with a risk of Progressive Multifocal Leukoencephalopathy (PML)?

Back: Natalizumab (blocks $\alpha_4$-integrin; crosses BBB).

124. Front: How does Vedolizumab differ from Natalizumab in safety?

Back: Vedolizumab is gut-specific ($\alpha_4\beta_7$); it does not cross the BBB or cause PML.

125. Front: MOA of Cyclosporine in "rescue therapy" for Ulcerative Colitis?

Back: Calcineurin inhibitor; inhibits IL-2 transcription.

126. Front: Major side effects of Cyclosporine?

Back: Nephrotoxicity, Gingival hyperplasia, Hirsutism ("the three H's").

Section 13: Specialized GI & Metabolism

127. Front: MOA of Miglustat in Gaucher Disease (GI-related metabolic)?

Back: Glucosylceramide synthase inhibitor ($\downarrow$ substrate formation).

128. Front: MOA of Sevelamer?

Back: Non-absorbable phosphate binder; used in GI tract for CKD patients.

129. Front: MOA of Lanthanum carbonate?

Back: Phosphate binder (used for hyperphosphatemia).

130. Front: MOA of Crofelemer?

Back: Chloride channel inhibitor; used for HIV-associated non-infectious diarrhea.

131. Front: Drug used to treat refractory Celiac disease?

Back: Azathioprine or Budesonide (locally acting steroid).

132. Front: MOA of Budesonide’s "local" effect in IBD?

Back: High first-pass metabolism in the liver limits systemic exposure.

133. Front: Drug used for the "Urea Breath Test"?

Back: $^{13}C$-labeled Urea.

Section 14: Clinical Toxicity & Antidotes

134. Front: Treatment for Methanol or Ethylene Glycol ingestion (GI/metabolic)?

Back: Fomepizole (inhibits alcohol dehydrogenase).

135. Front: Treatment for Amanita phalloides (mushroom) poisoning?

Back: Silibinin (Silybin) or Penicillin G.

136. Front: GI side effect of Colchicine?

Back: Severe diarrhea (disrupts rapidly dividing GI mucosal cells).

137. Front: Drug causing "Crystalline nephropathy" if not hydrated during GI infection treatment?

Back: Sulfadiazine or Acyclovir.

138. Front: Mechanism of "Red Man Syndrome" with Vancomycin?

Back: Direct Histamine release from mast cells (not a true allergy).

139. Front: Classic GI side effect of Metronidazole?

Back: Disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol (flushing, tachycardia).

Section 15: Rare & High-Level (Sleisenger Focus)

140. Front: MOA of Pasireotide?

Back: Somatostatin analog with high affinity for SSTR5; used in Cushing’s or specialized neuroendocrine GI tumors.

141. Front: MOA of Resins (e.g., Cholestyramine) in Pruritus?

Back: Binds bile acids in the gut, preventing their reabsorption and deposition in skin (common in PBC).

142. Front: MOA of Chenodeoxycholic acid?

Back: Bile acid that suppresses hepatic synthesis of cholesterol (stone dissolution).

143. Front: MOA of Nitroglycerin paste in GI?

Back: Relaxes the internal anal sphincter (used for anal fissures).

144. Front: MOA of Dicyclomine/Hyoscyamine?

Back: Antispasmodics (Anticholinergics) used for IBS-related cramping.

145. Front: Why avoid Loperamide in patients with C. diff or high fever?

Back: Risk of inducing Toxic Megacolon.

146. Front: Standard drug treatment for Whipple Disease?

Back: Ceftriaxone (initially) followed by Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) for 1 year.

147. Front: MOA of Ezetimibe in the GI tract?

Back: Inhibits NPC1L1 transporter $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ cholesterol absorption at the brush border.

148. Front: MOA of Sacrosidase?

Back: Enzyme replacement for Congenital Sucrase-Isomaltase Deficiency (CSID).

149. Front: First-line treatment for Achalasia (pharmacologic)?

Back: Nifedipine (CCB) or Nitrospray (rarely used; usually surgery/botox).

150. Front: MOA of Botulinum toxin in Achalasia?

Back: Inhibits ACh release at the Lower Esophageal Sphincter $\rightarrow$ relaxation.

Section 16: GI Hormone Pharmacology

151. Front: MOA of Exenatide/Liraglutide (GI-hormone based)?

Back: GLP-1 agonists; $\downarrow$ gastric emptying and $\uparrow$ insulin.

152. Front: GI side effect of GLP-1 agonists?

Back: Nausea, vomiting, and potential risk of Pancreatitis.

153. Front: MOA of Sitagliptin/Linagliptin?

Back: DPP-4 inhibitors; prevent the breakdown of endogenous GLP-1.

154. Front: Drug used to treat Gastrinoma when surgery is not possible?

Back: High-dose PPIs.

155. Front: Which drug inhibits the absorption of Fat-Soluble vitamins?

Back: Orlistat and Bile acid sequestrants (Cholestyramine).


156. Front: MOA of Obeticholic acid in Primary Biliary Cholangitis?

Back: FXR agonist $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ bile acid synthesis and $\uparrow$ bile acid transport out of hepatocytes.

157. Front: Most common side effect of Obeticholic acid?

Back: Severe pruritus (itching).

158. Front: MOA of Pancrelipase administration?

Back: Supplies lipase, protease, and amylase; must be taken with meals to digest macronutrients.

159. Front: Why are PPIs often co-administered with Pancrelipase?

Back: To prevent gastric acid from denaturing the supplemental enzymes before they reach the duodenum.

160. Front: Drug of choice for treating Biliary Colic (acute pain)?

Back: NSAIDs (e.g., Ketorolac) are often superior to opioids for biliary pain.

Section 18: Microbiota & Antibiotic-Related GI

161. Front: MOA of Rifaximin in Travelers' Diarrhea?

Back: Inhibits bacterial RNA synthesis (non-absorbable, active against E. coli).

162. Front: MOA of Nitazoxanide in Giardia or Cryptosporidium?

Back: Interferes with the PFOR (pyruvate:ferredoxin oxidoreductase) enzyme-dependent electron transfer reaction.

163. Front: Treatment for Whipple’s Disease (long-term)?

Back: Ceftriaxone (2 weeks) followed by TMP-SMX (1 year).

164. Front: MOA of Bezlotoxumab?

Back: Human monoclonal antibody that binds C. difficile toxin B.

165. Front: Clinical use of Bezlotoxumab?

Back: Prevention of C. difficile recurrence in high-risk patients.

Section 19: Targeted Therapeutics & Rare Conditions

166. Front: MOA of Somatostatin analogs in dumping syndrome?

Back: Slows gastric emptying and inhibits the release of insulin and GI hormones.

167. Front: MOA of Glycerol phenylbutyrate in Urea Cycle Disorders (GI/Metabolic)?

Back: Nitrogen scavenger; helps eliminate ammonia via non-urea pathways.

168. Front: MOA of Chenodiol?

Back: Naturally occurring bile acid that $\downarrow$ biliary cholesterol secretion (used for radiolucent gallstones).

169. Front: First-line pharmacotherapy for Hemochromatosis (if phlebotomy is not an option)?

Back: Deferoxamine (IV) or Deferasirox (Oral) – Iron chelators.

170. Front: MOA of Miglustat?

Back: Inhibits glucosylceramide synthase; used for Type 1 Gaucher disease.

Section 20: GI Chemotherapy & Radiation Support

171. Front: MOA of Palonosetron?

Back: Second-generation 5-HT3 receptor antagonist with a longer half-life (40 hours).

172. Front: MOA of Dexamethasone in emesis?

Back: Glucocorticoid; used as an adjunct to 5-HT3 antagonists to $\uparrow$ antiemetic efficacy.

173. Front: MOA of Amifostine in GI radiation?

Back: Cytoprotective agent; scavenges free radicals to reduce xerostomia (dry mouth) and proctitis.

174. Front: Treatment for Radiation Proctitis (topical)?

Back: Sucralfate enemas or Sucralfate paste.

175. Front: MOA of Cannabinoids (Dronabinol) in GI?

Back: Agonist at CB1 receptors in the vomiting center; also stimulates appetite.

Section 21: Motility & Functional Disorders

176. Front: MOA of Plecanatide?

Back: Guanylate cyclase-C (GC-C) agonist (similar to Linaclotide) for Chronic Idiopathic Constipation.

177. Front: MOA of Tenapanor?

Back: NHE3 (Sodium-Hydrogen Exchanger 3) inhibitor; $\downarrow$ sodium absorption in the gut $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ luminal water.

178. Front: MOA of Alvimopan?

Back: Peripherally acting $\mu$-opioid receptor antagonist; used specifically for post-operative ileus.

179. Front: MOA of Pyridostigmine in Ogilvie Syndrome (Acute Colonic Pseudo-obstruction)?

Back: Acetylcholinesterase inhibitor; $\uparrow$ parasympathetic activity to stimulate colonic contraction.

180. Front: Primary side effect of Pyridostigmine to watch for in GI patients?

Back: Excessive salivation and Bradycardia (cholinergic crisis).

Section 22: Advanced Pharmacology Integration

181. Front: Why is Metoclopramide avoided in bowel obstruction?

Back: Prokinetic effect against a physical obstruction can lead to perforation.

182. Front: Which GI drug can cause "Cinchonism" (tinnitus, headache, dizziness)?

Back: Quinidine (occasionally used for GI-related protozoal infections, though rare now).

183. Front: MOA of Sulfasalazine in decreasing inflammation?

Back: Inhibits prostaglandin synthesis and leukotriene synthesis (via the 5-ASA moiety).

184. Front: Which H2RA has the most drug-drug interactions?

Back: Cimetidine (potent P450 inhibitor).

185. Front: MOA of Octreotide in Chylothorax/Chylous ascites?

Back: $\downarrow$ intestinal fat absorption and $\downarrow$ triglyceride content in lymph.


186. Front: A patient on long-term PPIs develops a fracture. What is the biochemical reason?

Back: $\downarrow$ Gastric acidity $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ solubility of Calcium Carbonate $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ intestinal Calcium absorption.

187. Front: Why is Magnesium Hydroxide usually combined with Aluminum Hydroxide in commercial antacids?

Back: To offset their side effects (Magnesium causes diarrhea; Aluminum causes constipation).

188. Front: Which antiemetic class is best for "Delayed" chemotherapy-induced nausea (2–5 days post-chemo)?

Back: NK1 receptor antagonists (e.g., Aprepitant).

189. Front: Which drug is used for "rescue" in severe, steroid-refractory Ulcerative Colitis?

Back: Infliximab or Cyclosporine.

190. Front: MOA of Bismuth in the "Quadruple Therapy" for H. pylori?

Back: Direct antimicrobial effect + prevents bacterial adhesion to gastric epithelium.

Section 24: Physiology-Pharmacology Integration

191. Front: Effect of Somatostatin on Splanchnic blood flow?

Back: Potent vasoconstriction (via inhibition of vasodilator hormones like Glucagon).

192. Front: Which drug blocks the enzyme that converts Testosterone to DHT, but is also a P450 inhibitor used in GI?

Back: Cimetidine (though it primarily blocks the androgen receptor, it has complex endocrine side effects).

193. Front: MOA of Misoprostol in preventing ulcers vs. its use in obstetrics?

Back: Both involve $EP_3$ receptor agonism; GI $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ cAMP in parietal cells; OB $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ Calcium in uterine smooth muscle.

194. Front: Why does Loperamide not cause CNS opioid effects (euphoria/analgesia)?

Back: It is a substrate for the P-glycoprotein efflux pump, which pumps it out of the brain.

195. Front: MOA of Senna-induced "Melanosis Coli"?

Back: Anthraquinone-induced apoptosis of colonic epithelial cells $\rightarrow$ pigment-laden macrophages in the lamina propria.

Section 25: Rare Toxins & Specific GI Management

196. Front: Drug for pruritus in a patient with Primary Biliary Cholangitis?

Back: Cholestyramine (binds bile acids) or Rifampin (induces enzymes to metabolize pruritogens).

197. Front: Treatment for a patient with Wilson's Disease who cannot tolerate Penicillamine?

Back: Trientine (another chelator) or Zinc (blocks intestinal Copper absorption via Metallothionein).

198. Front: Which drug used for IBD requires testing for Thiopurine Methyltransferase (TPMT) activity?

Back: Azathioprine / 6-Mercaptopurine (Low TPMT $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ risk of life-threatening myelosuppression).

199. Front: MOA of Methotrexate in Crohn’s Disease?

Back: Inhibits Dihydrofolate Reductase (DHFR) $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ DNA synthesis; also $\uparrow$ Adenosine (anti-inflammatory).

200. Front: MOA of Natalizumab's GI benefit?

Back: Blocks $\alpha_4$-integrin $\rightarrow$ prevents leukocyte adhesion and migration into gut tissue.


=====================================================================
=====================================================================

General Pathology:

Front: Definition of Hypertrophy?

Back: Increase in the size of cells resulting in an increase in the size of the organ; no new cells.

Front: Definition of Hyperplasia?

Back: Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus.

Front: What is Metaplasia?

Back: A reversible change in which one adult cell type is replaced by another adult cell type (e.g., Barrett esophagus).

Front: Hallmarks of irreversible cell injury?

Back: Profound mitochondrial dysfunction and severe disturbances in membrane function (plasma and lysosomal).

Front: Features of Pyknosis?

Back: Nuclear shrinkage and increased basophilia.

Front: Features of Karyorrhexis?

Back: Fragmentation of the pyknotic nucleus.

Front: Features of Karyolysis?

Back: Fading of nuclear basophilia due to DNA degradation by endonucleases.

Front: Mechanism of Coagulative Necrosis?

Back: Denaturation of structural proteins and enzymes, preserving the architecture of dead tissue for days (common in infarcts, except brain).

Front: Main characteristic of Liquefactive Necrosis?

Back: Digestion of dead cells into a liquid viscous mass; seen in focal bacterial/fungal infections and CNS hypoxic death.

Front: Definition of Caseous Necrosis?

Back: "Cheese-like" friable white appearance; characteristic of a focus of inflammation known as a granuloma (e.g., Tuberculosis).

Front: What is Fat Necrosis?

Back: Focal areas of fat destruction typically resulting from the release of activated pancreatic lipases (saponification).

Front: Fundamental mechanism of Fibrinoid Necrosis?

Back: Complexes of antigens and antibodies deposited in the walls of arteries, appearing bright pink and amorphous on H&E.

Front: Pathological trigger for the Intrinsic (Mitochondrial) Pathway of Apoptosis?

Back: Increased permeability of the mitochondrial outer membrane leading to the release of cytochrome c into the cytosol.

Front: Executioner Caspases in Apoptosis?

Back: Caspase-3 and Caspase-6.

Front: Definition of Dystrophic Calcification?

Back: Deposition of calcium salts in dead or dying tissues; occurs despite normal serum calcium levels.

Front: Definition of Metastatic Calcification?

Back: Deposition of calcium salts in normal tissues due to hypercalcemia (e.g., hyperparathyroidism).

Section 2: Inflammation and Repair

Front: Five cardinal signs of Acute Inflammation?

Back: Rubor (redness), Calor (heat), Tumor (swelling), Dolor (pain), and Functio laesa (loss of function).

Front: Primary mediator of increased vascular permeability in immediate transient responses?

Back: Histamine (acting on postcapillary venules).

Front: Sequence of Leukocyte Extravasation?

Back: 1. Margination/Rolling, 2. Adhesion, 3. Transmigration (Diapedesis), 4. Chemotaxis.

Front: Molecules responsible for Leukocyte Rolling?

Back: Selectins (E-selectin, P-selectin, L-selectin).

Front: Molecules responsible for Firm Adhesion of leukocytes?

Back: Integrins (e.g., VLA-4, LFA-1) binding to ICAM-1 and VCAM-1.

Front: Major Opsonins in phagocytosis?

Back: IgG, C3b, and Mannose-binding lectin.

Front: Key enzyme in the Respiratory Burst?

Back: NADPH Oxidase (converts $O_2$ to Superoxide).

Front: Genetic defect in Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD)?

Back: Deficiency in NADPH Oxidase.

Front: Principal mediator of Pain in acute inflammation?

Back: Bradykinin and Prostaglandins ($PGE_2$).

Front: Definition of a Granuloma?

Back: A collection of activated macrophages (epithelioid cells) rimmed by lymphocytes; a form of chronic inflammation.

Front: Cytokine primarily responsible for attracting and activating neutrophils?

Back: IL-8.

Front: Cytokines responsible for the Systemic Acute-Phase Response (Fever)?

Back: TNF, IL-1, and IL-6.

Front: Function of Metalloproteinases (MMPs) in tissue repair?

Back: Degrade collagen and other ECM components to allow for remodeling of the scar.

Front: Components of Granulation Tissue?

Back: Fibroblasts, delicate capillaries (angiogenesis), and scattered inflammatory cells.

Front: Difference between Primary and Secondary Union (Intention)?

Back: Primary: Clean surgical incision; Secondary: Large tissue defect requiring extensive contraction and scarring.

Section 3: Hemodynamic Disorders, Thromboembolism, and Shock

Front: Definition of Anasarca?

Back: Severe and generalized edema with widespread subcutaneous tissue swelling.

Front: Virchow’s Triad for Thrombosis?

Back: 1. Endothelial injury, 2. Stasis or turbulent blood flow, 3. Hypercoagulability.

Front: Define "Lines of Zahn"?

Back: Laminations in a thrombus representing alternating layers of platelets/fibrin (pale) and RBCs (dark); signifies formation in flowing blood.

Front: Most common source of Pulmonary Embolism?

Back: Deep Vein Thrombosis (DVT) in the leg (above the knee).

Front: Definition of a "Paradoxical Embolus"?

Back: A systemic embolus that originates in the venous circulation but enters the arterial system through a septal defect (e.g., PFO).

Front: Common clinical triad of Fat Embolism Syndrome?

Back: Hypoxemia, Neurologic symptoms, and Petechial rash (24-72 hours post-fracture).

Front: Definition of Red (Hemorrhagic) Infarct?

Back: Occurs in venous occlusions, loose tissues (lung), or tissues with dual circulation (liver/small intestine).

Front: Definition of White (Anemic) Infarct?

Back: Occurs in arterial occlusions in solid organs with end-arterial circulation (heart, spleen, kidney).

Front: The three stages of Shock?

Back: 1. Nonprogressive (compensated), 2. Progressive (hypoperfusion/metabolic imbalance), 3. Irreversible.

Section 4: Genetic Disorders

Front: Mode of inheritance for Marfan Syndrome?

Back: Autosomal Dominant (Mutation in FBN1 gene, encoding Fibrillin-1).

Front: Biochemical defect in Tay-Sachs Disease?

Back: Hexosaminidase A deficiency (accumulation of GM2 ganglioside).

Front: Characteristic physical finding in Tay-Sachs and Niemann-Pick?

Back: Cherry-red spot on the macula.

Front: Mode of inheritance for Cystic Fibrosis?

Back: Autosomal Recessive (CFTR gene mutation).

Front: Karyotype for Klinefelter Syndrome?

Back: 47,XXY.

Front: Karyotype for Turner Syndrome?

Back: 45,X.

Section 5: Diseases of the Immune System

Front: Definition of Type I Hypersensitivity?

Back: Immediate (IgE-mediated); Mast cell degranulation (e.g., Anaphylaxis).

Front: Definition of Type II Hypersensitivity?

Back: Antibody-mediated; IgG/IgM bind to fixed antigens on cells (e.g., Goodpasture syndrome).

Front: Definition of Type III Hypersensitivity?

Back: Immune complex-mediated; Deposition of antigen-antibody complexes (e.g., SLE, Arthus reaction).

Front: Definition of Type IV Hypersensitivity?

Back: Cell-mediated; T-lymphocyte driven (e.g., Contact dermatitis, TB skin test).

Front: Pathognomonic cell for Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Reed-Sternberg cell ("Owl-eye" appearance).

Front: Diagnostic criteria for SLE (Serology)?

Back: Positive ANA (sensitive) and Anti-dsDNA or Anti-Smith (specific).

Front: Primary defect in DiGeorge Syndrome?

Back: 22q11.2 deletion; failure of 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouch development (Thymic hypoplasia).

Front: Deficiency in X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (Bruton’s)?

Back: Mutation in Bruton Tyrosine Kinase (BTK); failure of B-cell maturation.

Front: Defining feature of Amyloidosis?

Back: Extracellular deposition of misfolded proteins that stain with Congo Red and show apple-green birefringence under polarized light.

Section 6: Neoplasia

Front: Definition of Anaplasia?

Back: Lack of differentiation; a hallmark of malignancy.

Front: Difference between Benign and Malignant tumors regarding borders?

Back: Benign: Circumscribed/Encapsulated; Malignant: Poorly defined/Invasive.

Front: Definition of Choristoma?

Back: Ectopic rest of normal tissue (e.g., pancreatic tissue in the stomach wall).

Front: Definition of Hamartoma?

Back: Disorganized but benign mass of cells indigenous to the particular site.

Front: TNM Staging System components?

Back: T: Size/Extent of primary tumor; N: Involvement of regional lymph nodes; M: Distant metastasis.

Front: Most important factor in tumor prognosis: Grade or Stage?

Back: Stage.

Front: Primary route of Carcinoma spread?

Back: Lymphatics.

Front: Primary route of Sarcoma spread?

Back: Hematogenous.

Front: The "Two-Hit" Hypothesis refers to which type of genes?

Back: Tumor Suppressor Genes (e.g., RB gene).

Front: Function of the p53 gene?

Back: "Guardian of the Genome"; senses DNA damage and induces cell cycle arrest or apoptosis.

Front: Function of BCL2 in follicular lymphoma?

Back: Anti-apoptotic protein; overexpressed due to t(14;18) translocation.

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome associated with Squamous Cell Lung Carcinoma?

Back: Hypercalcemia (due to PTHrP).

Front: Common tumor marker for Colon Cancer?

Back: CEA (Carcinoembryonic Antigen) - used for monitoring, not screening.

Front: Common tumor marker for Ovarian Cancer?

Back: CA-125.

Section 7: Environmental and Nutritional Pathology

Front: Leading cause of preventable death in the US?

Back: Smoking.

Front: Primary organ affected by Aflatoxin $B_1$ exposure?

Back: Liver (leads to Hepatocellular Carcinoma).

Front: Difference between Marasmus and Kwashiorkor?

Back: Marasmus: Total calorie deficiency (wasting); Kwashiorkor: Protein deficiency (edema, "flaky paint" skin).

Front: Vitamin C deficiency symptoms (Scurvy)?

Back: Bone pain, hemorrhages, and "corkscrew" hair due to impaired collagen synthesis (hydroxylation).

Front: Vitamin D deficiency in children vs. adults?

Back: Children: Rickets; Adults: Osteomalacia.

Section 8: Miscellaneous General Pathology

Front: Characteristics of "Labile" tissues?

Back: Continuously dividing (e.g., surface epithelia, hematopoietic cells).

Front: Characteristics of "Stable" tissues?

Back: Quiescent ($G_0$ phase) but can divide in response to injury (e.g., Liver, Kidney).

Front: Characteristics of "Permanent" tissues?

Back: Terminally differentiated; non-proliferative (e.g., Neurons, Cardiac myocytes).

Front: Definition of Hypercalcemia of Malignancy?

Back: Result of PTHrP secretion or osteolytic bone metastases.

Front: What is "Atrophy"?

Back: Shrinkage in the size of the cell by loss of cell substance.

Front: Role of Ubiquitin-Proteasome pathway in atrophy?

Back: Degrades nuclear and cytosolic proteins when cells are under-stimulated or malnourished.

Front: Mechanism of Steatosis (Fatty Change)?

Back: Abnormal accumulation of triglycerides within parenchymal cells (most common in liver).

Front: Definition of Hemosiderosis?

Back: Systemic overload of iron without associated tissue damage.

Front: Definition of Hemochromatosis?

Back: Genetic or acquired iron overload resulting in tissue damage (liver, heart, pancreas).

Front: Function of Telomerase in cancer?

Back: Maintains telomere length, providing "immortality" to cancer cells by avoiding replicative senescence.

Front: Most common paraneoplastic syndrome?

Back: Hypercalcemia.

Front: Definition of Cachexia?

Back: Progressive loss of body fat and lean body mass, accompanied by profound weakness and anorexia (mediated by TNF).

Front: Primary mediator of the Vasodilation phase of inflammation?

Back: Histamine and Nitric Oxide (NO).

Front: Cell type that predominates in Chronic Inflammation?

Back: Macrophages.

Front: Cytokine that induces Cachexia?

Back: TNF-alpha.

Front: What is "Desmoplasia"?

Back: Abundant collagenous stroma induced by certain tumors (e.g., breast cancer), making them feel hard/gritty.

Front: Definition of "Carcinoma in situ"?

Back: Full-thickness dysplasia of the epithelium without penetration of the basement membrane.

Front: Significance of the $c-MYC$ oncogene?

Back: Transcription factor involved in cell cycle progression; associated with Burkitt Lymphoma t(8;14).

Front: Definition of "Transudate"?

Back: Fluid with low protein content and low specific gravity; result of hydrostatic/oncotic pressure imbalance.

Front: Definition of "Exudate"?

Back: Fluid with high protein content and cellular debris; result of increased vascular permeability (inflammation).

Front: Most common type of embolus?

Back: Thromboembolus.

Front: What are "Heart Failure Cells"?

Back: Hemosiderin-laden macrophages in the lungs resulting from chronic pulmonary congestion.

Front: Effect of Aspirin on the Arachidonic Acid pathway?

Back: Irreversible inhibition of Cyclooxygenase (COX-1 and COX-2).

Front: Key cytokine for Fibrosis and Scarring?

Back: TGF-beta.

Front: Primary enzyme in Neutrophil granules that produces HOCl?

Back: Myeloperoxidase (MPO).

Front: Definition of Dysplasia?

Back: Disordered growth; loss of cellular uniformity and architectural orientation (often pre-neoplastic).

Front: What is an "Infarct"?

Back: An area of ischemic necrosis caused by occlusion of either the arterial supply or the venous drainage.

Front: Typical outcome of Septic Shock?

Back: Multiple Organ Dysfunction Syndrome (MODS).

Front: Role of P-glycoprotein in cancer cells?

Back: An efflux pump that can expel chemotherapeutic drugs, leading to multi-drug resistance.

Front: Cause of "Nutmeg Liver"?

Back: Chronic passive congestion of the liver due to right-sided heart failure.

Front: Definition of "Hypoplasia"?

Back: Incomplete development of an organ so that it fails to reach adult size.

Front: Definition of "Aplasia"?

Back: Failure of an organ or tissue to develop or to function normally.

Front: What are "Psammoma Bodies"?

Back: Laminated, concentric calcified spheres seen in Papillary Thyroid CA, Meningioma, and Serous Papillary Ovarian CA.

Front: Principal mediator of fever?

Back: Prostaglandins (specifically $PGE_2$ in the hypothalamus).

Front: Most common mutation in human cancers?

Back: $TP53$.

Front: Primary chemical mediator of the "Late Phase" of Type I Hypersensitivity?

Back: Leukotrienes ($LTC_4, LTD_4, LTE_4$).

Front: Major function of $IFN-\gamma$ in inflammation?

Back: Activation of macrophages ("Classical pathway").

Front: What characterizes "Zenker Degeneration"?

Back: Severe waxy/hyaline degeneration of skeletal muscle (often in rectus abdominis) during severe infections like Typhoid.

Front: What is the "Warburg Effect"?

Back: Preference of cancer cells for aerobic glycolysis (fermentation of glucose to lactate) even in the presence of oxygen.

Front: Primary trigger for the Lectin Pathway of Complement?

Back: Plasma mannose-binding lectin binding to carbohydrates on microbes.

Front: Component of Complement that acts as a potent Chemotactic agent?

Back: C5a.

Front: Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) components?

Back: C5b-C9.

Front: Primary cell type involved in the formation of Keloids?

Back: Fibroblasts (excessive collagen deposition).

Front: Key difference between Hypertrophy and Hyperplasia in Cardiac Muscle?

Back: Cardiac muscle undergoes Hypertrophy only (permanent tissue).

Front: Mechanism of "Lipofuscin" accumulation?

Back: "Wear-and-tear" pigment; byproduct of lipid peroxidation of subcellular membranes in aging or atrophied cells.

Front: Primary finding in "Wet Gangrene"?

Back: Liquefactive necrosis superimposed on coagulative necrosis (usually due to bacterial infection).

Front: What is "Autophagy"?

Back: Process in which a cell eats its own contents for survival during nutrient deprivation.

Front: Definition of "SIRS" (Systemic Inflammatory Response Syndrome)?

Back: Systemic inflammation triggered by various insults (not just infection); precursor to Sepsis.

Front: Major role of Vitamin A in epithelial health?

Back: Necessary for orderly differentiation of specialized epithelia; deficiency leads to squamous metaplasia.

Front: Definition of "Hapten"?

Back: A small molecule that can elicit an immune response only when attached to a large carrier such as a protein.


Front: Mechanism of Mitochondrial Permeability Transition Pore (MPTP) opening?

Back: Triggered by high cytosolic $Ca^{2+}$, oxidative stress, and lipid breakdown; results in loss of mitochondrial membrane potential and ATP depletion.

Front: Consequence of ATP depletion on the Na+/K+ pump?

Back: Pump failure leads to intracellular $Na^+$ accumulation, $K^+$ efflux, and cellular swelling (hydropic change) due to iso-osmotic water gain.

Front: Primary source of Free Radicals in the Fenton Reaction?

Back: $Fe^{2+} + H_2O_2 \rightarrow Fe^{3+} + OH^{\bullet} + OH^{-}$.

Front: Enzyme that neutralizes Superoxide ($O_2^{\bullet-}$)?

Back: Superoxide Dismutase (SOD), converting it to $H_2O_2$.

Front: Effect of Cytosolic Calcium increase on enzymes?

Back: Activates Phospholipases (membrane damage), Proteases (cytoskeleton damage), Endonucleases (DNA fragmentation), and ATPases.

Front: Definition of "Mallory Bodies"?

Back: Ubiquitinated cytokeratin intermediate filaments found in hepatocytes, typically in Alcoholic Liver Disease.

Section 10: The Complement System & Mediators

Front: Function of C3a and C5a (Anaphylatoxins)?

Back: Stimulate histamine release from mast cells; increase vascular permeability and cause vasodilation.

Front: Which complement component is the primary Opsonin?

Back: C3b.

Front: Prostaglandin responsible for Bronchoconstriction and Platelet Aggregation?

Back: Thromboxane $A_2$ ($TXA_2$).

Front: Action of Leukotrienes $C_4, D_4, E_4$?

Back: Potent vasoconstriction, bronchospasm, and increased vascular permeability (more potent than histamine).

Front: Two major anti-inflammatory cytokines that "turn off" the immune response?

Back: TGF-$\beta$ and IL-10.

Section 11: Hemodynamics & Vascular Pathology

Front: Pathophysiology of Septic Shock?

Back: Endotoxins (LPS) trigger massive cytokine release (TNF, IL-1), leading to systemic vasodilation, endothelial injury, and DIC.

Front: What is "Trousseau Sign" (Malignancy)?

Back: Migratory thrombophlebitis; venous thrombi appearing and disappearing in different sites, often associated with Pancreatic Cancer.

Front: Factor V Leiden mutation mechanism?

Back: Mutation makes Factor V resistant to cleavage by Protein C, leading to a hypercoagulable state.

Front: Definition of "Mural Thrombus"?

Back: Thrombi occurring in heart chambers or in the aortic lumen.

Front: Effect of Nitric Oxide (NO) on platelets?

Back: Inhibits platelet aggregation and adhesion (potent vasodilator).

Section 12: Immunology & Autoimmunity

Front: Mechanism of Central Tolerance (Thymus)?

Back: Negative selection; deletion of self-reactive T cells that recognize self-antigens presented by MHC.

Front: Role of AIRE (Autoimmune Regulator) protein?

Back: Stimulates expression of peripheral tissue-restricted antigens in the thymus to facilitate negative selection.

Front: Genetic association with Ankylosing Spondylitis?

Back: HLA-B27.

Front: Serologic marker for Drug-Induced Lupus?

Back: Anti-histone antibodies (associated with Hydralazine, Procainamide, Isoniazid).

Front: Characteristic histological finding in Sjogren Syndrome?

Back: Lymphocytic infiltration of exocrine glands (lacrimal and salivary) with associated fibrosis.

Front: Definition of "CREST" Syndrome (Limited Scleroderma)?

Back: Calcinosis, Raynaud's, Esophageal dysmotility, Sclerodactyly, Telangiectasia.

Section 13: Neoplasia & Molecular Oncology

Front: Role of the $VHL$ gene?

Back: Part of an ubiquitin ligase complex that degrades Hypoxia-Inducible Factor ($HIF1\alpha$); mutation leads to Renal Cell Carcinoma.

Front: Significance of the $HER2/neu$ (ERBB2) gene?

Back: Epidermal growth factor receptor amplified in ~20% of breast cancers; targeted by Trastuzumab.

Front: Mechanism of "Chemical Carcinogenesis" (Initiation vs. Promotion)?

Back: Initiation results in permanent DNA damage; Promotion stimulates clonal expansion of the mutated cell.

Front: Most common location for Distant Metastasis of abdominal cancers?

Back: Liver (via the portal circulation).

Front: Definition of "Paraneoplastic Syndrome"?

Back: Symptom complexes in cancer patients that cannot be explained by local/distant spread of the tumor or hormones indigenous to the tissue of origin.

Section 14: Repair, Regeneration, and ECM

Front: Type of collagen found in a "Mature Scar"?

Back: Type I Collagen (strongest).

Front: Primary growth factor for Angiogenesis?

Back: VEGF (Vascular Endothelial Growth Factor).

Front: Definition of "Contracture"?

Back: Exaggeration of wound contraction resulting in deformities of the wound and surrounding tissues (common after serious burns).

Front: What is "Primary Hemostasis"?

Back: Formation of the platelet plug.

Front: What is "Secondary Hemostasis"?

Back: Deposition of insoluble fibrin via the coagulation cascade.

Section 15: Environmental & Nutritional

Front: Clinical sign of Lead Poisoning in children?

Back: Burton lines (bluish line on gingiva), abdominal colic, and "Lead lines" on metaphyses of long bones on X-ray.

Front: Organ system most sensitive to Carbon Monoxide (CO) poisoning?

Back: Central Nervous System (cherry-red discoloration of skin and mucous membranes).

Front: Effect of Vitamin E deficiency?

Back: Hemolytic anemia, posterior column and spinocerebellar tract demyelination (mimics Vitamin B12 deficiency but without megaloblastic anemia).

Front: Major risk factor for Mesothelioma?

Back: Asbestos exposure.

Front: What is "Anticipation" in genetics?

Back: Increased severity or earlier onset of a disease in succeeding generations (e.g., Huntington Disease, Fragile X).

Front: Definition of Prader-Willi Syndrome?

Back: Deletion of paternal 15q11-q13; characterized by hyperphagia, obesity, and hypogonadism.

Front: Definition of Angelman Syndrome?

Back: Deletion of maternal 15q11-q13; characterized by "Happy Puppet" gait, seizures, and laughter.

Front: Mechanism of Fragile X Syndrome?

Back: CGG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the $FMR1$ gene.

Section 17: Amyloidosis (Detailed)

Front: Major protein type in Secondary (Reactive) Amyloidosis?

Back: AA Protein (derived from Serum Amyloid-Associated protein; seen in chronic inflammation).

Front: Major protein type in Primary Amyloidosis?

Back: AL Protein (derived from Ig Light Chains; seen in Multiple Myeloma).

Front: Type of amyloid found in Alzheimer’s Disease?

Back: $A\beta$ amyloid (derived from Amyloid Precursor Protein/APP).

Front: Amyloid type associated with Chronic Renal Dialysis?

Back: $\beta_2$-microglobulin.

Front: Amyloid type found in Medullary Carcinoma of the Thyroid?

Back: A-Cal (derived from Calcitonin).

Section 18: Advanced Neoplasia & Oncoviruses

Front: Viral protein of HPV 16/18 that inhibits p53?

Back: E6.

Front: Viral protein of HPV 16/18 that inhibits Rb?

Back: E7.

Front: Virus associated with Nasopharyngeal Carcinoma and Burkitt Lymphoma?

Back: Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV).

Front: Virus associated with Kaposi Sarcoma?

Back: HHV-8.

Front: Mechanism of the $t(9;22)$ Philadelphia Chromosome?

Back: $BCR-ABL$ fusion gene; creates a constitutively active tyrosine kinase (CML).

Front: Primary oncogene in Neuroblastoma?

Back: $N-MYC$ (amplification).

Front: Definition of "Sentinel Lymph Node"?

Back: The first node in a regional lymphatic basin that receives lymph flow from a primary tumor.

Section 19: Fluid, Electrolyte & Acid-Base Basics

Front: Difference between "Pitting" and "Non-pitting" Edema?

Back: Pitting: Low protein (Transudate); Non-pitting: Lymphatic obstruction or Myxedema.

Front: Major cause of decreased plasma oncotic pressure?

Back: Hypoalbuminemia (e.g., Nephrotic syndrome, Cirrhosis).

Front: Effect of Hyperaldosteronism on Potassium?

Back: Hypokalemia (increased renal excretion of $K^+$).

Front: Definition of "Respiratory Acidosis"?

Back: Decreased pH due to $CO_2$ retention (Hypoventilation).

Section 20: Cellular Aging & Storage Diseases

Front: Genetic defect in Niemann-Pick Disease?

Back: Sphingomyelinase deficiency.

Front: Histological appearance of Gaucher cells?

Back: "Crinkled paper" or "Crumpled tissue paper" cytoplasm (macrophages laden with glucocerebroside).

Front: Cause of "I-cell Disease" (Inclusion cell disease)?

Back: Failure of Golgi to phosphorylate mannose residues (Mannose-6-Phosphate); enzymes are secreted extracellularly instead of to lysosomes.

Front: Major theory of cellular senescence?

Back: Telomere shortening (the Hayflick limit).

Section 21: Vascular Pathology & Infarction

Front: Definition of "Phlebothrombosis"?

Back: Venous thrombosis without preceding inflammation.

Front: Typical site for "Liquefactive Necrosis" in the absence of infection?

Back: The Brain (due to high lipid content and lack of supportive stroma).

Front: Difference between "Sepsis" and "Septicemia"?

Back: Sepsis: Life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by dysregulated host response to infection; Septicemia: Viable bacteria in the blood.

Section 22: Inflammation (The "Refined" Details)

Front: Enzyme that converts Arachidonic Acid to Leukotrienes?

Back: 5-Lipoxygenase.

Front: Component of the inflammasome that activates IL-1?

Back: Caspase-1.

Front: Primary function of "Hageman Factor" (Factor XII)?

Back: Links the coagulation, complement, and kinin systems during injury.

Front: Difference between "Granulomatous Inflammation" and "Granulation Tissue"?

Back: Granulomatous: Chronic inflammation (macrophages); Granulation: Healing/repair (fibroblasts/vessels).




Front: Marker used to identify Hematopoietic Stem Cells?
Back: CD34+.

Front: Primary receptor for Rhinovirus?
Back: ICAM-1.

Front: Primary receptor for EBV on B-cells?
Back: CD21 (CR2).

Front: Primary receptor for HIV?
Back: CD4 (with CXCR4 or CCR5 co-receptors).

Front: Enzyme that is a serum marker for Myocardial Infarction (early)?Back: Troponin I or T (peaks at 24 hours, stays elevated for 7–10 days).

Section 24: Vascular & Coagulation Pathways

Front: The "Extrinsic Pathway" of coagulation is triggered by?Back: Tissue Factor (Factor III/Thromboplastin).

Front: The "Intrinsic Pathway" of coagulation is triggered by?Back: Factor XII (Hageman Factor) contact with subendothelial collagen.

Front: Lab test used to monitor the Extrinsic Pathway?
Back: PT (Prothrombin Time) / INR.

Front: Lab test used to monitor the Intrinsic Pathway?
Back: PTT (Partial Thromboplastin Time).

Front: Function of Protein C and Protein S?
Back: Degrade Factors Va and VIIIa (act as natural anticoagulants).

Front: Effect of Antithrombin III?
Back: Inhibits Thrombin (IIa) and Factor Xa.

Section 25: Specific Genetic Mutations in Cancer

Front: Mutation associated with Li-Fraumeni Syndrome?
Back: Germline mutation of TP53.

Front: Mutation associated with Familial Adenomatous Polyposis (FAP)?Back: APC gene.

Front: Genetic translocation in Ewing Sarcoma?
Back: t(11;22).

Front: Genetic translocation in Mantle Cell Lymphoma?
Back: t(11;14) (Cyclin D1 overexpression).

Front: Gene involved in MEN 2A and 2B?
Back: RET proto-oncogene.

Section 26: Immunodeficiency & Hypersensitivity Fine-tuning

Front: What is the "Arthus Reaction"?
Back: A localized Type III hypersensitivity reaction; skin necrosis following antigen injection in a previously sensitized individual.

Front: Mechanism of Chronic Transplant Rejection?
Back: T-cell mediated process (Type IV) resulting in vascular smooth muscle proliferation and parenchymal fibrosis.

Front: Feature of Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome?
Back: WATER: Wiskott-Aldrich, Thrombocytopenia, Eczema, Recurrent infections (defective WASP protein).

Front: Defect in Hyper-IgM Syndrome?
Back: Defective CD40L on T-cells; B-cells cannot undergo class switching.

Section 27: Physical & Chemical Injury

Front: Most common cause of hyperthermia (Heat Stroke)?
Back: Failure of thermoregulatory mechanisms leading to core temp > 40°C (104°F).

Front: Difference between 1st, 2nd, and 3rd Degree Burns?
Back: 1st: Epidermis (Red/Pain); 2nd: Dermis (Blisters); 3rd: Full thickness (Painless/White/Leathery).

Front: Mechanism of Ionizing Radiation damage?Back: Direct DNA damage or Indirect damage via production of free radicals from water (Radiolysis).

Front: Primary toxic metabolite of Acetaminophen?
Back: NAPQI (depletes Glutathione).

Section 28: Nutrition & Trace Elements

Front: Symptom of Zinc deficiency?
Back: Delayed wound healing, suppressed immunity, and Acrodermatitis enteropathica.

Front: Consequence of Vitamin B12 deficiency (besides anemia)?Back: Subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord (dorsal columns and corticospinal tracts).

Front: Vitamin required for the hydroxylation of Proline and Lysine in collagen?
Back: Vitamin C.


=====================================================================
=====================================================================

General Pharmacology:



Front: Definition of Bioavailability ($F$)?

Back: The fraction of administered drug that reaches systemic circulation unchanged.

Front: Bioavailability of an intravenous (IV) drug?

Back: 100% ($F = 1$).

Front: Formula for Volume of Distribution ($V_d$)?

Back: $V_d = \frac{\text{amount of drug in the body}}{\text{plasma drug concentration}}$.

Front: Characteristics of drugs with a low $V_d$?

Back: Large/charged molecules; often plasma protein-bound (remains in vascular compartment).

Front: Characteristics of drugs with a high $V_d$?

Back: Small lipophilic molecules; distributed into tissues and fat.

Front: Formula for Clearance ($CL$)?

Back: $CL = \frac{0.693 \times V_d}{t_{1/2}}$ or $CL = \frac{\text{rate of elimination}}{\text{plasma concentration}}$.

Front: Definition of Half-life ($t_{1/2}$)?

Back: The time required to reduce the plasma concentration of a drug by 50%.

Front: How many half-lives are required to reach steady-state concentration ($C_{ss}$)?

Back: Approximately 4 to 5 half-lives.

Front: Formula for Loading Dose ($LD$)?

Back: $LD = \frac{C_p \times V_d}{F}$ ($C_p$ = target plasma concentration).

Front: Formula for Maintenance Dose ($MD$)?

Back: $MD = \frac{C_p \times CL \times \tau}{F}$ ($\tau$ = dosage interval).

Front: Primary site of drug metabolism?

Back: Liver.

Front: Primary site of drug excretion?

Back: Kidney.

Section 2: Drug Elimination Kinetics

Front: Zero-order elimination definition?

Back: A constant amount of drug is eliminated per unit time (independent of concentration).

Front: Examples of drugs with Zero-order elimination?

Back: Phenytoin, Ethanol, Aspirin (at high doses). Mnemonic: PEA.

Front: First-order elimination definition?

Back: A constant fraction of drug is eliminated per unit time (proportional to concentration).

Front: Which elimination type shows a linear decrease on a plasma concentration vs. time graph?

Back: Zero-order.

Front: Effect of urinary acidification on weak base excretion?

Back: Increases excretion (traps the drug in its ionized, polar form).

Front: Agent used to acidify urine?

Back: Ammonium chloride ($NH_4Cl$).

Front: Effect of urinary alkalinization on weak acid (e.g., Aspirin) excretion?

Back: Increases excretion.

Front: Agent used to alkalinize urine?

Back: Sodium bicarbonate ($NaHCO_3$).

Section 3: Metabolism (Phase I & II)

Front: Purpose of Phase I metabolism?

Back: Reduction, oxidation, hydrolysis via Cytochrome P450 to yield slightly polar, water-soluble metabolites.

Front: Key enzyme system in Phase I metabolism?

Back: Cytochrome P450 (CYP450).

Front: Purpose of Phase II metabolism?

Back: Conjugation (Glucuronidation, Acetylation, Sulfonation) to yield very polar, inactive metabolites.

Front: Phase II reaction that Geriatric patients retain longest?

Back: Conjugation (Glucuronidation).

Front: Impact of "Fast Acetylators" vs "Slow Acetylators"?

Back: Slow acetylators have increased side effects from drugs like Isoniazid or Procainamide due to decreased metabolism.

Front: Definition of a Prodrug?

Back: An inactive compound that is metabolized into a pharmacologically active drug.

Section 4: Pharmacodynamics (Receptors & Potency)

Front: Definition of Efficacy ($V_{max}$)?

Back: The maximal effect a drug can produce regardless of dose.

Front: Definition of Potency ($EC_{50}$)?

Back: The amount of drug needed to produce a given effect (usually 50% of peak).

Front: Effect of a Competitive Antagonist on $V_{max}$ and $K_m$?

Back: No change in $V_{max}$; Increases $K_m$ (decreases potency).

Front: Effect of a Non-competitive Antagonist on $V_{max}$ and $K_m$?

Back: Decreases $V_{max}$; No change in $K_m$.

Front: Definition of an Irreversible Antagonist?

Back: Binds covalently to the receptor; cannot be displaced by increasing agonist concentration.

Front: Definition of a Partial Agonist?

Back: Acts at the same site as a full agonist but with lower maximal efficacy.

Front: Therapeutic Index ($TI$) formula?

Back: $TI = \frac{TD_{50}}{ED_{50}}$ (Toxic dose / Effective dose).

Front: Clinical significance of a low $TI$?

Back: Narrow window between effective and toxic dose; requires frequent monitoring (e.g., Warfarin, Digoxin, Lithium).

Section 5: Cytochrome P450 Interactions

Front: Common CYP450 Inducers?

Back: Griseofulvin, Phenytoin, Carbamazepine, Rifampin, St. John’s Wort, Barbiturates, Chronic alcohol. (Mnemonic: GP Craves Rifampin St. Barbs).

Front: Common CYP450 Inhibitors?

Back: Sodium Valproate, Isoniazid, Cimetidine, Ketoconazole, Erythromycin, Grapefruit juice, Amiodarone, Ritonavir. (Mnemonic: SICK GARA).

Front: Effect of CYP450 Inhibitors on co-administered drugs?

Back: Decreases metabolism, leading to increased drug levels and potential toxicity.

Front: Effect of CYP450 Inducers on co-administered drugs?

Back: Increases metabolism, leading to decreased drug levels and subtherapeutic effects.

Section 6: Autonomic Pharmacology Basics

Front: Primary neurotransmitter for Pre-ganglionic Autonomic fibers?

Back: Acetylcholine (ACh).

Front: Receptor type at Autonomic Ganglia?

Back: Nicotinic ($N_n$).

Front: Neurotransmitter for Parasympathetic Post-ganglionic fibers?

Back: Acetylcholine (ACh).

Front: Neurotransmitter for Sympathetic Post-ganglionic fibers (except sweat glands)?

Back: Norepinephrine (NE).

Front: Receptor type on skeletal muscle motor endplate?

Back: Nicotinic ($N_m$).

Front: Rate-limiting step in Catecholamine synthesis?

Back: Tyrosine hydroxylase (converts Tyrosine to DOPA).

Front: Enzyme that degrades NE in the presynaptic nerve terminal?

Back: Monoamine Oxidase (MAO).

Section 7: Toxicology & Antidotes

Front: Antidote for Acetaminophen toxicity?

Back: N-acetylcysteine (restores Glutathione).

Front: Antidote for Benzodiazepine overdose?

Back: Flumazenil.

Front: Antidote for Opioid overdose?

Back: Naloxone.

Front: Antidote for Heparin toxicity?

Back: Protamine sulfate.

Front: Antidote for Warfarin toxicity (immediate)?

Back: Fresh Frozen Plasma (FFP) or Prothrombin Complex Concentrate (PCC).

Front: Antidote for Carbon Monoxide poisoning?

Back: 100% $O_2$ or Hyperbaric $O_2$.

Front: Antidote for Cyanide poisoning?

Back: Nitrite + Sodium Thiosulfate, or Hydroxocobalamin.

Front: Antidote for Lead poisoning?

Back: EDTA, Dimercaprol, Succimer (in children).

Front: Antidote for Organophosphate (nerve gas) poisoning?

Back: Atropine + Pralidoxime (2-PAM).

Front: Antidote for Iron toxicity?

Back: Deferoxamine.

Front: Antidote for Methanol or Ethylene Glycol poisoning?

Back: Fomepizole (inhibits Alcohol Dehydrogenase).

Front: Antidote for Digoxin toxicity?

Back: Digoxin-specific Fab fragments.

Section 8: Adverse Drug Reactions (High Yield)

Front: Drug associated with Gray Baby Syndrome?

Back: Chloramphenicol.

Front: Drug associated with Red Man Syndrome?

Back: Vancomycin.

Front: Drug associated with Disulfiram-like reactions?

Back: Metronidazole, certain Cephalosporins, Griseofulvin.

Front: Classic drugs causing Stevens-Johnson Syndrome (SJS)?

Back: Anticonvulsants (Lamotrigine, Phenytoin), Sulfonamides, Penicillin, Allopurinol.

Front: Drug associated with Tendon Rupture?

Back: Fluoroquinolones (e.g., Ciprofloxacin).

Front: Drug associated with Ototoxicity and Nephrotoxicity?

Back: Aminoglycosides, Cisplatin, Loop diuretics.

Front: Drug class causing "Cinchonism" (tinnitus, headache)?

Back: Quinidine, Quinine.

Front: Drug associated with Gingival Hyperplasia?

Back: Phenytoin, Calcium Channel Blockers (Verapamil, Nifedipine), Cyclosporine.

Front: Common drugs causing Drug-Induced Lupus?

Back: Sulfa drugs, Hydralazine, Isoniazid, Procainamide, Phenytoin, Etanercept. (Mnemonic: SHIPPE).

Section 9: Signal Transduction Pathways

Front: $G_q$ receptor mechanism?

Back: Activates Phospholipase C $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ $IP_3$, $DAG$, $Ca^{2+}$.

Front: $G_s$ receptor mechanism?

Back: Activates Adenylate Cyclase $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ $cAMP$ $\rightarrow$ Protein Kinase A.

Front: $G_i$ receptor mechanism?

Back: Inhibits Adenylate Cyclase $\rightarrow$ $\downarrow$ $cAMP$.

Front: Receptors using $G_q$ pathway?

Back: $H_1$, $\alpha_1$, $V_1$, $M_1$, $M_3$ (Mnemonic: HAV 1 M&M).

Front: Receptors using $G_s$ pathway?

Back: $\beta_1$, $\beta_2$, $\beta_3$, $D_1$, $V_2$, $H_2$.

Front: Receptors using $G_i$ pathway?

Back: $M_2$, $\alpha_2$, $D_2$ (Mnemonic: MAD 2s).

Section 10: Legal & Developmental

Front: FDA Pregnancy Category X definition?

Back: Studies demonstrate fetal abnormalities; risks clearly outweigh benefits.

Front: Phase I Clinical Trial focus?

Back: Safety and Pharmacokinetics (Small group of healthy volunteers).

Front: Phase II Clinical Trial focus?

Back: Efficacy and Optimal Dosing (Small group of patients with disease).

Front: Phase III Clinical Trial focus?

Back: Comparison to Standard of Care (Large group of patients).

Front: Phase IV Clinical Trial focus?

Back: Post-marketing surveillance for rare side effects.


Front: Primary effect of $\alpha_1$ receptor activation?

Back: Vasoconstriction, increased peripheral resistance, mydriasis, and increased internal urethral sphincter tone.

Front: Primary effect of $\alpha_2$ receptor activation?

Back: Inhibition of NE, ACh, and insulin release; decreased aqueous humor production.

Front: Primary effect of $\beta_1$ receptor activation?

Back: Increased heart rate (chronotropy), increased contractility (inotropy), and increased renin release.

Front: Primary effect of $\beta_2$ receptor activation?

Back: Vasodilation, bronchodilation, increased lipolysis, and decreased uterine tone (tocolysis).

Front: Primary effect of $\beta_3$ receptor activation?

Back: Increased lipolysis and thermogenesis; relaxation of the detrusor muscle in the bladder.

Front: Effect of $\alpha_1$ activation on the eye?

Back: Contraction of the pupillary dilator muscle (Mydriasis).

Front: Dominant receptor on the heart?

Back: $\beta_1$.

Front: Dominant receptor on bronchial smooth muscle?

Back: $\beta_2$.

Section 12: Cholinergic Receptors (Parasympathetic)

Front: Location of $M_1$ receptors?

Back: CNS and enteric nervous system.

Front: Location and effect of $M_2$ receptors?

Back: Heart (atria/nodes); decreases heart rate and atrial contractility.

Front: Effect of $M_3$ activation on the eye?

Back: Contraction of the pupillary sphincter (Miosis) and ciliary muscle (Accommodation).

Front: Effect of $M_3$ activation on the GI/GU tract?

Back: Increased peristalsis, increased secretions, and contraction of the detrusor muscle (voiding).

Front: Effect of $M_3$ activation on blood vessels?

Back: Vasodilation via Nitric Oxide (NO) release (though vessels lack direct PSNS innervation).

Front: Result of Nicotinic ($N_n$) receptor stimulation at the adrenal medulla?

Back: Release of Epinephrine and Norepinephrine into the blood.

Section 13: Second Messengers (The "QISS" Rule)

Front: Signal pathway for $\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \beta_1, \beta_2$ (in order)?

Back: $q, i, s, s$ (QISS).

Front: Signal pathway for $M_1, M_2, M_3$ (in order)?

Back: $q, i, q$ (QIQ).

Front: Receptors that increase $cAMP$ levels?

Back: $\beta_1, \beta_2, \beta_3, D_1, V_2, H_2$.

Front: Receptors that decrease $cAMP$ levels?

Back: $M_2, \alpha_2, D_2$.

Front: Receptors that increase $IP_3/DAG$ ($Ca^{2+}$)?

Back: $\alpha_1, M_1, M_3, H_1, V_1$.

Section 14: Sympathomimetics & Blockers

Front: Epinephrine's effect at low doses?

Back: Primarily $\beta$ effects (vasodilation, bronchodilation).

Front: Epinephrine's effect at high doses?

Back: Primarily $\alpha$ effects (vasoconstriction).

Front: What is "Epinephrine Reversal"?

Back: Administration of an $\alpha$-blocker before Epinephrine, causing a net decrease in BP due to unopposed $\beta_2$ action.

Front: Primary use of Isoproterenol?

Back: Electrophysiologic evaluation of tachyarrhythmias (pure $\beta_1 = \beta_2$ agonist).

Front: Mechanism of Cocaine as a sympathomimetic?

Back: Inhibits NET (Norepinephrine Transporter), preventing reuptake of NE into the presynaptic neuron.

Front: Mechanism of Amphetamines?

Back: Displaces stored NE from vesicles into the synapse via NET reversal.

Front: Why are $\beta$-blockers contraindicated in Cocaine use?

Back: Leads to "unopposed $\alpha_1$ stimulation," causing severe hypertensive crisis.

Front: Selective $\beta_1$ blockers (AmeBA)?

Back: Atenolol, Metoprolol, Esmolol, Bisoprolol, Acebutolol.

Front: Non-selective $\alpha$ and $\beta$ blockers?

Back: Carvedilol and Labetalol.

Section 15: Cholinomimetics & Anticholinergics

Front: Mechanism of Neostigmine?

Back: Reversible AChE (Acetylcholinesterase) inhibitor; does not cross the BBB.

Front: Clinical use of Edrophonium?

Back: Historically used to diagnose Myasthenia Gravis (Tensilon test).

Front: Symptoms of Organophosphate poisoning?

Back: DUMBBELLS: Diarrhea, Urination, Miosis, Bronchospasm, Bradycardia, Emesis, Lacrimation, Lethargy, Salivation.

Front: Treatment for Atropine overdose?

Back: Physostigmine (crosses the BBB).

Front: Contraindications for Atropine?

Back: Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma, BPH, Hyperthermia.

Section 16: Clinical Pharmacokinetics Equations

Front: Calculation for Drug Bioavailability ($F$)?

Back: $F = \frac{AUC_{oral}}{AUC_{IV}}$ ($AUC$ = Area Under the Curve).

Front: Relationship between $V_d$ and Half-life?

Back: As $V_d$ increases, half-life ($t_{1/2}$) increases.

Front: Relationship between Clearance ($CL$) and Half-life?

Back: As $CL$ increases, half-life ($t_{1/2}$) decreases.

Front: Loading dose correction for renal/hepatic failure?

Back: Loading dose remains the same (it depends on $V_d$, not $CL$).

Front: Maintenance dose correction for renal/hepatic failure?

Back: Maintenance dose must be decreased (it depends on $CL$).

Front: How many half-lives to eliminate >90% of a drug?

Back: 3.3 half-lives (90%) to 4 half-lives (93.75%).

Section 17: Toxicology & High-Yield Associations

Front: Drug causing Cyanide toxicity via metabolism?

Back: Nitroprusside (at high doses or prolonged use).

Front: Mechanism of Nitrites in treating Cyanide poisoning?

Back: Oxidizes Hemoglobin to Methemoglobin, which has a high affinity for Cyanide (sequesters it).

Front: Side effect of Thiazide diuretics on electrolytes?

Back: HyperGLUC: Hyperglycemia, Hyperlipidemia, Hyperuricemia, Hypercalcemia.

Front: Drug class associated with "Flash Pulmonary Edema"?

Back: Loop Diuretics (often used to treat it, but high doses of NSAIDs can worsen the underlying failure).

Front: Side effect of ACE inhibitors?

Back: CATCHH: Cough, Angioedema, Teratogen, Creatinine increase, Hyperkalemia, Hypotension.

Section 18: Drug-Drug Interaction Mechanics

Front: Definition of Synergism?

Back: The combined effect of two drugs is greater than the sum of their individual effects ($1+1=3$).

Front: Definition of Potentiation?

Back: A drug with no effect on its own increases the effect of another drug ($0+1=2$).

Front: Definition of Tachyphylaxis?

Back: Acute, rapid decrease in drug response after repeated administration (e.g., Nitroglycerin, MDMA).

Front: How to prevent Nitroglycerin tachyphylaxis?

Back: Provide a "drug-free interval" (typically 8–12 hours at night).

Section 19: Teratogens (Robbins/First Aid)

Front: Effect of Thalidomide on the fetus?

Back: Phocomelia (limb defects).

Front: Effect of Tetracyclines on the fetus?

Back: Discolored teeth and inhibited bone growth.

Front: Effect of Aminoglycosides on the fetus?

Back: Ototoxicity (CN VIII damage).

Front: Effect of Isotretinoin (Vitamin A) on the fetus?

Back: Severe CNS, craniofacial, and cardiac defects.

Front: Effect of Warfarin on the fetus?

Back: Bone/cartilage deformities and fetal hemorrhage (heparin is preferred in pregnancy).

Section 20: Receptor Dynamics

Front: Up-regulation occurs when?

Back: Chronic use of an antagonist (body creates more receptors to compensate).

Front: Down-regulation occurs when?

Back: Chronic use of an agonist (body removes receptors to avoid overstimulation).

Front: What happens to the Dose-Response curve during Desensitization?

Back: It shifts to the right (higher dose needed for same effect).

Front: Spare Receptors definition?

Back: Receptors that exist in excess of those required to produce a maximal response.

Front: How do you detect Spare Receptors?

Back: If $EC_{50} < K_d$ (the concentration for half-maximal effect is less than the concentration for half-maximal binding).


Front: Mechanism of Passive Diffusion?

Back: Movement of drug down its concentration gradient; no energy or carrier required.

Front: Type of transport that requires ATP and moves against a gradient?

Back: Active Transport.

Front: Type of transport that uses a carrier but no energy (down-gradient)?

Back: Facilitated Diffusion.

Front: Property of drugs that can cross the Blood-Brain Barrier (BBB)?

Back: Small, non-polar, and highly lipid-soluble.

Front: Primary protein responsible for drug efflux (pumping drugs out of cells)?

Back: P-glycoprotein (MDR1).

Front: Consequence of P-glycoprotein expression in cancer cells?

Back: Multi-drug resistance (pumping chemotherapeutic agents out of the cell).

Section 22: Advanced Signal Transduction

Front: Mechanism of the Insulin receptor?

Back: Receptor Tyrosine Kinase (RTK) $\rightarrow$ MAP-kinase pathway.

Front: Mechanism of the ANP/BNP receptors?

Back: Membrane-bound Guanylate Cyclase $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ $cGMP$ $\rightarrow$ Protein Kinase G (PKG).

Front: Mechanism of Nitric Oxide (NO)?

Back: Diffuses into cell $\rightarrow$ Soluble Guanylate Cyclase $\rightarrow$ $\uparrow$ $cGMP$.

Front: Mechanism of Steroid/Thyroid hormone receptors?

Back: Intracellular/Nuclear receptors that act as transcription factors to alter gene expression.

Front: Receptors that utilize the JAK/STAT pathway?

Back: Cytokines, Growth Hormone, Erythropoietin, Prolactin. (Mnemonic: PIGGLy: Prolactin, Immunomodulators, Growth hormone, G-CSF, Leptin).

Section 23: Receptor Mathematics & Graphs

Front: Definition of Dissociation Constant ($K_d$)?

Back: The concentration of drug at which 50% of receptors are occupied.

Front: In a Lineweaver-Burk plot, what does the x-intercept represent?

Back: $-1/K_m$.

Front: In a Lineweaver-Burk plot, what does the y-intercept represent?

Back: $1/V_{max}$.

Front: Effect of a Non-competitive inhibitor on a Lineweaver-Burk plot?

Back: x-intercept is unchanged; y-intercept shifts upward ($V_{max}$ decreases).

Front: Effect of a Competitive inhibitor on a Lineweaver-Burk plot?

Back: y-intercept is unchanged; x-intercept shifts right/closer to zero ($K_m$ increases).

Front: Quantal Dose-Response curve vs. Graded Dose-Response curve?

Back: Quantal = "All or none" response (e.g., sleep vs. awake); Graded = Incremental response (e.g., BP change).

Section 24: High-Yield Toxicology Part II

Front: Antidote for Arsenic poisoning?

Back: Dimercaprol or Succimer.

Front: Antidote for Copper toxicity (Wilson’s Disease)?

Back: Penicillamine or Trientine.

Front: Antidote for $\beta$-blocker overdose?

Back: Glucagon (increases cAMP via a non-adrenergic pathway).

Front: Antidote for Theophylline toxicity?

Back: $\beta$-blockers (to treat tachyarrhythmias) and Activated Charcoal.

Front: Treatment for Salicylate (Aspirin) overdose?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate (alkalinizes urine to trap the acid).

Front: Antidote for Amphetamine overdose?

Back: Ammonium Chloride (acidifies urine to trap the base).

Front: Mechanism of Carbon Monoxide toxicity?

Back: Competitive inhibition of $O_2$ binding to hemoglobin + left shift of the dissociation curve.

Section 25: Specific Drug Side Effects (Step 1 Classics)

Front: Drug causing "Cushing-like" symptoms?

Back: Corticosteroids (Prednisone).

Front: Drug causing Diabetes Insipidus?

Back: Lithium or Demeclocycline.

Front: Drug causing Fanconi Syndrome?

Back: Expired Tetracyclines or Ifosfamide.

Front: Drug causing Hemorrhagic Cystitis?

Back: Cyclophosphamide.

Front: Agent used to prevent Cyclophosphamide-induced Hemorrhagic Cystitis?

Back: Mesna.

Front: Drug causing Grayish skin discoloration (Blue-man syndrome)?

Back: Amiodarone.

Front: Drug class causing "First-dose Syncope"?

Back: $\alpha_1$-blockers (e.g., Prazosin).

Front: Drug causing Pulmonary Fibrosis?

Back: Bleomycin, Amiodarone, Methotrexate, Busulfan (Mnemonic: BAMB).

Section 26: Enzyme Kinetics & Pharmacology

Front: Definition of the Therapeutic Window?

Back: The range of drug doses which can treat disease effectively without having toxic effects.

Front: Formula for the Extraction Ratio ($ER$)?

Back: $ER = \frac{C_{in} - C_{out}}{C_{in}}$.

Front: Difference between Redistribution and Elimination?

Back: Redistribution = drug moving from brain/highly perfused organs to fat/muscle (e.g., Thiopental); Elimination = metabolism/excretion.

Front: Reason for the short duration of action of Thiopental?

Back: Rapid redistribution from the brain to skeletal muscle and fat.

Section 27: Autonomic Odds and Ends

Front: Mechanism of Clonidine?

Back: Centrally acting $\alpha_2$-agonist; decreases sympathetic outflow.

Front: Clinical use of Methyldopa?

Back: Hypertension in pregnancy.

Front: Mechanism of Metyrosine?

Back: Inhibits Tyrosine Hydroxylase (stops catecholamine synthesis); used for Pheochromocytoma.

Front: Mechanism of Reserpine?

Back: Inhibits VMAT (blocks storage of NE/Dopamine in vesicles).

Front: Which Autonomic drug causes "Pupillary Block" or worsens Glaucoma?

Back: Epinephrine (due to mydriasis; only in closed-angle).

Front: Drug of choice for hypertensive emergency?

Back: Nitroprusside, Fenoldopam, or Labetalol.

Section 28: Phase of Metabolism Details

Front: Most common Phase II reaction?

Back: Glucuronidation.

Front: Why are neonates susceptible to Chloramphenicol toxicity?

Back: They lack UDP-glucuronosyltransferase (cannot perform Glucuronidation).

Front: Effect of Sulfonamides on Bilirubin in neonates?

Back: Displacement of bilirubin from albumin $\rightarrow$ Kernicterus.

Front: Enzyme inhibited by Disulfiram?

Back: Acetaldehyde dehydrogenase.

Section 29: Final High-Yield Formulas

Front: Relationship between $pH$, $pK_a$, and Drug Ionization (Henderson-Hasselbalch)?

Back: $pH - pK_a = \log(\frac{\text{non-protonated}}{\text{protonated}})$.

Front: Formula for Volume of Distribution ($V_d$) involving body weight?

Back: $V_d = V_{area} \times \text{Weight}$ (often expressed as L/kg).

Front: Clearance ($CL$) in terms of Renal Blood Flow?

Back: $CL = \text{Extraction Ratio} \times \text{Renal Blood Flow}$.


Front: Consequence of Succinylcholine administration in a patient with Pseudocholinesterase deficiency?

Back: Prolonged apnea/paralysis (inability to degrade the drug).

Front: Risk of using Primaquine or Rasburicase in a patient with G6PD deficiency?

Back: Hemolytic anemia (oxidative stress).

Front: Genetic marker associated with hypersensitivity to Abacavir?

Back: HLA-B*5701.

Front: Genetic marker associated with SJS risk from Carbamazepine in Asian populations?

Back: HLA-B*1502.

Front: Effect of CYP2D6 "Ultra-rapid metabolism" on Codeine?

Back: Increased conversion to Morphine, leading to potential opioid toxicity.

Front: Consequence of Thiopurine S-methyltransferase (TPMT) deficiency?

Back: Increased toxicity when treated with 6-Mercaptopurine or Azathioprine (bone marrow suppression).

Section 31: Advanced Drug Interactions & Mechanisms

Front: Mechanism of "Red Wine/Cheese Effect" with MAO Inhibitors?

Back: Tyramine displaces stored NE, causing a hypertensive crisis (Tyramine is usually metabolized by MAO-A).

Front: Drug combination causing "Serotonin Syndrome"?

Back: Any combination of SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs, TCAs, Tramadol, or Triptans.

Front: Physical exam findings in Serotonin Syndrome?

Back: Hyperreflexia, clonus, tremors, and autonomic instability.

Front: Physical exam findings in Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS)?

Back: "Lead-pipe" rigidity, hyperthermia, and elevated Creatine Kinase (CK).

Front: Treatment for Malignant Hyperthermia?

Back: Dantrolene (blocks RyR1 Calcium release).

Section 32: Environmental & Industrial Toxicology

Front: Classic presentation of Lead poisoning in children?

Back: Abdominal pain, constipation, and cognitive impairment (encephalopathy).

Front: Classic presentation of Lead poisoning in adults?

Back: Foot drop/wrist drop (peripheral neuropathy), nephropathy, and "Lead lines" on gums.

Front: Microscopic finding in Lead poisoning (Blood smear)?

Back: Basophilic stippling.

Front: Source of Mercury poisoning and its primary symptom?

Back: Seafood (methylmercury); results in "Minamata disease" (ataxia, paresthesias, visual field loss).

Front: Classic breath odor in Arsenic poisoning?

Back: Garlic breath.

Front: EKG finding in Tricyclic Antidepressant (TCA) overdose?

Back: QRS prolongation (due to Sodium channel blockade).

Front: Treatment for QRS prolongation in TCA overdose?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate (increases extracellular $Na^{+}$ to overcome blockade).

Section 33: Receptor Specificity (High-Yield Comparison)

Front: Catecholamine that increases Systolic BP but decreases Diastolic BP?

Back: Epinephrine (low-dose) or Isoproterenol (via $\beta_2$ vasodilation).

Front: Catecholamine that increases Systolic BP, Diastolic BP, and causes Reflex Bradycardia?

Back: Norepinephrine (primarily $\alpha_1 > \alpha_2 > \beta_1$).

Front: Reflex response to a pure $\alpha_1$ agonist (e.g., Phenylephrine)?

Back: Increase in BP leading to a decrease in Heart Rate (Reflex Bradycardia).

Front: Reflex response to a pure $\beta_2$ agonist (e.g., Albuterol)?

Back: Decrease in BP leading to an increase in Heart Rate (Reflex Tachycardia).

Section 34: Clinical Trials & Regulatory

Front: Definition of an "Orphan Drug"?

Back: A drug developed to treat a rare medical condition (affecting <200,000 people in the US).

Front: Definition of "Compassionate Use"?

Back: Providing an experimental drug to a patient with a life-threatening illness outside of a clinical trial.

Front: What is the "Double-Blind" study design?

Back: Neither the patient nor the researcher knows who is receiving the treatment vs. the placebo.

Front: Primary goal of the FDA?

Back: To ensure the safety, efficacy, and security of drugs, biological products, and medical devices.

Section 35: Absorption & Distribution Odds

Front: Effect of gastric emptying rate on drug absorption?

Back: Faster emptying generally increases the rate of absorption (especially for drugs absorbed in the small intestine).

Front: What is "Enterohepatic Circulation"?

Back: Drugs excreted in bile are reabsorbed from the intestine back into the portal blood, prolonging their action.

Front: Drug that classically undergoes significant First-Pass metabolism?

Back: Nitroglycerin (why it is given sublingually).

Front: Primary plasma protein that binds Acidic drugs?

Back: Albumin.

Front: Primary plasma protein that binds Basic drugs?

Back: $\alpha_1$-acid glycoprotein.


Front: The most common mechanism by which drugs cause cell injury?Back: Formation of reactive free radicals (e.g., Acetaminophen $\rightarrow$ NAPQI).

Front: Mechanism of cellular injury in Acetaminophen toxicity?

Back: Depletion of Glutathione leading to high levels of the toxic metabolite NAPQI, causing free radical damage.

Front: Primary hemodynamic effect of a "Non-selective $\beta$-blocker" on a patient with heart failure?

Back: Decreased Cardiac Output and potential transient increase in Peripheral Resistance (due to $\beta_2$ blockade).

Front: How do NSAIDs decrease the efficacy of Loop Diuretics?

Back: NSAIDs inhibit Prostaglandin synthesis; Prostaglandins are required to maintain renal blood flow and support the action of diuretics.

Front: What is "Physiologic Antagonism"?

Back: Two agonists acting at different receptors to produce opposite effects (e.g., Histamine vs. Epinephrine on bronchial smooth muscle).

Front: Drug class that causes "Redistribution" as its primary mechanism of action termination?

Back: Highly lipid-soluble IV anesthetics (e.g., Thiopental, Propofol).

Section 37: Mathematical Review (The "Must-Knows")

Front: If a drug has a half-life of 10 hours, how much remains after 30 hours?

Back: 12.5% ($100\% \rightarrow 50\% \rightarrow 25\% \rightarrow 12.5\%$).

Front: Formula for Renal Clearance ($CL_r$)?

Back: $CL_r = \frac{U \times V}{P}$ ($U$ = urine conc, $V$ = urine flow rate, $P$ = plasma conc).

Front: How to calculate the new Maintenance Dose when Bioavailability ($F$) changes?

Back: $MD_{new} = \frac{MD_{old} \times F_{old}}{F_{new}}$.

Front: Calculation for $V_d$ if 500mg is given and plasma concentration is 5mg/L?

Back: $V_d = 100L$ ($500 / 5$).


Front: Definition of an Inverse Agonist?

Back: A ligand that binds to the same receptor as an agonist but induces a pharmacological response opposite to that of the agonist (decreases constitutive activity).

Front: Concept of "Constitutive Activity"?

Back: The ability of a receptor to exert a biological effect even in the absence of a bound ligand.

Front: What does a Schild Plot measure?

Back: The $pK_B$ (affinity) of a competitive antagonist.

Front: Definition of the "Ceiling Effect"?

Back: The point at which increasing the dose of a drug no longer increases its therapeutic effect.

Front: Difference between Affinity and Intrinsic Activity?

Back: Affinity is the ability to bind to a receptor; Intrinsic Activity is the ability to activate the receptor and produce a response.

Front: Intrinsic Activity ($α$) value for a Full Agonist?

Back: $α = 1$.

Front: Intrinsic Activity ($α$) value for a Competitive Antagonist?

Back: $α = 0$.

Section 39: Pharmacokinetics (Lippincott & Rang & Dale Details)

Front: Definition of "Volume of Distribution at Steady State" ($V_{ss}$)?

Back: The volume in which the drug appears to be distributed when the rate of drug entry into the systemic circulation equals the rate of elimination.

Front: Purpose of the "Two-Compartment Model"?

Back: To describe drugs that rapidly distribute into highly perfused organs (central) and then slowly into peripheral tissues.

Front: Definition of "Clearance" in terms of Extraction Ratio ($ER$)?

Back: $CL = Q \times ER$ (where $Q$ is blood flow to the organ).

Front: How does "Capacity-Limited Elimination" differ from "Flow-Limited Elimination"?

Back: Capacity-limited depends on enzyme saturation (e.g., Ethanol); Flow-limited depends on how much blood reaches the organ (e.g., Lidocaine).

Front: Effect of Plasma Protein Binding on drug dialyzability?

Back: Highly protein-bound drugs are poorly removed by hemodialysis.

Section 40: Drug Transporters & Signaling (Rang & Dale)

Front: Mechanism of the SLC (Solute Carrier) Transporters?

Back: Facilitated diffusion or secondary active transport (e.g., SERT for serotonin, NET for norepinephrine).

Front: Mechanism of ABC (ATP-Binding Cassette) Transporters?

Back: Primary active transport using ATP (e.g., P-glycoprotein).

Front: Definition of "Signal Amplification" (The Cascade)?

Back: The process where one drug-receptor interaction activates multiple G-proteins, which in turn activate many more enzymes/second messengers.

Front: Function of Arrestin proteins?

Back: They bind to phosphorylated G-protein coupled receptors (GPCRs) to mediate receptor desensitization and internalization.

Section 41: Clinical & Regulatory Pharma (Lippincott)

Front: Definition of "Bioequivalence"?

Back: Two drug products show comparable bioavailability (rate and extent of absorption) when administered at the same dose under similar conditions.

Front: What is a "Black Box Warning"?

Back: The strictest warning put in the labeling of prescription drugs by the FDA when there is reasonable evidence of a serious hazard.

Front: Definition of an "Idiosyncratic Drug Reaction"?

Back: An unusual or unexpected drug response, often genetically determined, not related to the drug's known pharmacological mechanism.

Front: What is "Maintenance of Steady State"?

Back: Rate of administration = Rate of elimination.

Section 42: Autonomic Nuances (Katzung)

Front: What are "Auto-receptors"?

Back: Receptors located on the presynaptic terminal that respond to the neurotransmitter released by that same terminal (usually providing negative feedback, e.g., $\alpha_2$).

Front: What are "Hetero-receptors"?

Back: Presynaptic receptors that respond to neurotransmitters released from different neurons (e.g., ACh receptors on NE terminals).

Front: Mechanism of "Botulinum Toxin"?

Back: Cleaves SNARE proteins, preventing the fusion of ACh vesicles with the presynaptic membrane (inhibits ACh release).

Front: Mechanism of "Latrotoxin" (Black Widow Spider Venom)?

Back: Triggers massive, explosive release of Acetylcholine.

Front: Most common clinical use of Mirabegron?

Back: Overactive bladder ($\beta_3$ agonist).

Section 43: Advanced Toxicology & Safety (Lippincott/Katzung)

Front: Definition of "Therapeutic Index" ($TI$) vs. "Certain Safety Factor" ($CSF$)?

Back: $TI = TD_{50}/ED_{50}$; $CSF = LD_1/ED_{99}$ (CSF is a more conservative measure of safety).

Front: Side effect of "Chlorpromazine" on the eye?

Back: Corneal and lens deposits (Contrast with Thioridazine: Retinal deposits).

Front: Primary mechanism of "Vincristine" neurotoxicity?

Back: Inhibition of microtubule formation in axons.

Front: Classic drug-drug interaction between Sildenafil and Nitrates?

Back: Synergistic increase in $cGMP$ leading to severe, life-threatening hypotension.
=====================================================================
=====================================================================


General Microbiology:

Front: Bacterial cell wall component unique to Gram-positives?

Back: Teichoic acid (lipoteichoic acid induces TNF-$\alpha$ and IL-1).

Front: Function of the bacterial capsule?

Back: Antiphagocytic; protects against opsonization.

Front: Location of the bacterial "Endotoxin"?

Back: Outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria (Lipid A of LPS).

Front: Only bacteria with Polypeptide capsule (D-glutamate)?

Back: Bacillus anthracis.

Front: Bacteria that do not Gram stain well because they are intracellular?

Back: Legionella, Chlamydia, Rickettsia.

Front: Visualization method for Treponema pallidum?

Back: Dark-field microscopy or Fluorescent antibody stain.

Front: Media used to isolate N. gonorrhoeae?

Back: Thayer-Martin (VPN) agar.

Front: Media used for C. diphtheriae?

Back: Löffler medium or Tellurite agar.

Front: Metabolic requirement of H. influenzae?

Back: Factors V (NAD+) and X (Hematin).

Front: Function of Protein A in S. aureus?

Back: Binds Fc region of IgG; prevents opsonization and phagocytosis.

Front: Mechanism of Diphtheria toxin?

Back: Inactivation of EF-2 via ADP-ribosylation (inhibits protein synthesis).

Front: Mechanism of Cholera toxin?

Back: Permanently activates Gs, increasing cAMP, leading to Cl- and $H_2O$ secretion.

Front: Mechanism of Shiga toxin?

Back: Cleaves 28S rRNA (60S ribosome subunit); inhibits protein synthesis.

Front: Common characteristic of Clostridium species?

Back: Gram-positive, spore-forming, obligate anaerobic bacilli.

Front: Mechanism of Tetanospasmin?
