Front: Cause of "Pain Crisis" in Sickle Cell Disease?

Back: Vaso-occlusion (infarction) in small vessels due to sickled RBCs.

Front: Immediate treatment for Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT)?

Back: Stop Heparin immediately and start a Direct Thrombin Inhibitor (e.g., Argatroban).

Front: Lab marker for effective B12 replacement therapy?

Back: Significant increase in Reticulocyte count within 3-5 days.

Front: Most common cause of Vitamin K deficiency in newborns?

Back: Lack of gut flora and poor placental transfer (prevented by Vitamin K injection at birth).

Front: Marker for identifying "Blast" cells in Acute Leukemia?

Back: CD34 (marker of immaturity).

Front: Most common genetic mutation in Systemic Mastocytosis?

Back: KIT D816V mutation.

Front: Enzyme deficient in Congenital Erythropoietic Porphyria (Gunther Disease)?

Back: Uroporphyrinogen III cosynthase.

Front: Effect of Hemolysis on Serum LDH (Lactate Dehydrogenase) and 왜?

Back: LDH is significantly increased; it is an enzyme abundant inside RBCs released upon rupture.

Front: What is the "Ham Test" (Acidified Serum Test) historically used for?

Back: Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria (PNH) – superseded by flow cytometry for CD55/59.

Section 21: Clinical Morphology & Specific Smear Findings

Front: "Pencil Cells" on peripheral smear are characteristic of?

Back: Iron Deficiency Anemia.

Front: "Acanthocytes" (Spur Cells) are most commonly associated with?

Back: Liver disease or Abetalipoproteinemia.

Front: "Echinocytes" (Burr Cells) are most commonly associated with?

Back: Uremia (Renal Failure) or Pyruvate Kinase deficiency.

Front: "Degmacytes" is the technical term for which cell type?

Back: Bite Cells (seen in G6PD deficiency).

Front: What are "Cabot Rings" and when are they seen?

Back: Remnants of the mitotic spindle; seen in Megaloblastic Anemia or Lead Poisoning.

Section 22: Rare Coagulopathies & Vessel Disorders

Front: Mechanism of Hereditary Hemorrhagic Telangiectasia (Osler-Weber-Rendu)?

Back: Autosomal dominant disorder of blood vessels leading to telangiectasias and AVMs.

Front: Screening test for Factor XIII deficiency?

Back: 5-Molar Urea Solubility Test (Standard PT/PTT will be normal).

Front: Function of Protein S?

Back: Acts as a cofactor for Protein C to inactivate Factors Va and VIIIa.

Front: What is "Lupus Anticoagulant" paradox?

Back: It prolongs PTT in vitro but causes a hypercoagulable state (thrombosis) in vivo.

Front: Most common cause of "Acquired" Hemophilia?

Back: Autoantibodies against Factor VIII (often postpartum or malignancy-associated).

Section 23: The Spleen & Reticuloendothelial System

Front: What are "Pitting" and "Culling" in the spleen?

Back: Pitting: Removing inclusions (Heinz bodies) without destroying the cell. Culling: Removing damaged RBCs entirely.

Front: Immunological consequence of Splenectomy?

Back: Decreased IgM and decreased complement activation, leading to susceptibility to encapsulated organisms.

Front: The "Encapsulated Trio" to vaccinate against post-splenectomy?

Back: S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae type b, and N. meningitidis.

Front: Cause of "Gaucher Cells" (crinkled paper appearance) in the marrow?

Back: Glucocerebrosidase deficiency.

Section 24: Treatment-Specific High Yields

Front: Specific antidote for Dabigatran?

Back: Idarucizumab.

Front: Specific antidote for Factor Xa inhibitors (Rivaroxaban/Apixaban)?

Back: Andexanet alfa.

Front: Primary side effect of Desmopressin (DDAVP) when treating vWD?

Back: Hyponatremia (due to its ADH-like effect).

Front: What is "Leucovorin Rescue" used for?

Back: To reduce the toxicity of high-dose Methotrexate by providing a source of reduced folate.

Section 25: Quantitative Indices & Formulae

Front: Formula for Mean Corpuscular Hemoglobin Concentration (MCHC)?

Back: $MCHC = (Hemoglobin / Hematocrit) \times 100$.

Front: What condition is the ONLY one with an increased MCHC?

Back: Hereditary Spherocytosis (due to membrane loss and cellular dehydration).

Front: Corrected Reticulocyte Count formula?

Back: $Retics \% \times (Hct / 45)$.

Front: How many days does it take for a Reticulocyte to mature in the peripheral blood?

Back: Approximately 1 day.

Section 26: Differential Diagnoses (The "Final Boss" Cards)

Front: Differentiate "Iron Deficiency" vs "Anemia of Chronic Disease" via TIBC?

Back: Iron Def: TIBC is $\uparrow$. Chronic Disease: TIBC is $\downarrow$.

Front: Differentiate "Leukemoid Reaction" vs "CML" via Cytogenetics?

Back: CML will have $t(9;22)$; Leukemoid reaction will be cytogenetically normal.

Front: Differentiate "TTP" vs "HUS" in clinical presentation?

Back: HUS typically presents with bloody diarrhea (EHEC) and predominant renal failure; TTP has more neurological symptoms.

Front: Differentiate "Follicular Lymphoma" vs "Reactive Lymph Node Hyperplasia"?

Back: Follicular Lymphoma: BCL-2 (+), lacks tingible body macrophages, monoclonal.

Front: Differentiate "Multiple Myeloma" vs "Primary Amyloidosis" (AL)?

Back: MM is a plasma cell malignancy; AL is a protein folding disorder (misfolded light chains) often secondary to a plasma cell dyscrasia.

Front: Most common inherited cause of Sideroblastic Anemia?

Back: X-linked deficiency of ALAS2 (erythroid-specific $\delta$-aminolevulinic acid synthase).

Front: Direct effect of Lead on the heme pathway enzymes?

Back: Inhibits Ferrochelatase and ALA Dehydratase.

Front: Which enzyme deficiency is protected against P. falciparum malaria?

Back: G6PD deficiency and Pyruvate Kinase deficiency.

Front: Mechanism of Pyruvate Kinase (PK) deficiency?

Back: $\downarrow$ ATP $\rightarrow$ rigid RBCs $\rightarrow$ extravascular hemolysis in the splenic red pulp.

Front: Distinctive finding on peripheral smear for Pyruvate Kinase deficiency?

Back: Echinocytes (Burr cells) that are not related to uremia.

Section 28: Lymphoid and Myeloid Nuances

Front: What is the "TRAP" stain?

Back: Tartrate-Resistant Acid Phosphatase; used for Hairy Cell Leukemia.

Front: Typical immunophenotype for Hairy Cell Leukemia?

Back: CD11c, CD25, CD103.

Front: The "Sezary Syndrome" triad?

Back: Erythroderma, Lymphadenopathy, and circulating Sezary cells (cerebriform nuclei).

Front: Distinction between Myelofibrosis and CML on marrow biopsy?

Back: Myelofibrosis shows "collagen fibrosis" (Silver/Reticulin stain); CML is hypercellular with myeloid maturation.

Front: What is "Aleukemic Leukemia"?

Back: Leukemia where the abnormal cells are confined to the marrow; the peripheral WBC count is normal or low.

Section 29: Coagulation and Thrombosis "Deep Dives"

Front: Why is the "Bleeding Time" usually normal in Hemophilia?

Back: Bleeding time tests primary hemostasis (platelets); Hemophilia is a defect in secondary hemostasis (clotting factors).

Front: Mechanism of Tranexamic Acid (TXA)?

Back: Antifibrinolytic; inhibits Plasminogen activation to Plasmin.

Front: What is "Glucanzmann Thrombasthenia" vs "Bernard-Soulier" on Ristocetin test?

Back: Bernard-Soulier: No agglutination with Ristocetin. Glanzmann: Normal agglutination with Ristocetin (but fails with ADP/Collagen).

Front: What is the "Russell Viper Venom Time" (dRVVT) used for?

Back: To confirm the presence of Lupus Anticoagulant (Antiphospholipid Syndrome).

Section 30: Hemoglobin Variants and Advanced Physiology

Front: What is "Hemoglobin Barts"?

Back: $\gamma_4$ (four gamma chains); seen in 4-gene deletion $\alpha$-Thalassemia (Hydrops Fetalis).

Front: What is "Hemoglobin H"?

Back: $\beta_4$ (four beta chains); seen in 3-gene deletion $\alpha$-Thalassemia.

Front: Effect of "Acidosis" on the P50 of the Oxygen-Hb curve?

Back: Increases the P50 (shifts curve to the right, meaning lower affinity).

Front: What is the "P50"?

Back: The partial pressure of oxygen at which hemoglobin is 50% saturated.

Front: Which Hemoglobin variant is most "Polymerization-prone"?

Back: Deoxygenated HbS.

Section 31: Therapeutic Monitoring and Toxicology

Front: Why is Cimetidine or Sulfa drugs dangerous for G6PD patients?

Back: They induce oxidative stress, leading to Hemolysis.

Front: Treatment of choice for "Iron Overload" (Hemochromatosis)?

Back: Therapeutic phlebotomy (or Deferoxamine/Deferasirox chelation if phlebotomy is contraindicated).

Front: Common side effect of EPO (Erythropoietin) therapy?

Back: Hypertension and increased risk of thromboembolic events.

Front: What is the "D-dimer" specifically measuring?

Back: Fibrin degradation products that have been cross-linked by Factor XIII.

Section 32: Clinical "Associations" (Rapid Fire)

Front: Association: Thymoma?

Back: Pure Red Cell Aplasia (PRCA) or Myasthenia Gravis.

Front: Association: "Doughnut-shaped" nuclei in neutrophils?

Back: Pseudo-Pelger-Huet anomaly (Myelodysplastic Syndrome).

Front: Association: "Hyper-segmented" neutrophils + Glossitis?

Back: Megaloblastic Anemia (B12 or Folate).

Front: Association: Pruritus after a hot shower?

Back: Polycythemia Vera (Mast cell degranulation).


=====================================================================
=====================================================================


Endocrine Pathology:

Front: Most common cause of hyperpituitarism?
Back: Adenoma arising in the anterior lobe.

Front: Functional vs. Non-functional pituitary adenoma definition?
Back: Functional secretes hormones; Non-functional does not (often presents with mass effect).

Front: Most common type of hyperfunctioning pituitary adenoma?
Back: Prolactinoma.

Front: Clinical manifestations of Prolactinoma in females?
Back: Amenorrhea, galactorrhea, and infertility.

Front: Effect of Prolactinoma on males?
Back: Decreased libido and erectile dysfunction.

Front: Consequence of Somatotroph Adenoma before epiphyses close?
Back: Gigantism.

Front: Consequence of Somatotroph Adenoma after epiphyses close?
Back: Acromegaly.

Front: Best screening test for Acromegaly?
Back: Serum IGF-1 (Insulin-like Growth Factor-1).

Front: Definitive diagnostic test for Acromegaly?
Back: Oral glucose suppression test (failure to suppress Growth Hormone).

Front: Cause of Nelson Syndrome?
Back: Rapid enlargement of a pre-existing ACTH-producing adenoma after bilateral adrenalectomy.

Front: Common symptoms of pituitary mass effect?
Back: Bitemporal hemianopsia and headache.

Front: Definition of Sheehan Syndrome?
Back: Ischemic necrosis of the anterior pituitary following postpartum hemorrhage.

Front: Classic presentation of Sheehan Syndrome?
Back: Failure to lactate and loss of pubic hair.

Front: Definition of Empty Sella Syndrome?
Back: Herniation of the arachnoid and CSF into the sella, compressing the pituitary.

Front: Posterior pituitary: Central Diabetes Insipidus (DI) cause?
Back: ADH deficiency.

Front: Diagnostic finding in Diabetes Insipidus?
Back: Low urine specific gravity and high serum osmolality.

Front: Primary cause of SIADH?
Back: Ectopic ADH secretion (often Small Cell Carcinoma of the lung).

Front: Electrolyte hallmark of SIADH?
Back: Hyponatremia.

Thyroid Gland: Hyper & Hypothyroidism
Front: Most common cause of hyperthyroidism (Thyrotoxicosis)?
Back: Graves Disease.

Front: Classic triad of Graves Disease?
Back: Hyperthyroidism, exophthalmos, and pretibial myxedema.

Front: Antibody found in Graves Disease?
Back: Thyroid Stimulating Immunoglobulin (TSI) - IgG antibody.

Front: Most common cause of hypothyroidism in iodine-sufficient areas?
Back: Hashimoto Thyroiditis.

Front: Histological hallmark of Hashimoto Thyroiditis?
Back: Intense lymphocytic infiltrate with germinal centers and Hurthle cells.

Front: Antibodies associated with Hashimoto?
Back: Anti-thyroid peroxidase (anti-TPO) and anti-thyroglobulin.

Front: Definition of De Quervain (Subacute) Thyroiditis?
Back: Granulomatous thyroiditis often following a viral infection.

Front: Clinical hallmark of De Quervain Thyroiditis?
Back: Painful, tender thyroid gland.

Front: Definition of Riedel Thyroiditis?
Back: Extensive fibrosis of the thyroid that may extend to adjacent neck structures.

Front: Physical exam finding in Riedel Thyroiditis?
Back: "Rock-hard," painless thyroid.

Front: Definition of Cretinism?
Back: Hypothyroidism in infancy or early childhood causing mental retardation and short stature.

Front: Definition of Myxedema?
Back: Hypothyroidism in older children or adults.

Thyroid Neoplasia
Front: Most common type of thyroid cancer?
Back: Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: Key nuclear features of Papillary Carcinoma?
Back: Orphan Annie eye nuclei and nuclear grooves.

Front: Characteristic calcified structures in Papillary Carcinoma?
Back: Psammoma bodies.

Front: Distinguishing feature between Follicular Adenoma and Carcinoma?
Back: Capsular or vascular invasion (requires histological exam).

Front: Genetic mutation associated with Follicular Carcinoma?
Back: RAS mutation or PAX8-PPARG translocation.

Front: Cell of origin for Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma?
Back: Parafollicular C cells.

Front: Hormone secreted by Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma?
Back: Calcitonin.

Front: Staining characteristic of Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma?
Back: Amyloid stroma staining with Congo Red.

Front: Genetic association with Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma?
Back: RET proto-oncogene (MEN 2A and 2B).

Front: Characteristics of Anaplastic Thyroid Carcinoma?
Back: Highly aggressive, occurs in elderly, rapidly enlarging neck mass.

Parathyroid Glands
Front: Most common cause of Primary Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: Solitary parathyroid adenoma.

Front: Classic "Bones, Stones, Groans, Moans" refers to?
Back: Hypercalcemia symptoms in Primary Hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Bone lesion characteristic of Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: Osteitis fibrosa cystica (brown tumors).

Front: Most common cause of Secondary Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: Chronic Renal Failure.

Front: Laboratory findings in Primary Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: High PTH, high Calcium, low Phosphate.

Front: Physical sign of Hypocalcemia: Chvostek sign?
Back: Facial nerve tapping leads to facial twitching.

Front: Physical sign of Hypocalcemia: Trousseau sign?
Back: Carpal spasm after inflating a BP cuff.

Front: Lab findings in Hypoparathyroidism?
Back: Low PTH, low Calcium, high Phosphate.

Pancreas: Diabetes Mellitus
Front: Pathophysiology of Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus?
Back: Autoimmune destruction of Beta cells leading to absolute insulin deficiency.

Front: HLA associations for Type 1 DM?
Back: HLA-DR3 and HLA-DR4.

Front: Primary pathophysiology of Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus?
Back: Insulin resistance and relative insulin deficiency.

Front: Most common cause of death in Diabetics?
Back: Myocardial Infarction (Macrovascular disease).

Front: Leading cause of acquired blindness in the US?
Back: Diabetic Retinopathy.

Front: Classic histology of the kidney in Diabetes?
Back: Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodules (nodular glomerulosclerosis).

Front: Metabolic emergency in Type 1 DM?
Back: Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA).

Front: Metabolic emergency in Type 2 DM?
Back: Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS).

Front: Triad of Insulinoma (Whipple Triad)?
Back: Hypoglycemia symptoms, low blood glucose, and relief of symptoms with glucose.

Front: Clinical syndrome of Gastrinoma (Zollinger-Ellison)?
Back: Peptic ulcers and diarrhea.

Adrenal Cortex: Hyperfunction
Front: Definition of Cushing Syndrome?
Back: Hypercortisolism from any cause.

Front: Definition of Cushing Disease?
Back: Hypercortisolism due to an ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma.

Front: Physical features of Cushing Syndrome?
Back: Moon facies, buffalo hump, and abdominal striae.

Front: Primary Hyperaldosteronism (Conn Syndrome) cause?
Back: Adrenal adenoma or bilateral adrenal hyperplasia.

Front: Clinical hallmark of Conn Syndrome?
Back: Hypertension and hypokalemia with low renin.

Front: Most common enzyme deficiency in Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH)?
Back: 21-hydroxylase deficiency.

Front: Clinical presentation of 21-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: Salt wasting, hypotension, and virilization.

Front: Lab finding in 21-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: Increased 17-hydroxyprogesterone.

Adrenal Insufficiency & Medulla
Front: Definition of Addison Disease?
Back: Chronic primary adrenocortical insufficiency.

Front: Most common cause of Addison Disease in developed countries?
Back: Autoimmune adrenalitis.

Front: Most common cause of Addison Disease worldwide?
Back: Tuberculosis.

Front: Physical hallmark of Primary Adrenal Insufficiency?
Back: Hyperpigmentation (due to increased ACTH/MSM).

Front: Definition of Waterhouse-Friderichsen Syndrome?
Back: Massive bilateral adrenal hemorrhage due to Neisseria meningitidis sepsis.

Front: Origin of Pheochromocytoma?
Back: Chromaffin cells of the adrenal medulla.

Front: Rule of 10s for Pheochromocytoma?
Back: 10% bilateral, 10% malignant, 10% extra-adrenal, 10% in children.

Front: Urinary metabolites for diagnosing Pheochromocytoma?
Back: Vanillylmandelic acid (VMA) and metanephrines.

Front: Classic symptom triad of Pheochromocytoma?
Back: Episodic headache, sweating, and palpitations.

Front: Most common extracranial solid tumor of childhood?
Back: Neuroblastoma.

Front: Genetic marker for poor prognosis in Neuroblastoma?
Back: N-myc amplification.

Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia (MEN)
Front: Components of MEN 1 (Wermer Syndrome)?
Back: Pituitary, Parathyroid, and Pancreatic tumors (3 Ps).

Front: Components of MEN 2A (Sipple Syndrome)?
Back: Medullary thyroid carcinoma, Pheochromocytoma, and Parathyroid hyperplasia.

Front: Components of MEN 2B?
Back: Medullary thyroid carcinoma, Pheochromocytoma, and Mucosal neuromas/Marfanoid habitus.

High-Yield Histology & Markers
Front: Histological finding in Diabetic islet cells (Type 2)?
Back: Amyloid deposition (Amylin).

Front: Immunohistochemical marker for Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma?
Back: Calcitonin or Chromogranin.

Front: Adrenal finding in Chronic Exogenous Glucocorticoid use?
Back: Bilateral cortical atrophy.

Front: Adrenal finding in Cushing Disease (Pituitary)?
Back: Bilateral cortical hyperplasia.

Front: Characteristic cells in Neuroblastoma?
Back: Homer-Wright rosettes.

Front: "Grape-like" clusters in pituitary?
Back: Potential description of Rathke Cleft Cyst.

Front: Significance of Crooke Hyaline Change?
Back: Replacement of cytoplasm in ACTH cells with keratin in response to high cortisol.

Front: Thyroid condition associated with increased risk of B-cell lymphoma?
Back: Hashimoto Thyroiditis.

Front: Marker for Neuroendocrine tumors?
Back: Synaptophysin and Chromogranin A.

Front: Primary mediator of bone resorption in Malignancy-associated hypercalcemia?
Back: PTHrP (PTH-related protein).

Front: Key difference between MEN 2A and 2B?
Back: 2A has Parathyroid involvement; 2B has Neuromas and Marfanoid habitus.

Front: Mechanism of Methimazole/PTU?
Back: Inhibition of thyroid peroxidase (TPO).

Front: Effect of Amiodarone on the thyroid?
Back: Can cause either hyperthyroidism or hypothyroidism (due to high iodine content).

Front: Most common cause of Thyroid Storm?
Back: Stress, infection, or surgery in a patient with untreated hyperthyroidism.

Front: Definition of Jod-Basedow phenomenon?
Back: Iodine-induced hyperthyroidism in a patient with a multinodular goiter.

Front: Definition of Wolff-Chaikoff effect?
Back: High iodine levels inhibiting thyroid hormone synthesis (protective).

Front: Ectopic thyroid tissue location?
Back: Base of the tongue (Lingual thyroid).

Front: Remnant of the descent of the thyroid gland?
Back: Thyroglossal duct cyst (midline neck mass).

Front: Branchial cleft cyst location vs. Thyroglossal?
Back: Lateral neck vs. Midline.

Front: Relationship between Calcium and Albumin?
Back: Total calcium drops by 0.8 for every 1.0 decrease in albumin.

Front: Psammoma body mnemonics (PSaMMoma)?
Back: Papillary thyroid, Serous ovary, Meningioma, Mesothelioma.

Front: Primary location of the "Sertoli-cell only" equivalent in endocrine?
Back: Not applicable, but used to differentiate primary vs secondary failure.

Front: Treatment for Prolactinoma?
Back: Dopamine agonists (Cabergoline, Bromocriptine).

Front: Relationship between Prolactin and GnRH?
Back: Prolactin inhibits GnRH secretion.

Front: Most common cause of hypopituitarism in adults?
Back: Non-secreting pituitary adenoma (compression).

Front: Most common cause of hypopituitarism in children?
Back: Craniopharyngioma.

Front: Origin of Craniopharyngioma?
Back: Rathke pouch remnants.

Front: Classic imaging appearance of Craniopharyngioma?
Back: Calcified, cystic mass with "machinery oil" fluid.

Front: Diagnosis for patient with hypercalcemia and low urinary calcium?
Back: Familial Hypocalciuric Hypercalcemia (FHH).

Front: Defect in FHH?
Back: Defective G-protein coupled calcium-sensing receptors (CaSR).

Front: Effect of 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: Hypertension (due to 11-deoxycorticosterone) and virilization.

Front: Effect of 17-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: Hypertension and primary amenorrhea (low sex hormones).

Front: Source of Aldosterone?
Back: Zona Glomerulosa.

Front: Source of Cortisol?
Back: Zona Fasciculata.

Front: Source of Androgens?
Back: Zona Reticularis.

Front: Mnemonic for Adrenal layers (G-F-R)?
Back: Salt (Glomerulosa), Sugar (Fasciculata), Sex (Reticularis).

Front: Major side effect of Spironolactone in males?
Back: Gynecomastia.

Front: Mechanism of Graves exophthalmos?
Back: Fibroblast proliferation and GAG deposition triggered by TSH-receptor antibodies.

Front: Effect of pregnancy on Total T4?
Back: Increases due to increased Thyroid Binding Globulin (TBG).

Front: Best indicator of thyroid function status?
Back: TSH level.

Front: Precursor of ACTH and MSH?
Back: Pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC).

Front: Histological appearance of Pituitary Adenoma?
Back: Monomorphism (cells look identical) and absence of a reticulin network.

Front: Difference between Pituitary Adenoma and Hyperplasia?
Back: Adenomas lack reticulin; Hyperplasia preserves the reticulin framework.

Front: Most common cause of Pituitary Apoplexy?
Back: Sudden hemorrhage into a pre-existing pituitary adenoma.

Front: Classic presentation of Pituitary Apoplexy?
Back: Sudden "thunderclap" headache, visual loss, and cardiovascular collapse.

Front: Most common location of ectopic ACTH production?
Back: Small cell carcinoma of the lung or bronchial carcinoids.

Front: Distinction between Central and Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus via Desmopressin?
Back: Central DI responds to Desmopressin (urine osmolality increases); Nephrogenic does not.

Thyroid & Parathyroid Fine Points
Front: Mechanism of the "Cold Nodule" on thyroid scan?
Back: Area of thyroid that does not take up radioactive iodine; higher risk for malignancy.

Front: Significance of the BRAF mutation in thyroid cancer?
Back: Strongly associated with Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma and poor prognosis.

Front: Histological feature of Graves Disease?
Back: Tall, columnar follicular cells with "scalloping" of the colloid.

Front: Cause of Goiter in Iodine Deficiency?
Back: Low T4 leads to high TSH, which causes hypertrophy and hyperplasia of follicular cells.

Front: Definition of Plummar Syndrome?
Back: Toxic Multinodular Goiter (hyperfunctioning nodules independent of TSH).

Front: Difference between Toxic Multinodular Goiter and Graves?
Back: Toxic MNG lacks exophthalmos and pretibial myxedema.

Front: Most common cause of Hypocalcemia (overall)?
Back: Hypoalbuminemia (though "true" ionized calcium remains normal).

Front: Primary lab finding in Pseudohypoparathyroidism?
Back: High PTH but low Calcium (due to end-organ resistance to PTH).

Front: Genetic defect in Albright Hereditary Osteodystrophy?
Back: Defective Gs-protein alpha subunit (GNAS1 mutation).

Front: Physical phenotype of Albright Hereditary Osteodystrophy?
Back: Short stature and shortened 4th/5th metacarpals.

Adrenal Cortex & Medulla Nuances
Front: Impact of chronic Ketoconazole use on the Adrenal gland?
Back: Inhibits desmolase/steroid synthesis, leading to adrenal insufficiency.

Front: High-dose Dexamethasone suppression test: No suppression of ACTH/Cortisol?
Back: Suggests Ectopic ACTH production (e.g., Small Cell Lung Cancer).

Front: High-dose Dexamethasone suppression test: Suppression of Cortisol?
Back: Confirms Cushing Disease (Pituitary Adenoma).

Front: Cause of Primary Pigmented Nodular Adrenocortical Disease?
Back: Part of Carney Complex (PRKAR1A mutation).

Front: Spironolactone vs. Eplerenone in Conn Syndrome?
Back: Eplerenone is more selective and lacks the anti-androgenic side effects (gynecomastia).

Front: Diagnostic use of MIBG scan?
Back: Localization of extra-adrenal Pheochromocytomas (Paragangliomas).

Front: Pre-operative management of Pheochromocytoma?
Back: Alpha-blockade (Phenoxybenzamine) followed by Beta-blockade.

Front: Why must Alpha-blockade precede Beta-blockade in Pheochromocytoma?
Back: To prevent hypertensive crisis from unopposed alpha-1 stimulation.

Diabetes & Pancreatic Neuroendocrine Tumors
Front: Common HLA alleles for Type 1 DM?
Back: HLA-DR3 and HLA-DR4.

Front: Histological finding in early Type 1 DM islets?
Back: Insulitis (lymphocytic infiltration).

Front: Pathogenesis of Non-enzymatic Glycosylation (NEG) in Diabetes?
Back: Glucose attaches to proteins, forming Advanced Glycation End-products (AGEs).

Front: Mechanism of Sorbitol damage in Diabetics?
Back: Glucose converted to Sorbitol via Aldose Reductase, causing osmotic swelling in Schwann cells and Lens.

Front: Classic presentation of Glucagonoma?
Back: Necrolytic migratory erythema (rash), diabetes, and anemia.

Front: Presentation of Somatostatinoma?
Back: Diabetes, steatorrhea, and gallstones (due to inhibition of CCK and insulin).

Front: Clinical syndrome of VIPoma?
Back: WDHA Syndrome: Watery Diarrhea, Hypokalemia, Achlorhydria.

Systemic Endocrine & MEN Syndromes
Front: Definition of Carcinoid Syndrome?
Back: Flushing, diarrhea, and right-sided heart valve lesions.

Front: Why are Carcinoid heart lesions only right-sided?
Back: Lung MAO (Monoamine Oxidase) metabolizes serotonin before it reaches the left heart.

Front: Urinary marker for Carcinoid Syndrome?
Back: 5-HIAA (5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid).

Front: Common trigger for Carcinoid Syndrome symptoms?
Back: Alcohol or emotional stress causing hormone release.

Front: Association of "Brown Tumors"?
Back: Osteitis fibrosa cystica in Hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Liddle Syndrome mimic?
Back: Hyperaldosteronism presentation but with low Aldosterone/Renin (gain of function of ENaC).

Front: Syndrome of Apparent Mineralocorticoid Excess (AME) cause?
Back: Deficiency of 11-beta-HSD2 (often from licorice), allowing Cortisol to activate Mineralocorticoid receptors.

Front: Difference between MEN 2A and 2B?
Back: 2A includes Hyperparathyroidism; 2B includes Mucosal Neuromas and Marfanoid habitus.

Front: Common genetic screening for families of MEN 2 patients?
Back: Testing for RET proto-oncogene mutations.

Front: Prophylactic treatment for RET mutation carriers in MEN 2?
Back: Prophylactic thyroidectomy (due to 100% risk of Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma).

Front: Histological characteristic of an Adrenal Adenoma?
Back: Well-circumscribed yellow mass with cells resembling the normal zona fasciculata.

Front: Most common site of ectopic adrenal tissue?
Back: Along the path of gonadal descent (e.g., retroperitoneum, ovaries, testes).

Front: Histological appearance of Adrenocortical Carcinoma?
Back: Large, invasive mass with areas of necrosis, hemorrhage, and cystic change.

Front: Distinction between Adrenal Adenoma and Carcinoma?
Back: Carcinomas are typically larger (>100g), show vascular invasion, and have higher mitotic rates.

Front: Effect of 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency on Renin levels?
Back: Low Renin (due to hypertension caused by 11-deoxycorticosterone).

Front: Underlying cause of Adrenal Crisis in Addison patients?
Back: Any form of acute stress (infection, trauma, surgery) that increases cortisol demand.

Front: "Zellballen" pattern on histology is characteristic of?
Back: Pheochromocytoma (nests of cells surrounded by vascular stroma).

Front: Immunohistochemical stain positive in Pheochromocytoma?
Back: Chromogranin and Synaptophysin.

Front: S100 stain utility in Pheochromocytoma?
Back: Highlights the sustenacular cells surrounding the Zellballen nests.

Thyroid & Parathyroid: Nuance & Morphology
Front: Definition of the "Tall Cell Variant" of Papillary Carcinoma?
Back: Aggressive variant with height of cells at least twice their width and abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm.

Front: Significance of the RET/PTC rearrangement?
Back: Genetic driver commonly found in Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: Follicular Thyroid Carcinoma spread pattern?
Back: Hematogenous (unlike Papillary, which is primarily lymphatic).

Front: Pathogenesis of Secondary Hyperparathyroidism in Renal Failure?
Back: Hyperphosphatemia leads to Hypocalcemia, which chronically stimulates PTH secretion.

Front: Tertiary Hyperparathyroidism definition?
Back: Autonomous PTH secretion following long-standing secondary hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Major cause of "Hungry Bone Syndrome"?
Back: Sudden drop in PTH after parathyroidectomy, causing rapid calcium uptake by bones.

Front: Result of PTH on Renal Phosphorus handling?
Back: Decreases phosphate reabsorption in the proximal convoluted tubule (Phosphate Trashing).

Front: Histology of Parathyroid Adenoma?
Back: Predominantly chief cells with a rim of normal parathyroid tissue and absence of stromal fat.

Pancreatic Islets & Neuroendocrine Nuances
Front: Relationship between Obesity and Insulin Resistance?
Back: Increased adipokines (e.g., Resistin) and free fatty acids interfere with insulin signaling.

Front: Primary cause of Polyuria in Diabetes?
Back: Osmotic diuresis resulting from glucosuria.

Front: Diagnostic threshold for HbA1c in Diabetes Mellitus?
Back: Greater than or equal to 6.5%.

Front: Definition of "Insular" amyloidosis?
Back: Deposition of Amylin (IAPP) in the islets, characteristic of Type 2 DM.

Front: Most common location of a Gastrinoma?
Back: The "Gastrinoma Triangle" (Duodenum, pancreas, and porta hepatis).

Front: Clinical finding in VIPoma (WDHA Syndrome)?
Back: Watery Diarrhea, Hypokalemia, and Achlorhydria.

Pituitary & Hypothalamic Specifics
Front: Hormone deficiency sequence in Progressive Hypopituitarism?
Back: GH is usually lost first, followed by LH/FSH, then TSH and ACTH.

Front: Definition of Kallmann Syndrome?
Back: Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism with anosmia due to failure of GnRH neurons to migrate.

Front: Effect of Craniopharyngioma on the Pituitary Stalk?
Back: Compression can cause Hyperprolactinemia (loss of dopamine inhibition).

Front: Histology of "Adamantinomatous" Craniopharyngioma?
Back: Squamous epithelium, "wet" keratin, and calcifications.

Integrated Endocrine & Rare Syndromes
Front: Definition of Autoimmune Polyendocrine Syndrome Type 1 (APS-1)?
Back: Triad of Hypoparathyroidism, Adrenal insufficiency, and Chronic Mucocutaneous Candidiasis.

Front: Genetic defect in APS-1?
Back: Mutation in the AIRE (Autoimmune Regulator) gene.

Front: Major endocrine risk in patients with Cowden Syndrome?
Back: Increased risk of Follicular Thyroid Carcinoma (PTEN mutation).

Front: McCune-Albright Syndrome endocrine manifestations?
Back: Precocious puberty, hyperthyroidism, and GH-secreting adenomas.

Front: Physical triad of McCune-Albright Syndrome?
Back: Polyostotic fibrous dysplasia, Café-au-lait spots, and autonomous endocrine hyperfunction.

Front: Metabolic cause of "Thyroidization" of the kidney?
Back: Chronic Pyelonephritis (islets of thyroid-like tissue in renal parenchyma; distinct from thyroid pathology).

Front: Difference between "Primary" and "Secondary" Hyperaldosteronism Renin levels?
Back: Primary has Low Renin; Secondary has High Renin.

Front: Cause of Secondary Hyperaldosteronism?
Back: Decreased renal perfusion (e.g., Renal Artery Stenosis, Heart Failure).

High-Yield Laboratory Interferences
Front: Effect of high Biotin intake on Thyroid Function Tests?
Back: Can falsely elevate T4/T3 and falsely lower TSH.

Front: Why is C-peptide useful in Hypoglycemia workup?
Back: Distinguishes endogenous insulin production (high C-peptide) from exogenous insulin injection (low C-peptide).

Front: Lab profile of Factitious Hyperthyroidism?
Back: Low TSH, High T4, and Low Thyroglobulin (due to exogenous ingestion).

Front: Most common cause of a "Thyroid Storm"?
Back: Acute stress (infection, surgery, or trauma) in a patient with pre-existing Graves disease.

Front: Effect of Pregnancy on Total T4 vs. Free T4?
Back: Total T4 increases (due to high TBG), but Free T4 remains normal (eustates).

Front: Significance of "Cold" vs "Hot" nodules on RAIU scan?
Back: Cold nodules are non-functional and more likely to be malignant; Hot nodules are functional and usually benign.

Front: Histological feature of a Hurthle Cell?
Back: Large follicular cell with abundant, granular, eosinophilic cytoplasm and numerous mitochondria.

Front: Condition where Hurthle Cells are most prominently seen?
Back: Hashimoto Thyroiditis and Hurthle Cell Adenoma/Carcinoma.

Front: Most common location for an ectopic Parathyroid gland?
Back: Within the Thymus (due to shared origin from the 3rd pharyngeal pouch).

Front: Pathogenesis of Calciphylaxis?
Back: Systemic calcification of small vessels leading to skin necrosis, often seen in end-stage renal disease/secondary hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Difference between Osteitis Fibrosa Cystica and Renal Osteodystrophy?
Back: Osteitis Fibrosa Cystica is specific to high PTH; Renal Osteodystrophy is the broader bone disease seen in chronic kidney failure.

Adrenal & Pituitary: Advanced Diagnostics
Front: Mechanism of the Metyrapone stimulation test?
Back: Blocks 11-beta-hydroxylase; a normal response is an increase in ACTH and 11-deoxycortisol.

Front: Utility of the Metyrapone test?
Back: To evaluate the integrity of the entire Hypothalamic-Pituitary-Adrenal (HPA) axis.

Front: Histological finding in the pituitary of a patient with Cushing Syndrome?
Back: Crooke Hyaline Change (accumulation of intermediate filaments in ACTH-producing cells).

Front: Primary difference between Lymphocytic Hypophysitis and Pituitary Adenoma?
Back: Lymphocytic Hypophysitis is an autoimmune inflammation (often postpartum) that can mimic a mass on MRI.

Front: Genetic mutation associated with MEN 1?
Back: Mutation in the MENIN tumor suppressor gene.

Front: Most common pituitary tumor in MEN 1?
Back: Prolactinoma.

Front: Relationship between Pituitary Gigantism and the Cardiovascular system?
Back: Often leads to heart failure due to concentric left ventricular hypertrophy and cardiomyopathy.

Metabolic & Pancreatic: The "Grey Zones"
Front: Mechanism of "Amyloidosis" in Type 2 Diabetes?
Back: Co-secretion of Islet Amyloid Polypeptide (Amylin) with insulin leads to its deposition in islets.

Front: Distinction between Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) and Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS)?
Back: DKA (Type 1) has ketones and acidosis; HHS (Type 2) has extreme dehydration and higher glucose (>600 mg/dL).

Front: Primary reason for the "Dawn Phenomenon" in Diabetics?
Back: Early morning rise in blood sugar due to the physiological surge of Growth Hormone and Cortisol.

Front: Definition of the "Somogyi Effect"?
Back: Rebound hyperglycemia in the morning in response to an undiagnosed hypoglycemic episode during the night.

Front: Diagnostic use of the Glucagon Stimulation Test?
Back: Used to diagnose Pheochromocytoma (triggers catecholamine release) or growth hormone deficiency.

Front: Hormone responsible for the "Incretin Effect"?
Back: GLP-1 (Glucagon-like peptide-1) and GIP, which increase insulin secretion after oral glucose.

Neuroendocrine & Rare Syndromes
Front: Definition of Paraganglioma?
Back: A tumor of the extra-adrenal sympathetic or parasympathetic ganglia (the "extra-adrenal pheochromocytoma").

Front: Most common location for a Paraganglioma?
Back: The Organ of Zuckerkandl (near the origin of the inferior mesenteric artery).

Front: Components of Carney Complex?
Back: Myxomas (heart/skin), hyperpigmentation (lentigines), and endocrine overactivity (adrenal/pituitary).

Front: Characteristic of Von Hippel-Lindau (VHL) syndrome in Endocrine?
Back: High risk for Pheochromocytoma (often bilateral).

Front: Difference in Pheochromocytoma risk between MEN 2A and 2B?
Back: The risk is roughly equal (~50%), but 2B presents with a more aggressive thyroid component.

Front: Histological "Salt and Pepper" chromatin is a classic description of?
Back: Neuroendocrine tumors (e.g., Carcinoid, Pancreatic NETs, Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma).

Front: Most common hormone secreted by a Pancreatic Neuroendocrine Tumor (PanNET)?
Back: Insulin (Insulinoma).

Front: What is the "Rule of 10s" for Insulinomas?
Back: Unlike Pheochromocytoma, only 10% of Insulinomas are malignant (most are benign).

Front: Most common cause of death in patients with Acromegaly?
Back: Heart failure (due to dilated cardiomyopathy).

Front: Mechanism of hypercalcemia in Sarcoidosis?
Back: 1-alpha-hydroxylase activity in granulomatous macrophages converts Vitamin D to its active form (1,25-OH).

Front: Histological appearance of the thyroid in Riedel Thyroiditis?
Back: Dense hyalinized fibrosis and a chronic inflammatory infiltrate.

Front: Effect of chronic Lithium use on the thyroid?
Back: Inhibition of thyroid hormone release, commonly causing hypothyroidism.

Front: Definition of the "Pendred Syndrome"?
Back: Genetic disorder causing sensorineural hearing loss and goiter due to iodine transport defects.

Front: Primary difference in laboratory findings between 21-hydroxylase and 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: 21-hydroxylase deficiency presents with hypotension; 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency presents with hypertension.

Front: Why does 17-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency cause hypertension?
Back: Accumulation of 11-deoxycorticosterone, which has mineralocorticoid activity.

Front: Most common cause of primary adrenal insufficiency in children?
Back: Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH).

Front: Distinguishing feature of an Adrenal Myelolipoma?
Back: A benign, non-functioning tumor composed of mature adipose tissue and hematopoietic elements.

Pediatric & Developmental Endocrine Pathology
Front: Presentation of a Lingual Thyroid?
Back: A mass at the base of the tongue; may be the only functional thyroid tissue present.

Front: Most common cause of congenital hypothyroidism worldwide?
Back: Thyroid dysgenesis (aplasia, hypoplasia, or ectopic gland).

Front: Effect of maternal gestational diabetes on the newborn?
Back: Islet cell hyperplasia in the fetus leading to neonatal hypoglycemia after birth.

Front: Classic cardiac defect associated with infants of diabetic mothers?
Back: Transposition of the Great Arteries or Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (septal hypertrophy).

Front: What is the "Water-Clear Cell" hyperplasia?
Back: A rare form of parathyroid hyperplasia where cells have abundant clear cytoplasm.

Finishing Touches: "Buzzword" Review
Front: "Psammoma bodies" in the thyroid?
Back: Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: "Amyloid stroma" in the thyroid?
Back: Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: "Orphan Annie Eye" nuclei?
Back: Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: "Rock-hard, fixed thyroid"?
Back: Riedel Thyroiditis (or Anaplastic Carcinoma, but Riedel is the classic "hard" association).

Front: "Pot-bellied, pale, puffy-faced infant with a protruding tongue"?
Back: Cretinism (Congenital Hypothyroidism).

Front: "Scalloped colloid"?
Back: Graves Disease.

Front: "Hurthle cells and lymphoid follicles with germinal centers"?
Back: Hashimoto Thyroiditis.

Front: "Zellballen" nests?
Back: Pheochromocytoma.

Front: "Homer-Wright Rosettes"?
Back: Neuroblastoma.

Front: "Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodules"?
Back: Diabetic Nephropathy.

=====================================================================
=====================================================================


Systemic Pharmacology:

Front: Primary neurotransmitter released by all preganglionic autonomic fibers?

Back: Acetylcholine (ACh).

Front: Mechanism of action of Botulinum toxin at the neuromuscular junction?

Back: Cleaves SNARE proteins to prevent ACh vesicle fusion and release.

Front: Effect of low-dose Dopamine on renal blood flow?

Back: Increases blood flow via D1 receptor-mediated vasodilation.

Front: Classic triad of Atropine overdose?

Back: "Mad as a hatter (delirium), red as a beet (flushing), dry as a bone (anhidrosis)."

Front: Preferred treatment for Pheochromocytoma prior to surgical resection?

Back: Non-selective alpha-blockers (e.g., Phenoxybenzamine) followed by beta-blockers.

Front: Reason why Beta-blockers are used in Hyperthyroidism/Thyroid storm?

Back: To decrease heart rate and inhibit peripheral conversion of T4 to T3.

Front: Pathophysiological indication for Bethanechol?

Back: Postoperative ileus and non-obstructive urinary retention.

Front: Antidote for Organophosphate (cholinesterase inhibitor) poisoning?

Back: Atropine (muscarinic antagonist) and Pralidoxime (AChE regenerator).

Cardiovascular Pharmacology

Front: Primary mechanism of action of Digoxin on cardiac myocytes?

Back: Inhibition of $Na^+/K^+-ATPase$ pump, leading to increased intracellular $Ca^{2+}$.

Front: Drug of choice for paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia (PSVT) termination?

Back: Adenosine.

Front: Common side effect of ACE inhibitors caused by increased Bradykinin?

Back: Dry, non-productive cough.

Front: Mechanism of Aliskiren?

Back: Direct Renin inhibitor.

Front: Diuretic class that causes "Ototoxicity" as a side effect?

Back: Loop Diuretics (e.g., Furosemide).

Front: Primary metabolic side effect of Spironolactone?

Back: Hyperkalemia and gynecomastia (anti-androgenic effect).

Front: Electrolyte abnormality that increases the risk of Digoxin toxicity?

Back: Hypokalemia.

Front: First-line antihypertensive for a pregnant patient?

Back: Hydralazine, Methyldopa, Labetalol, or Nifedipine.

Front: Mechanism of action of Hydralazine?

Back: Increases cGMP to cause smooth muscle relaxation (predominantly arterioles).

Front: Lipid-lowering agent known to cause intense cutaneous flushing?

Back: Niacin (Vitamin B3).

Front: Mechanism of Statins?

Back: Competitive inhibition of HMG-CoA reductase.

Front: Class III antiarrhythmic drug known for causing pulmonary fibrosis?

Back: Amiodarone.

Renal & Diuretics

Front: Site of action for Thiazide diuretics?

Back: Distal Convoluted Tubule (Inhibition of $Na^+/Cl^-$ symporter).

Front: Acid-base disturbance caused by Acetazolamide?

Back: Hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis.

Front: Most potent class of diuretics for managing pulmonary edema?

Back: Loop Diuretics (acting on the Thick Ascending Limb).

Central Nervous System (CNS)

Front: Mechanism of Benzodiazepines on GABA-A receptors?

Back: Increases the frequency of $Cl^-$ channel opening.

Front: Mechanism of Barbiturates on GABA-A receptors?

Back: Increases the duration of $Cl^-$ channel opening.

Front: Antidote for Benzodiazepine overdose?

Back: Flumazenil.

Front: Competitive NMDA receptor antagonist used in Alzheimer’s disease?

Back: Memantine.

Front: Inhaled anesthetic associated with malignant hyperthermia?

Back: Halothane (and Succinylcholine).

Front: Drug used to treat Malignant Hyperthermia?

Back: Dantrolene (prevents $Ca^{2+}$ release from sarcoplasmic reticulum).

Front: Mechanism of action of Sumatriptan in migraine treatment?

Back: 5-HT 1B/1D agonist causing vasoconstriction.

Front: Major side effect of typical antipsychotics (e.g., Haloperidol) via D2 blockade?

Back: Extrapyramidal symptoms (EPS).

Front: Selective Serotonin Reuptake Inhibitor (SSRI) with the longest half-life?

Back: Fluoxetine.

Front: Life-threatening reaction characterized by "lead-pipe rigidity" and fever from neuroleptics?

Back: Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS).

Front: Primary neurotransmitter deficiency targeted in Parkinson’s treatment?

Back: Dopamine.

Front: Reason for co-administering Carbidopa with Levodopa?

Back: To inhibit peripheral DOPA decarboxylase, increasing CNS bioavailability.

Antimicrobials

Front: Mechanism of action of Penicillins?

Back: Bind Penicillin-Binding Proteins (PBPs) to inhibit peptidoglycan cross-linking.

Front: Classic side effect of Aminoglycosides (e.g., Gentamicin)?

Back: Nephrotoxicity and Ototoxicity.

Front: Antibiotic class associated with tendon rupture?

Back: Fluoroquinolones (e.g., Ciprofloxacin).

Front: Mechanism of action of Vancomycin?

Back: Inhibits cell wall synthesis by binding to the D-Ala-D-Ala terminus.

Front: Antibiotic known for causing "Gray Baby Syndrome"?

Back: Chloramphenicol.

Front: Treatment of choice for Methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA)?

Back: Vancomycin.

Front: Mechanism of Tetracyclines?

Back: Bind to the 30S ribosomal subunit, blocking aminoacyl-tRNA binding.

Front: Common side effect of Rifampin?

Back: Orange discoloration of body fluids (tears, urine, sweat).

Front: Antifungal that forms pores in the fungal cell membrane by binding Ergosterol?

Back: Amphotericin B.

Gastrointestinal Pharmacology

Front: Mechanism of action of Omeprazole?

Back: Irreversible inhibition of the $H^+/K^+-ATPase$ pump (Proton Pump).

Front: H2-receptor antagonist known for inhibiting Cytochrome P450?

Back: Cimetidine.

Front: Mechanism of Misoprostol in ulcer prevention?

Back: PGE1 analog that increases gastric mucus and decreases acid secretion.

Front: Laxative that acts as an osmotic agent to draw water into the lumen?

Back: Magnesium hydroxide (or Polyethylene glycol).

Front: Primary use of Ondansetron?

Back: Control of chemotherapy-induced nausea/vomiting (5-HT3 antagonist).

Endocrine Pharmacology

Front: Mechanism of action of Metformin?

Back: Inhibits hepatic gluconeogenesis and increases peripheral insulin sensitivity.

Front: Sulfonylurea mechanism of action?

Back: Closes $K^+$ channels in pancreatic beta cells to trigger insulin release.

Front: Serious side effect of Propylthiouracil (PTU) and Methimazole?

Back: Agranulocytosis.

Front: Clinical use of Desmopressin (DDAVP)?

Back: Central Diabetes Insipidus and von Willebrand disease.

Front: Drug of choice for replacement therapy in primary hypothyroidism?

Back: Levothyroxine (T4).

Respiratory Pharmacology

Front: Mechanism of Albuterol in asthma?

Back: Short-acting Beta-2 agonist (SABA) causing bronchodilation.

Front: Inhaled corticosteroid used for chronic asthma maintenance?

Back: Fluticasone (or Budesonide).

Front: Mechanism of Montelukast?

Back: Leukotriene receptor (CysLT1) antagonist.

Front: Mechanism of Ipratropium?

Back: Muscarinic (M3) antagonist that prevents bronchoconstriction.

Hematology & Oncology

Front: Mechanism of Heparin?

Back: Activates Antithrombin III, which inactivates Thrombin and Factor Xa.

Front: Antidote for Heparin reversal?

Back: Protamine Sulfate.

Front: Lab test used to monitor Warfarin therapy?

Back: PT/INR.

Front: Mechanism of Methotrexate?

Back: Inhibits Dihydrofolate Reductase (DHFR), decreasing DNA synthesis.

Front: Chemotherapeutic agent associated with dilated cardiomyopathy?

Back: Doxorubicin (Adriamycin).

Front: Chemotherapeutic agent associated with hemorrhagic cystitis?

Back: Cyclophosphamide.

Front: Drug used to prevent Cyclophosphamide-induced hemorrhagic cystitis?

Back: Mesna.

Autacoids & Inflammation

Front: Irreversible inhibitor of COX-1 and COX-2?

Back: Aspirin.

Front: Major side effect of Celecoxib?

Back: Increased risk of cardiovascular thrombosis (Selective COX-2 inhibitor).

Front: Mechanism of action of Colchicine in Gout?

Back: Inhibits tubulin polymerization to prevent leukocyte migration.

Front: First-line chronic treatment for preventing Gout attacks?

Back: Allopurinol (Xanthine oxidase inhibitor).

(Extended Topics - High Yield Synthesis)

Front: Mechanism of Sildenafil (Viagra)?

Back: PDE-5 inhibitor, increasing cGMP levels.

Front: Classic drug interaction with PDE-5 inhibitors?

Back: Nitrates (causes severe hypotension).

Front: Mechanism of Teriparatide?

Back: Recombinant PTH analog that stimulates osteoblast activity.

Front: Preferred treatment for Myasthenia Gravis?

Back: Pyridostigmine (long-acting AChE inhibitor).

Front: Mechanism of action of Clopidogrel?

Back: Irreversible inhibition of ADP (P2Y12) receptors on platelets.

Front: Side effect of Spironolactone that is avoided by using Eplerenone?

Back: Gynecomastia.

Front: Mechanism of Nitroprusside?

Back: Releases NO to dilate both arterioles and venules (decreases Preload and Afterload).

Front: Medication used to close a Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA)?

Back: Indomethacin (NSAID).

Front: Medication used to keep a Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA) open?

Back: Alprostadil (PGE1).

Front: Mechanism of Vincristine?

Back: Binds tubulin to inhibit microtubule formation (M-phase arrest).

Front: Mechanism of Paclitaxel?

Back: Hyperstabilizes microtubules, preventing breakdown (M-phase arrest).

Front: Toxicity associated with Bleomycin?

Back: Pulmonary fibrosis.

Front: Mechanism of Cyclosporine?

Back: Calcineurin inhibitor; prevents transcription of IL-2.

Front: Primary side effect of Tacrolimus?

Back: Nephrotoxicity.

Front: Mechanism of action of Sirolimus (Rapamycin)?

Back: mTOR inhibitor; prevents response to IL-2.

Front: Reversal agent for Warfarin overdose (immediate)?

Back: Fresh Frozen Plasma (FFP) or Prothrombin Complex Concentrate (PCC).

Front: Mechanism of action of Valproic acid?

Back: Blocks $Na^+$ channels and increases GABA concentration.

Front: Teratogenic effect of Phenytoin?

Back: Fetal Hydantoin Syndrome (cleft lip/palate, nail hypoplasia).

Front: Mechanism of Ethosuximide?

Back: Blocks T-type $Ca^{2+}$ channels in thalamic neurons.

Front: Drug of choice for Absence Seizures?

Back: Ethosuximide.

Front: Mechanism of action of Local Anesthetics (e.g., Lidocaine)?

Back: Block voltage-gated $Na^+$ channels from the inside.

Front: Order of nerve block by local anesthetics (size/myelination)?

Back: Small myelinated > small unmyelinated > large myelinated.

Front: Mechanism of action of Dantrolene?

Back: Ryanodine receptor antagonist.

Front: First-line treatment for ADHD?

Back: Methylphenidate (or Amphetamines).

Front: Mechanism of action of Bupropion?

Back: Inhibits reuptake of Norepinephrine and Dopamine.

Front: Major contraindication for Bupropion?

Back: Patients with Seizure disorders or Bulimia/Anorexia.

Front: Drug used for smoking cessation that is a partial nicotinic agonist?

Back: Varenicline.

Front: Mechanism of action of Lithium?

Back: Inhibits the Inositol recycling pathway.

Front: Most common cardiac malformation associated with Lithium use in pregnancy?

Back: Ebstein Anomaly (tricuspid valve displacement).

Front: Class of drugs that cause "Cinchonism"?

Back: Quinidine and Quinine.

Front: Drug of choice for treating Giardia lamblia?

Back: Metronidazole.

Front: Mechanism of action of Terbinafine?

Back: Inhibits Squalene Epoxidase.

Front: Mechanism of action of Zidovudine (AZT)?

Back: Nucleoside Reverse Transcriptase Inhibitor (NRTI).

Front: Protease inhibitor side effect common to the class?

Back: Hyperglycemia and lipodystrophy.

Front: Mechanism of action of Acyclovir?

Back: Guanosine analog that is phosphorylated by viral Thymidine Kinase.

Front: Treatment for CMV retinitis that inhibits DNA polymerase?

Back: Ganciclovir.

Front: Mechanism of action of Isoniazid (INH)?

Back: Inhibits synthesis of mycolic acids.

Front: Vitamin co-administered with INH to prevent peripheral neuropathy?

Back: Vitamin B6 (Pyridoxine).

Front: First-line drug for Trichomonas vaginalis?

Back: Metronidazole.

Front: Mechanism of action of Sulfonamides?

Back: PABA analogs that inhibit Dihydropteroate Synthase.

Front: Side effect of Sulfonamides in newborns?

Back: Kernicterus.

Front: Drug of choice for MRSA skin infections (outpatient)?

Back: TMP-SMX or Clindamycin.

Front: Mechanism of action of Macrolides?

Back: Bind 50S subunit and inhibit translocation.

Front: Effect of Verapamil on the heart?

Back: Decreases HR and contractility (Non-dihydropyridine $Ca^{2+}$ channel blocker).

Front: Mechanism of Ezetimibe?

Back: Prevents cholesterol absorption at the small intestine brush border.

Front: First-line treatment for Acute Gout?

Back: NSAIDs (e.g., Indomethacin) or Glucocorticoids.

Front: Mechanism of Bisphosphonates?

Back: Induce apoptosis in osteoclasts.

Front: Side effect of Alendronate?

Back: Corrosive esophagitis.

Front: Mechanism of Raloxifene?

Back: SERM (Estrogen agonist in bone, antagonist in breast/uterus).

Front: Drug used to treat Cyanide poisoning?

Back: Nitrites (induce methemoglobinemia) and Sodium Thiosulfate.

Front: Mechanism of action of Acetylcysteine in APAP overdose?

Back: Restores Glutathione stores.

Front: Mechanism of action of Carbon Monoxide poisoning?

Back: Competitive inhibition of $O_2$ binding to Hemoglobin (left shift).

Front: Treatment for Methanol or Ethylene Glycol poisoning?

Back: Fomepizole.

Front: Mechanism of action of Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT)?

Back: IgG antibodies against Heparin-Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) complex.

Front: Treatment for HIT?

Back: Stop Heparin; start Direct Thrombin Inhibitor (e.g., Argatroban).

Front: Mechanism of action of Abciximab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against GPIIb/IIIa receptors.

Front: Reversal agent for Dabigatran?

Back: Idarucizumab.

Front: Mechanism of action of Thionamides (PTU/Methimazole)?

Back: Inhibit thyroid peroxidase, blocking iodine organification and coupling.

Front: Primary side effect of Flutamide?

Back: Gynecomastia and hepatotoxicity (Competitive androgen receptor antagonist).

Front: Mechanism of action of Finasteride?

Back: 5-alpha-reductase inhibitor (decreases conversion of Testosterone to DHT).

Front: Drug used to induce ovulation in patients with PCOS?

Back: Clomiphene (SERM that inhibits negative feedback by estrogen on the hypothalamus).

Front: Mechanism of action of Anastrozole?

Back: Aromatase inhibitor (decreases peripheral conversion of androgens to estrogen).

Front: Mechanism of action of Mifepristone?

Back: Progesterone receptor antagonist used for medical abortion.

Front: Mechanism of action of Tamsulosin?

Back: Alpha-1A/D selective antagonist (targets prostate smooth muscle, minimizes systemic hypotension).

CNS Pharmacology (Part 2 - Anesthetics & Addiction)

Front: Intravenous anesthetic associated with "dissociative anesthesia" and hallucinations?

Back: Ketamine (NMDA receptor antagonist).

Front: Intravenous anesthetic that is a potent GABA-A agonist and causes "milk of amnesia"?

Back: Propofol.

Front: Mechanism of action of Local Anesthetics?

Back: Blockade of voltage-gated sodium channels from the intracellular side.

Front: Effect of adding Epinephrine to a local anesthetic?

Back: Vasoconstriction (decreases systemic absorption, reduces bleeding, increases duration of action).

Front: Drug used to treat opioid-induced constipation that does not cross the BBB?

Back: Methylnaltrexone.

Front: Mechanism of action of Modafinil?

Back: Promotes wakefulness by increasing synaptic Dopamine (used for Narcolepsy).

Front: Treatment for Restless Leg Syndrome?

Back: Dopamine agonists (e.g., Pramipexole or Ropinirole).

Immunology & Autoimmunity

Front: Mechanism of action of Mycophenolate Mofetil?

Back: Reversibly inhibits IMP dehydrogenase, preventing purine synthesis in B and T cells.

Front: Targeted protein of Adalimumab and Etanercept?

Back: TNF-alpha.

Front: Mechanism of action of Thalidomide?

Back: Inhibits TNF-alpha and has anti-angiogenic properties (major teratogen: Phocomelia).

Front: Mechanism of action of Leflunomide?

Back: Inhibits dihydroorotate dehydrogenase, preventing pyrimidine synthesis.

Antimicrobials (Part 2 - Specialized Coverage)

Front: Mechanism of action of Polymyxins (Colistin)?

Back: Act as detergents to disrupt the bacterial cell membrane (Gram-negative coverage).

Front: Mechanism of action of Daptomycin?

Back: Lipopeptide that disrupts the cell membrane of Gram-positive cocci via depolarization.

Front: Common side effect of Daptomycin?

Back: Myopathy and elevated Creatine Kinase (CK).

Front: Drug of choice for prophylaxis against Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC) in AIDS?

Back: Azithromycin.

Front: Mechanism of action of Griseofulvin?

Back: Interferes with microtubule function to inhibit mitosis in dermatophytes.

Front: Mechanism of action of Permethrin?

Back: Inhibits sodium channel deactivation, leading to paralysis in mites/lice.

Front: Mechanism of action of Albendazole?

Back: Inhibits microtubule synthesis in helminths.

Autacoids & Respiratory (Part 2)

Front: Mechanism of action of Guaifenesin?

Back: Expectorant that thins respiratory secretions (does not suppress cough).

Front: Mechanism of action of Dextromethorphan?

Back: NMDA receptor antagonist that acts on the medullary cough center to suppress cough.

Front: Mechanism of action of Bosentan?

Back: Endothelin-1 receptor antagonist used for Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension.

Front: Common side effect of Bosentan?

Back: Hepatotoxicity.

Front: Mechanism of action of Omalizumab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody that binds unbound IgE (used in refractory asthma).

Toxicology & Antidotes (Comprehensive)

Front: Antidote for Iron toxicity?

Back: Deferoxamine.

Front: Antidote for Lead poisoning?

Back: EDTA, Dimercaprol, Succimer (in children).

Front: Antidote for Copper toxicity (Wilson’s Disease)?

Back: Penicillamine or Trientine.

Front: Antidote for Cyanide poisoning?

Back: Nitrites (to form methemoglobin) and Hydroxocobalamin (Vitamin B12a).

Front: Antidote for Ethylene Glycol or Methanol?

Back: Fomepizole (inhibits Alcohol Dehydrogenase).

Front: Mechanism of action of Sugammadex?

Back: Encapsulates and inactivates Rocuronium/Vecuronium (Neuromuscular blockade reversal).

Front: Antidote for Beta-blocker overdose?

Back: Glucagon (increases cAMP in cardiac myocytes via non-adrenergic pathway).

Front: Antidote for Salicylate (Aspirin) overdose?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate (alkalinizes urine to trap the drug).

Front: Drug class that causes "Fanconi Syndrome" if taken after expiration?

Back: Tetracyclines.

High-Yield Miscellany (The "Odds and Ends")

Front: Mechanism of action of Orlistat?

Back: Inhibits gastric and pancreatic lipases to decrease fat absorption.

Front: Mechanism of action of Ursodeoxycholic acid (Ursodiol)?

Back: Decreases cholesterol secretion into bile; used to dissolve gallstones.

Front: Mechanism of action of Cinacalcet?

Back: Sensitizes calcium-sensing receptors (CaSR) in the parathyroid gland to decrease PTH.

Front: Drug used to treat SIADH that is a Vasopressin antagonist?

Back: Conivaptan or Tolvaptan.

Front: Mechanism of action of Sevelamer?

Back: Phosphate binder used in Chronic Kidney Disease to prevent hyperphosphatemia.

Front: Mechanism of action of Riluzole?

Back: Decreases glutamate excitotoxicity; used to extend survival in ALS.

Front: Mechanism of action of Varenicline?

Back: Partial agonist at α4β2 nicotinic acetylcholine receptors.

Front: Mechanism of action of Sacubitril?

Back: Neprilysin inhibitor (prevents degradation of Natriuretic Peptides).

Front: Standard dual therapy for Heart Failure with Reduced Ejection Fraction (HFrEF)?

Back: Sacubitril/Valsartan (ARNI).

Front: Definition of Zero-order elimination?

Back: Rate of elimination is constant regardless of plasma concentration (e.g., Ethanol, Aspirin at high doses).

Front: Definition of First-order elimination?

Back: Constant fraction of drug is eliminated per unit time (rate depends on concentration).

Front: Formula for Loading Dose?

Back: $LD = (C_p \times V_d) / F$.

Front: Formula for Maintenance Dose?

Back: $MD = (C_p \times CL \times \tau) / F$.

Front: Number of half-lives required to reach steady state?

Back: Approximately 4 to 5.

Front: Effect of a competitive antagonist on $V_{max}$ and $K_m$?

Back: $V_{max}$ remains unchanged; $K_m$ increases.

Front: Effect of a non-competitive antagonist on $V_{max}$ and $K_m$?

Back: $V_{max}$ decreases; $K_m$ remains unchanged.

Front: Primary Phase 1 metabolism reaction?

Back: Oxidation, reduction, or hydrolysis (usually via Cytochrome P450).

Front: Primary Phase 2 metabolism reaction?

Back: Conjugation (Glucuronidation, Acetylation, Sulfation) to make drugs polar for excretion.

Advanced Oncology & Immunotherapy

Front: Mechanism of action of Imatinib (Gleevec)?

Back: Tyrosine kinase inhibitor of BCR-ABL (Philadelphia chromosome).

Front: Mechanism of action of Trastuzumab (Herceptin)?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against HER2/neu (ERBB2) receptor.

Front: Mechanism of action of Rituximab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against CD20 found on B-cells.

Front: Mechanism of action of Tamoxifen?

Back: Selective Estrogen Receptor Modulator (SERM); antagonist in breast, agonist in bone/uterus.

Front: Common side effect of Tamoxifen?

Back: Increased risk of endometrial cancer and thromboembolism.

Front: Mechanism of action of 5-Fluorouracil (5-FU)?

Back: Pyrimidine analog that covalently inhibits Thymidylate Synthase.

Front: Mechanism of action of Etoposide?

Back: Inhibits Topoisomerase II, causing DNA strand breaks.

Front: Mechanism of action of Irinotecan/Topotecan?

Back: Inhibits Topoisomerase I.

Front: Mechanism of action of Pembrolizumab/Nivolumab?

Back: PD-1 inhibitors that "release the brakes" on T-cells to attack tumors.

Advanced Infectious Disease (Rare/High-Yield)

Front: Treatment for prophylaxis against Pneumocystis jirovecii (PCP) in HIV?

Back: TMP-SMX (Bactrim).

Front: Mechanism of action of Linezolid?

Back: Binds 50S subunit to prevent formation of the initiation complex.

Front: Unique side effect of Linezolid related to antidepressants?

Back: Serotonin Syndrome (due to weak MAO inhibition).

Front: Mechanism of action of Nitrofurantoin?

Back: Bactericidal; damages bacterial DNA (used primarily for uncomplicated UTIs).

Front: Drug of choice for treating Neurosyphilis?

Back: Penicillin G (IV).

Clinical Interactions & Contraindications

Front: Interaction between St. John’s Wort and Cyclosporine?

Back: St. John’s Wort induces CYP3A4, decreasing Cyclosporine levels and causing transplant rejection.

Front: Interaction between Grapefruit juice and Statins?

Back: Grapefruit juice inhibits CYP3A4, increasing Statin levels and risk of myopathy.

Front: Why are Tetracyclines contraindicated in children and pregnancy?

Back: Chelation of calcium causes permanent tooth discoloration and bone growth inhibition.

Front: "Sulfa drugs" list (common cross-reactivity)?

Back: Sulfonamides, Thiazides, Loop diuretics, Sulfonylureas, Acetazolamide, Celecoxib.

Front: Drug-induced Lupus is most commonly associated with which drugs?

Back: Hydralazine, Isoniazid, Procainamide, Phenytoin (SHIPP).

Front: Characteristic finding of Digoxin toxicity on ECG?

Back: "Scooped" ST segment and various arrhythmias (most common is PVCs).

Final High-Yield Potpourri

Front: Mechanism of action of Ranolazine?

Back: Inhibits the late inward sodium current in cardiac myocytes (used for refractory angina).

Front: Mechanism of action of Milrinone?

Back: PDE-3 inhibitor; increases cAMP causing inotropy and vasodilation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Ivabradine?

Back: Selective inhibition of "funny" sodium channels ($I_f$) in the SA node.

Front: Mechanism of action of Aprepitant?

Back: Substance P antagonist (NK1 receptor blocker) used for chemotherapy-induced vomiting.

Front: Drug used to prevent uric acid stones by alkalinizing urine?

Back: Potassium Citrate.

Front: Mechanism of action of Denosumab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against RANKL (inhibits osteoclast maturation).

Front: Mechanism of action of Mupirocin?

Back: Inhibits bacterial isoleucyl-tRNA synthetase (topical for Impetigo/MRSA colonization).

Front: Drug of choice for Wilson’s Disease?

Back: Penicillamine.

Front: Mechanism of action of Octreotide?

Back: Somatostatin analog used for acromegaly, carcinoid syndrome, and variceal bleeding.

Front: Primary side effect of Amiodarone relating to the skin?

Back: Blue-gray skin discoloration (photodermatitis).

Front: Mechanism of action of Dipyridamole?

Back: Inhibits phosphodiesterase and adenosine uptake to inhibit platelet aggregation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Isotretinoin in acne treatment?

Back: Vitamin A derivative that reduces sebum production and induces apoptosis of sebaceous glands.

Front: Side effect of Topical Steroids used chronically on the skin?

Back: Skin atrophy, striae, and telangiectasia.

Front: Mechanism of Latanoprost in Glaucoma?

Back: Prostaglandin F2α analog that increases uveoscleral outflow.

Front: First-line medication for Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma to rapidly lower IOP?

Back: Acetazolamide (IV) or Pilocarpine.

Front: Side effect of Alpha-agonists (e.g., Brimonidine) in the eye?

Back: Blurry vision, ocular hyperemia, and foreign body sensation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Timolol in Glaucoma?

Back: Non-selective beta-blocker that decreases aqueous humor production.

Reproductive & Obstetric Pharmacology

Front: Drug used to prevent cerebral palsy and seizures in Preeclampsia?

Back: Magnesium Sulfate.

Front: Signs of Magnesium Sulfate toxicity?

Back: Loss of deep tendon reflexes, respiratory depression, and cardiac arrest.

Front: Antidote for Magnesium Sulfate toxicity?

Back: Calcium Gluconate.

Front: Mechanism of action of Oxytocin?

Back: Stimulates Gq-coupled receptors to increase uterine contractions and milk let-down.

Front: Mechanism of Terbutaline/Ritodrine in obstetrics?

Back: Beta-2 agonists used as tocolytics to relax the uterus and delay preterm labor.

Clinical Biochemical Pharmacology

Front: Mechanism of action of Allopurinol vs. Probenecid?

Back: Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase; Probenecid inhibits renal reabsorption of uric acid.

Front: Enzyme inhibited by Mycophenolate Mofetil?

Back: Inosine Monophosphate Dehydrogenase (IMPDH).

Front: Mechanism of action of Azathioprine?

Back: Pro-drug of 6-Mercaptopurine; inhibits de novo purine synthesis.

Front: Key interaction between Allopurinol and Azathioprine?

Back: Allopurinol inhibits Xanthine Oxidase, leading to toxic levels of 6-MP/Azathioprine.

Front: Mechanism of Pegloticase?

Back: Recombinant uricase that converts uric acid to allantoin (more soluble).

Specialized Infectious Disease & Vaccines

Front: Mechanism of action of Sofosbuvir in Hepatitis C?

Back: Inhibits HCV RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (NS5B).

Front: Mechanism of action of Ribavirin?

Back: Inhibits IMP dehydrogenase and viral RNA polymerase.

Front: Major side effect of Ribavirin?

Back: Dose-dependent hemolytic anemia and severe teratogenicity.

Front: Treatment for prophylaxis in a patient with a needle-stick injury from an HIV+ source?

Back: Post-Exposure Prophylaxis (PEP) with Tenofovir, Emtricitabine, and Raltegravir/Dolutegravir.

Musculoskeletal & Connective Tissue

Front: Mechanism of action of Cyclobenzaprine?

Back: Centrally acting skeletal muscle relaxant (structurally related to TCAs).

Front: Mechanism of action of Leflunomide in RA?

Back: Inhibits dihydroorotate dehydrogenase, arresting T-cell proliferation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Abatacept?

Back: Binds to CD80/86 on APCs to prevent T-cell costimulation via CD28.

Toxicology & Critical Care

Front: Treatment for Salicylate-induced hyperventilation/respiratory alkalosis?

Back: Supportive care (alkalinization of urine for the metabolic acidosis component).

Front: Clinical presentation of "Atropine Fever"?

Back: Hyperpyrexia due to suppression of sweat gland activity (anhidrosis).

Front: Difference between Succinylcholine and Rocuronium mechanism?

Back: Succinylcholine is a depolarizing blocker (ACh agonist); Rocuronium is a non-depolarizing blocker (competitive antagonist).

Front: Phase of blockade for Succinylcholine that can be reversed by AChE inhibitors?

Back: Phase II (prolonged exposure/desensitization); Phase I is worsened by AChE inhibitors.

Front: Mechanism of action of Theophylline?

Back: Inhibits Phosphodiesterase (PDE), increasing cAMP, and antagonizes Adenosine receptors.

Front: Why is Theophylline rarely used now?

Back: Narrow therapeutic index and many drug interactions (CYP450 metabolism).

Final Scrutiny Cards

Front: Mechanism of action of Prucalopride?

Back: 5-HT4 receptor agonist used for chronic idiopathic constipation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Rifaximin in Hepatic Encephalopathy?

Back: Non-absorbable antibiotic that kills ammonia-producing gut bacteria.

Front: Mechanism of action of Lactulose in Hepatic Encephalopathy?

Back: Converted by gut bacteria to lactic acid, trapping $NH_3$ as $NH_4^+$ in the lumen.

Front: Mechanism of action of Fenoldopam?

Back: Dopamine D1 receptor agonist; used in hypertensive emergencies to dilate renal/coronary vessels.

Front: Mechanism of action of Midodrine?

Back: Alpha-1 agonist used to treat symptomatic orthostatic hypotension.

Front: Genetic deficiency that increases the risk of hemolytic anemia with Primaquine or Sulfonamides?

Back: Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency.

Front: Genetic variation that necessitates a lower dose of Warfarin?

Back: Polymorphisms in CYP2C9 or VKORC1.

Front: Effect of being a "Slow Acetylator" on Isoniazid (INH) therapy?

Back: Increased risk of peripheral neuropathy and drug-induced lupus.

Front: HLA allele associated with a high risk of Stevens-Johnson Syndrome (SJS) when taking Abacavir?

Back: HLA-B*5701.

Front: HLA allele associated with SJS in Asian populations taking Carbamazepine?

Back: HLA-B*1502.

Advanced Cardiovascular & Autonomic Nuances

Front: Effect of "Low Dose" vs "High Dose" Epinephrine on Blood Pressure?

Back: Low dose decreases BP (Beta-2 vasodilation); High dose increases BP (Alpha-1 vasoconstriction).

Front: Primary side effect of Minoxidil (other than hypertrichosis)?

Back: Reflex tachycardia and fluid retention (should be given with a beta-blocker and diuretic).

Front: Mechanism of action of Sacubitril?

Back: Inhibits Neprilysin, increasing levels of atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP) and BNP.

Front: Why must ACE inhibitors be stopped 36 hours before starting Sacubitril/Valsartan?

Back: To avoid a significant increase in the risk of angioedema.

Front: Mechanism of "Nitrate Tolerance"?

Back: Depletion of sulfhydryl groups or oxidative stress; managed by a "nitrate-free interval" (usually at night).

Specialized Toxicology & Environmental

Front: Treatment for Lead poisoning in an adult with severe symptoms?

Back: Dimercaprol and EDTA.

Front: Mechanism of Arsenic poisoning?

Back: Inhibits lipoic acid, a necessary cofactor for Pyruvate Dehydrogenase.

Front: Classic clinical presentation of Arsenic poisoning?

Back: Garlic breath, rice-water stools, and "mees lines" (white lines on nails).

Front: Antidote for Arsenic poisoning?

Back: Dimercaprol.

Front: Mechanism of action of Cyanide?

Back: Inhibits Cytochrome c oxidase in the electron transport chain (inhibits aerobic respiration).

Neuro-Psychiatry & Behavioral Health (Advanced)

Front: Mechanism of action of Ramelteon?

Back: Melatonin receptor (MT1 and MT2) agonist used for insomnia.

Front: Mechanism of action of Suvorexant?

Back: Orexin (hypocretin) receptor antagonist used for insomnia.

Front: Drug of choice for Bulimia Nervosa?

Back: Fluoxetine.

Front: Mechanism of action of Atomoxetine?

Back: Selective Norepinephrine Reuptake Inhibitor (SNRI) used for ADHD.

Front: Why are MAO inhibitors contraindicated with Tyramine-rich foods (cheese, wine)?

Back: Tyramine displaces stored Norepinephrine, leading to a hypertensive crisis.

Hematology & Oncology (Niche Agents)

Front: Mechanism of action of Bortezomib?

Back: Proteasome inhibitor; induces apoptosis (used in Multiple Myeloma).

Front: Mechanism of action of Bevacizumab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against VEGF (inhibits angiogenesis).

Front: Mechanism of action of Cetuximab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against EGFR (Epidermal Growth Factor Receptor).

Front: Mechanism of action of Rasburicase?

Back: Recombinant uricase that catalyzes the metabolism of uric acid to allantoin.

Front: Common use for Rasburicase in Oncology?

Back: Prevention and treatment of Tumor Lysis Syndrome.

Clinical "Best" Practices (Quick Hitters)

Front: Best drug for treating hypertensive emergency in a patient with aortic dissection?

Back: Esmolol (requires rapid heart rate and BP control).

Front: Preferred anticoagulant for a pregnant patient with a DVT?

Back: Low Molecular Weight Heparin (LMWH), such as Enoxaparin.

Front: Preferred induction agent for a patient with asthma or hypotension?

Back: Ketamine (causes bronchodilation and sympathetic stimulation).

Front: First-line medication for trigeminal neuralgia?

Back: Carbamazepine.

Front: Best drug for prophylaxis of migraines?

Back: Propranolol, Topiramate, or Amitriptyline.

Front: Best drug for acute management of status epilepticus?

Back: IV Benzodiazepines (Lorazepam or Diazepam).

Final Check: Systemic "Oddities"

Front: Mechanism of action of Pentazocine?

Back: Opioid Kappa-receptor agonist and Mu-receptor partial agonist/antagonist.

Front: Risk of giving Pentazocine to a patient already on Morphine?

Back: May precipitate withdrawal due to Mu-receptor antagonism.

Front: Mechanism of action of Tranexamic Acid?

Back: Antifibrinolytic; inhibits Plasminogen activation.

Front: Drug that causes "Yellow-green" vision distortion?

Back: Digoxin.

Front: Drug that causes "Blue-tinted" vision (Cyanopsia)?

Back: Sildenafil (PDE-5 inhibitors).

Front: Mechanism of action of Denosumab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against RANKL, inhibiting osteoclast maturation (used in Osteoporosis).

Front: Mechanism of action of Romosozumab?

Back: Inhibits Sclerostin, increasing bone formation and decreasing resorption.

Front: Mechanism of action of Evolocumab/Alirocumab?

Back: PCSK9 inhibitors; increase LDL receptor recycling to lower plasma LDL levels.

Front: Mechanism of action of Omalizumab?

Back: Binds unbound IgE to prevent it from binding Mast cells (used in allergic asthma).

Front: Mechanism of action of Dupilumab?

Back: IL-4 receptor alpha antagonist; inhibits IL-4 and IL-13 signaling (used in Eczema/Asthma).

Front: Mechanism of action of Ustekinumab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against IL-12 and IL-23 (used in Psoriasis/Crohn's).

Advanced Oncology & Targeted Therapy

Front: Mechanism of action of Vemurafenib?

Back: Small molecule inhibitor of BRAF V600E mutated kinase (used in Melanoma).

Front: Mechanism of action of Olaparib?

Back: PARP inhibitor; prevents DNA repair in BRCA-deficient cells (synthetic lethality).

Front: Mechanism of action of Ipilimumab?

Back: CTLA-4 inhibitor; enhances T-cell activation against tumor cells.

Front: Mechanism of action of Abemaciclib/Palbociclib?

Back: CDK4/6 inhibitors; arrest the cell cycle in the G1 phase.

Critical Drug-Drug Interactions (Final)

Front: Interaction between Amiodarone and Warfarin?

Back: Amiodarone inhibits CYP2C9, leading to increased Warfarin levels and bleeding risk.

Front: Interaction between Macrolides (except Azithromycin) and Statins?

Back: Inhibition of CYP3A4 increases Statin concentration, leading to Rhabdomyolysis.

Front: Effect of NSAIDs on Lithium levels?

Back: Decreased renal clearance of Lithium, leading to toxicity.

Front: Interaction between Sildenafil and Alpha-1 blockers?

Back: Can lead to severe orthostatic hypotension (spaced 4 hours apart).

Systemic Miscellany & Clinical "Pearls"

Front: Drug of choice for treating malignant hyperthermia?

Back: Dantrolene.

Front: Mechanism of action of Midazolam?

Back: Short-acting Benzodiazepine used for procedural sedation.

Front: Drug that causes "Red Man Syndrome" due to histamine release?

Back: Vancomycin (prevented by slow infusion).

Front: Drug class that causes "Fanconi-like" syndrome?

Back: Ifosfamide (or expired Tetracyclines).

Front: Medication used to treat the EPS side effect "Akathisia"?

Back: Beta-blockers or Benzodiazepines.

Front: Antidote for anticholinergic toxicity?

Back: Physostigmine (tertiary amine that crosses the BBB).

Front: Antidote for Beta-blocker or Calcium Channel Blocker overdose?

Back: Glucagon and/or high-dose Insulin with Glucose.

Front: Mechanism of action of Cilostazol?

Back: PDE-3 inhibitor; increases cAMP in platelets (inhibits aggregation) and dilates arteries.

Front: Primary use for Cilostazol?

Back: Symptomatic treatment of Intermittent Claudication.

Front: Mechanism of action of Roflumilast?

Back: PDE-4 inhibitor used to reduce exacerbations in severe COPD.

Front: Drug used to treat nocturnal enuresis by decreasing urine volume?

Back: Desmopressin (Oral or Nasal spray).

Front: Drug used for rapid sequence intubation that is a nicotinic ACh receptor agonist?

Back: Succinylcholine.

Front: Major electrolyte side effect of Succinylcholine in burn or trauma patients?

Back: Severe Hyperkalemia.

Front: Mechanism of action of Sugammadex?

Back: Selective relaxant binding agent used to reverse Rocuronium or Vecuronium.

Front: Mechanism of action of Ivabradine?

Back: Selective inhibitor of the $I_f$ (funny) current in the SA node.

Front: Specific indication for Ivabradine?

Back: Chronic HFrEF in patients with a resting heart rate $\ge$ 70 bpm who are on max beta-blockers.

Front: Mechanism of action of Epoprostenol?

Back: Prostacyclin ($PGI_2$) analog used for Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension to cause vasodilation.

Front: Primary side effect of Minoxidil (systemic)?

Back: Hypertrichosis and pericardial effusion.

Front: Mechanism of action of Milrinone?

Back: PDE-3 inhibitor that increases cAMP to provide positive inotropy and systemic vasodilation.

Front: Unique mechanism of Nitroprusside toxicity?

Back: Cyanide toxicity due to the release of 5 cyanide groups per molecule.

Front: First-line medication for Raynaud’s phenomenon?

Back: Calcium Channel Blockers (specifically Dihydropyridines like Nifedipine).

Endocrine & Metabolic "Niches"

Front: Mechanism of action of Miglitol or Acarbose?

Back: Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors that delay carbohydrate absorption in the brush border.

Front: Mechanism of action of Pramlintide?

Back: Amylin analog that delays gastric emptying and suppresses glucagon secretion.

Front: Mechanism of action of Exenatide or Liraglutide?

Back: GLP-1 receptor agonists that increase glucose-dependent insulin secretion.

Front: Major side effect of GLP-1 agonists?

Back: Nausea, vomiting, and risk of Pancreatitis.

Front: Mechanism of action of Canagliflozin (SGLT2 inhibitors)?

Back: Inhibit glucose reabsorption in the Proximal Convoluted Tubule.

specialized Cellular & Molecular Targets

Front: Mechanism of action of Dipyridamole?

Back: Inhibits phosphodiesterase and adenosine uptake to decrease platelet aggregation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Cilostazol in peripheral vascular disease?

Back: PDE-3 inhibitor that increases cAMP, causing vasodilation and anti-platelet effects.

Front: Mechanism of action of Abciximab vs. Eptifibatide?

Back: Both are GPIIb/IIIa inhibitors; Abciximab is a monoclonal antibody, Eptifibatide is a peptide.

Front: Mechanism of action of Tranexamic Acid?

Back: Antifibrinolytic that competitively inhibits plasminogen activation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Idarucizumab?

Back: Specific reversal agent (monoclonal antibody) for the direct thrombin inhibitor Dabigatran.
